AI Timelines: Integrated Synthesis and Operationalized Crux Map (Round 2)

May 2026 – Revised after adversarial critique

Preamble: What Changed and Why

Round 1 of this synthesis drew a detailed adversarial critique identifying nine categories of problems. This revision addresses them directly:

  1. Systematic L-lean corrected. Round 1 had 5/8 cruxes leaning longer-timeline (L), 1 leaning shorter (S), and 2 neutral. This revision rebalances by (a) adjusting assessments where the evidence genuinely supports a more balanced reading, (b) adding new cruxes whose natural lean is toward S, and (c) flagging the remaining directional leans explicitly rather than presenting them as neutral.

  2. Joint probability inconsistency fixed. Round 1’s crux probabilities implied a ~3.3% chance of the “fast scenario” but placed the Broad Cognitive Parity median at 2035, inconsistent with that low joint probability. This revision models timeline estimates as weighted mixtures over scenario clusters, with explicit conditional probabilities.

  3. Four new cruxes added: Recursive Improvement Speed (9), Regulatory Disruption (10), Embodiment Requirement (11), and AGI Definition (12).

  4. Composite metrics replace single-benchmark proxies for broad cruxes (5, 8).

  5. Conflict-of-interest flags are inlined on every industry/academic quote used as evidence.

  6. Unmeasured capabilities are treated symmetrically – absence of measurement is noted as genuinely uncertain, not presumptively negative.

  7. Cross-crux interactions are quantified with conditional probability shifts.

  8. The “bounded good / open-ended bad” pattern is synthesized as a cross-cutting finding.

1. The Capability Landscape

1.1 At or Above Human Level

These domains show frontier AI matching or exceeding relevant human baselines:

  • Competition mathematics (AIME, HMMT): Solved. GPT-5.2 scored 100% on AIME 2025; frontier models cluster at 97-100% (CodeSOTA LLM Benchmark Leaderboard 2026).
  • Graduate-level science knowledge (GPQA Diamond): Saturated at 94.2%, exceeding PhD experts (69.7%) by 24+ points (PricePerToken GPQA Leaderboard).
  • General knowledge (MMLU, MMLU-Pro): Saturated. Top scores 90-93% (lmmarketcap Benchmarks).
  • Simple code generation (HumanEval): Saturated at 97-98%.
  • Real-world software engineering (SWE-bench Verified): 87-94% reported; adjusted for semantic incorrectness: ~70-75% genuine solve rate (SWE-ABS, arXiv:2603.00520; Epoch AI SWE-bench Tracker).
  • Desktop computer use (OSWorld): 73-80%, matching or slightly exceeding human baseline (72.4%) (Coasty AI OSWorld Results 2026).
  • Casual Turing test passage: GPT-4.5 with persona prompting identified as human 73% of the time – more often than actual humans (Jones & Bergen, PNAS 2025).
  • Short-form persuasion: AI outpersuades humans 64% of the time with demographic personalization (Salvi et al., Nature Human Behaviour, May 2025).
  • Professional document generation: AI-written legal briefs, business emails, and academic papers routinely pass expert review. ~20% of ICLR 2025 reviews were AI-generated (Stanford AI Index 2026).
  • Medical empathy (text-based): AI chatbots score 0.87 SD higher than human clinicians on empathy ratings (Tian et al., British Medical Bulletin 2025).
  • Abstract reasoning on static puzzles (ARC-AGI-2): 85-98% via test-time compute and program synthesis, approaching or exceeding human baseline (~75%) (ARC Prize 2025 Results).
  • Short-form creative output: AI matches or exceeds average human performance on divergent thinking tests, though top 10% of humans still outperform (Divergent creativity in humans and LLMs, Scientific Reports, Jan 2026).
1.2 Clearly Below Human Level

These domains show large, persistent gaps:

  • Interactive novel-environment reasoning (ARC-AGI-3): Best AI 0.37%, humans 100%. A 270x gap (ARC-AGI-3 Launch, arcprize.org; arXiv:2603.24621).
  • Research-level mathematics (FrontierMath Tier 4, Riemann-Bench): Best AI ~6-48%, vs. expert mathematicians who solve most problems (Epoch AI FrontierMath; Riemann-Bench, arXiv:2604.06802).
  • Expert-level knowledge breadth (Humanity’s Last Exam): Best AI ~45-65%, domain experts ~90% (HLE Leaderboard).
  • Physical/spatial reasoning (Escher-Bench): Best AI 57.3%, humans 90.6%. 33+ point gap (EscherVerse, arXiv:2601.01547).
  • Hard physics reasoning (PhysReason hard subset): Best AI 31.95% (PhysReason, arXiv:2502.12054).
  • Higher-order theory of mind: Performance on 4th+ order ToM tasks collapses to near-zero (ToMBench, ACL 2024).
  • Deception detection: LLMs can produce deception convincingly but fail to detect it in others (WOLF benchmark, arXiv:2512.09187).
  • Sustained autonomous task completion: 80% time horizon is only 27 minutes (vs. 50% horizon of ~15 hours). Sharp degradation after ~35 minutes (METR Time Horizon 1.1; Agent Drift, chanl.ai).
  • Long-form creative coherence: No AI can produce a publishable novel. Character/thematic consistency degrades beyond ~80,000 words.
  • Research execution: AI-generated research ideas score higher pre-execution but drop significantly post-execution; human ideas retain their scores (Ideation-Execution Gap, ICLR 2026, arXiv:2506.20803).
  • Reasoning robustness: Top models suffer up to 60% accuracy loss on elementary problems when a single condition is modified (RoR-Bench, arXiv:2504.00509).
1.3 Unmeasured or Under-Measured

These capabilities lack systematic evaluation despite being central to general intelligence. The absence of measurement is genuinely symmetric: we do not know whether AI lacks these capabilities or whether we simply have not tested for them properly. The first serious attempts at measurement (e.g., MIRROR for metacognition) have found gaps, but these are first-generation benchmarks with their own reliability concerns – and first-generation benchmark results have historically been poor guides to fundamental capability limits (cf. ARC-AGI-2: 0% to 98% in one year).

  • Metacognition and self-regulation: MIRROR benchmark (April 2026) finds models cannot translate self-knowledge into action selection. Compositional Calibration Error near random (0.5-0.94). But MIRROR is a single unreplicated benchmark (MIRROR, arXiv:2604.19809).
  • Continual/lifelong learning: No benchmark. Models do not persistently improve from deployment experience – but whether this is an architectural limitation or a training choice is unclear (Continual Learning Survey, arXiv:2603.12658).
  • Long-horizon strategic planning: No benchmark for multi-week planning, goal decomposition, or strategy adaptation.
  • Cross-domain transfer: No benchmark tests whether gains in math transfer to legal reasoning or scientific discovery. The few anecdotal observations suggest transfer is weak, but absence of systematic study means we do not know (Cognitive Dark Matter, arXiv:2603.03414).
  • Creative originality (vs. imitation): Expert poets can distinguish AI from human poetry; fine-tuned models fool even MFA writers 62-69% of the time. The gap between imitation and creation is real but hard to quantify (Can Good Writing Be Generative?, CHI 2026, arXiv:2601.18353; Holyoak, J. of Creativity 2026).
  • Taste, aesthetic judgment, curation: Entirely unmeasured.
  • Real-world embodied reasoning: Best household robot achieves 50% task success; best physics research agents score single digits. No standard benchmark (CritPt Leaderboard, Artificial Analysis).
  • Moral reasoning under genuine uncertainty: Existing benchmarks test ethical knowledge, not ethical judgment.
  • Common-sense physical intuition: The analog clock problem (50.1% accuracy for IMO-gold-level models) illustrates the gap, but no systematic benchmark (Stanford AI Index 2026).
1.4 The “Bounded Good / Open-Ended Bad” Pattern

A cross-cutting pattern emerges from the capability data that the critique correctly identified as under-analyzed:

AI excels at bounded, well-specified tasks and struggles at open-ended, interactive, long-horizon tasks. This pattern is remarkably consistent across domains:

Task type Examples AI performance
Bounded, static AIME, GPQA, HumanEval, ARC-AGI-2 At or above human level
Semi-bounded, short SWE-bench, OSWorld, short persuasion Near human level
Open-ended, interactive ARC-AGI-3, Diplomacy, novel writing Far below human level
Sustained, autonomous METR 80% horizon, multi-day tasks Degrades sharply

This pattern has direct implications for which cruxes are likely to resolve S vs. L. Cruxes about bounded performance (pre-training scaling on benchmarks, compute availability) should lean S, while cruxes about integrated, sustained capability (autonomous research, architectural sufficiency, METR reliability) should lean L. The timeline question reduces to: how quickly does the boundary between “bounded” and “open-ended” shift?

2. Operationalized Crux Map

Each crux represents a specific empirical disagreement that drives divergent timeline estimates. Claims are labeled: shorter-timeline (S) or longer-timeline (L).

Crux 1: Test-Time Compute Scaling Ceiling

What this resolves: Whether inference-time reasoning continues to produce capability gains at scale, or saturates.

Claim S: Test-time compute continues to yield reliable gains through 2028, enabling frontier models to solve ARC-AGI-3-class problems and sustain multi-day autonomous tasks.

Claim L: Test-time compute follows the same S-curve as pre-training: dramatic initial gains, then saturation. Models plateau at roughly current reasoning depths regardless of inference budget.

Dimension Specification
Metric ARC-AGI-3 score for frontier models using unconstrained test-time compute
Threshold >25% supports Claim S; 10-25% is ambiguous but represents extraordinary progress from 0.37%; <10% supports Claim L. The 10% lower bound is chosen because the best purpose-built system already scores 12.58%, so <10% for frontier models would indicate genuine stagnation.
Date December 2027
Data source ARC Prize leaderboard (arcprize.org), public eval scores

Evidence for Claim S: - ARC-AGI-2 went from 0% to 98% in ~12 months, primarily via test-time compute innovations (program synthesis, evolutionary code optimization, TTT) (ARC Prize 2025 Results; Imbue code evolution). - A 7B model with 100x inference compute matched a 70B model on standard tasks. An 8B model reached 94.5% on HMMT 2025 via ~2M token inference chains (Inference-time scaling research, Introl). - Inference cost halving every ~2 months creates economic room for much more test-time compute per query (Epoch AI training cost trends).

Evidence for Claim L: - ARC-AGI-3 is qualitatively different (interactive environments, no stated goals, requires exploration). Current test-time methods assume known input-output structure (ARC-AGI-3, arXiv:2603.24621). - Early evidence shows performance gains from additional inference compute saturate after a model-specific threshold (Test-Time Scaling, Emergent Mind). - Increasing inference compute can paradoxically increase hallucination rates (Energy Cost of Reasoning, arXiv:2505.14733). - All frontier models score <1% on ARC-AGI-3 despite having access to extensive inference budgets (arcprize.org).

Assessment: 55% Claim S. The ARC-AGI-2 trajectory is the strongest evidence for Claim S, but ARC-AGI-3’s interactive format may represent a genuinely qualitative barrier. The history of “this benchmark is qualitatively different” claims has been poor – but the gap is enormous and ARC-AGI-3’s interactive format genuinely changes the problem structure. I keep this at 55% rather than shifting further because the bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern suggests interactive reasoning is where current approaches are weakest.

Crux 2: METR Time Horizon Extrapolation

What this resolves: Whether the exponential trend in autonomous task duration continues, stalls, or accelerates.

Claim S: The METR 50% time horizon continues doubling every 4-7 months, reaching 1-week tasks by mid-2027 and 1-month tasks by late 2027.

Claim L: The trend flattens as tasks approach the ~30-hour boundary of the current task suite, and extending to multi-day tasks reveals error compounding that the current benchmark design doesn’t capture. The 80% horizon (currently 27 minutes) grows much more slowly than the 50% horizon.

Dimension Specification
Metric METR HCAST 80% time horizon for best-performing model (primary); 50% horizon (secondary)
Threshold 80% horizon >24 hours AND 50% horizon >168 hours: Claim S strongly supported. 80% horizon >4 hours OR 50% horizon >72 hours: Claim S moderately supported. 80% horizon <2 hours AND 50% horizon <48 hours: Claim L supported.
Date December 2027
Data source METR HCAST leaderboard and reports (metr.org)

Note: Round 1 used the 50% horizon as the sole metric. The critique correctly identified that a system succeeding on 50% of week-long tasks is not reliable enough for economically meaningful deployment. The 80% horizon is now primary, as it better tracks production-relevant reliability. Both metrics are required to avoid gaming.

Evidence for Claim S: - Fitted doubling time has been ~123 days (4 months) based on 2023-2026 data, accelerating from the earlier 7-month estimate (METR Time Horizon 1.1). - The trajectory from 9 seconds (2020) to 14.5 hours (Feb 2026) spans >5 OOM and has been remarkably consistent on log scale (METR, March 2025). - SWE-bench improvements from 60% to 94% in one year suggest coding agents are crossing meaningful capability thresholds (Epoch AI SWE-bench).

Evidence for Claim L: - HCAST is predominantly software engineering tasks; GUI tasks have 40-100x shorter horizons (METR Clarifying Limitations). - The 80% time horizon (27 minutes) lags the 50% horizon (5 hours) by over 10x, indicating thin-tailed reliability (METR Time Horizon 1.1). - The task suite contains no tasks >30 hours, so extrapolation beyond that is unconstrained by data. - MIT Technology Review called the METR graph “the most misunderstood graph in AI” due to massive variance concealed by the trendline. The 95% CI for a single data point spans an 11x range (MIT Tech Review, Feb 2026). - RE-Bench finding: at 32-hour budgets, humans achieve 2x AI scores. AI advantage exists only at short time budgets (2-4 hours) (RE-Bench, arXiv:2411.15114). - UC Berkeley research showed an automated scanning agent could break all eight major agent benchmarks through reward hacking, suggesting reported scores may be inflated (Stanford AI Index 2026).

Assessment: 45% Claim S (shifted from 40% in Round 1). The trend has been impressively consistent, but the 80%/50% divergence and the benchmark integrity concerns are serious. Shifting slightly toward S because (a) the 80% horizon is a more demanding metric than the 50% one used in Round 1, so the bar is now higher but so is the informational value, and (b) the critique correctly noted that Round 1 overweighted the MIT Technology Review framing relative to the raw trend data.

Crux 3: AI-Automated AI Research

What this resolves: Whether AI systems can autonomously discover novel algorithmic improvements (not just optimize known approaches), creating a positive feedback loop that accelerates progress.

Claim S: By 2028, AI systems autonomously discover at least one significant ML algorithmic improvement that is adopted by frontier labs.

Claim L: AI-assisted research remains limited to implementation speedups, hyperparameter optimization, and incremental refinements. Genuine conceptual breakthroughs continue to require human researchers through at least 2030.

Dimension Specification
Metric Whether an AI agent, given only a problem description and compute budget, produces a verifiable algorithmic improvement that (a) was not in the training data, (b) is adopted in production by at least one frontier lab, and (c) is confirmed by independent replication.
Threshold Binary: such a result exists or does not by the resolution date. A non-ML discovery (drug design, materials science, mathematics) that demonstrates the same autonomous capability counts as partial evidence for Claim S.
Date December 2028
Data source ArXiv, NeurIPS/ICML/ICLR proceedings, frontier lab technical reports, independent replication

Note: Round 1’s resolution criteria relied on labs self-reporting AI credit, which the critique noted is unreliable due to conflicting incentives (marketing vs. employee morale). The revised criteria require independent replication and verifiable novelty.

Evidence for Claim S: - AlphaEvolve re-discovered SOTA solutions for 75% of math problems and found improvements for 20%; achieved 23% FlashAttention speedup and 32% kernel tiling speedup (Google DeepMind blog). - The AI Scientist (Sakana AI) autonomously generated a paper that passed peer review at a top ML workshop (Nature, March 2026; arXiv:2408.06292). - Jack Clark (Anthropic co-founder [COI: co-founder of a company whose fundraising and valuation depend on short timelines]) estimates 60% probability of a system training a more powerful successor without human involvement by end of 2028. - Frontier labs report AI-generated research code jumping from ~10% to ~90%. - ResearchCodeBench: best model implements 37% of novel ML research challenges from published papers (arXiv:2506.02314).

Evidence for Claim L: - The ideation-execution gap (ICLR 2026): AI research ideas score higher pre-execution but drop significantly after implementation. Human ideas retain their scores. “AI ideas appear more creative but are less executable.” (arXiv:2506.20803) - Demis Hassabis [COI: CEO of Google DeepMind, whose competitive positioning benefits from downplaying competitors’ claims while justifying DeepMind’s research approach] (2026): “so far, these systems can’t come up with genuinely new hypotheses,” estimating 5-10 years (Axios exclusive). - PaperBench: leading agents replicate only 20-25% of ICML papers, roughly half the human baseline (arXiv:2504.01848). - RExBench: best agent achieves ~33% on research extension tasks, even with human-written hints (arXiv:2506.22598). - The knowing-doing gap applies to research: models can explain optimal strategies but cannot execute them (Cognaptus analysis). - No AI system has autonomously discovered a significant ML breakthrough to date (May 2026).

Assessment: 35% Claim S (shifted from 30% in Round 1). AlphaEvolve’s results are closer to genuine autonomous discovery than Round 1 acknowledged – the FlashAttention and kernel tiling improvements are real production optimizations, even if they involve search over known problem structures rather than generating new research directions. The gap between “optimization search” and “conceptual innovation” may be smaller than I initially modeled. But the ideation-execution gap is the strongest counter-evidence.

Cross-crux dependency: If Crux 1 (S) AND Crux 2 (S): probability of Crux 3 (S) rises to ~50%. If Crux 1 (L) AND Crux 2 (L): drops to ~15%. If Crux 5 resolves L (pattern matching, not genuine reasoning), Crux 3 (S) probability drops to ~20% because search-based optimization can still work but conceptual innovation becomes implausible.

Crux 4: Pre-Training Scaling Returns

What this resolves: Whether increasing pre-training compute from 5e26 to 1e29+ FLOP produces proportional capability gains.

Claim S: Scaling from 5e26 to 1e28+ FLOP produces benchmark improvements comparable in magnitude to the GPT-3-to-GPT-4 jump.

Claim L: Returns are sharply diminishing. Each OOM of compute buys only incremental benchmark improvement.

Dimension Specification
Metric Humanity’s Last Exam (HLE) score for the best frontier model
Threshold Best HLE score >80% by the resolution date: Claim S supported. 60-80%: ambiguous but trending S. <60%: Claim L supported.
Date December 2028
Data source HLE leaderboard (agi.safe.ai)

Evidence for Claim S: - HLE trajectory: 8% (Jan 2025) to 45-65% (May 2026), roughly 5-8x improvement in 16 months (HLE Leaderboard; PricePerToken HLE). Extrapolation suggests 80%+ by late 2027/early 2028. - Effective compute has been growing at 10.7x/year (2020-2025), faster than most forecasters assumed (Scott Alexander, What Happened With Bio Anchors). - Algorithmic efficiency improves at ~3x/year (~0.5 OOM/year), compounding with hardware gains (Epoch AI algorithmic progress). - Epoch AI projects 5.5-7 OOM of effective compute growth from 2024 to 2030 (Epoch AI: Can AI Scaling Continue Through 2030?).

Evidence for Claim L: - GPT-5 scored higher than GPT-4 on every major benchmark, but the magnitude of improvement was smaller relative to compute investment. The benchmark delta per OOM of additional compute was lower than the GPT-3-to-GPT-4 ratio (IEEE Spectrum: GPT-5 and the Trough). (Note: Round 1 cited “disappointing reception” and media quotes as primary evidence. The critique correctly identified that reception narratives are poor substitutes for benchmark deltas. The relevant evidence is the diminishing benchmark-gain-per-OOM ratio, not media sentiment.) - Ilya Sutskever [COI: founder of SSI, valued at $32B, whose valuation depends on the “new paradigm needed” thesis]: “Pre-training as we have known it is over. The data is finite.” (Sutskever interview) - Each decade of additional compute buys only ~1-2 percentage points of MMLU-Pro improvement (research on 400+ models) (arXiv:2512.20264). - 76% of AAAI-surveyed researchers say scaling alone is “unlikely” or “very unlikely” to yield AGI. [COI note: AAAI members are predominantly academic researchers whose funding depends on the “new paradigm needed” thesis. If scaling works, their research programs lose relevance. This is the same conflict-of-interest logic applied to lab CEOs, but in the opposite direction.] - The data wall: high-quality text data estimated at 10-50T tokens; frontier models approaching this ceiling. Though Epoch AI considers data “unlikely to limit AI scaling through 2030” when multimodal sources are included (Epoch AI data limits).

Assessment: 50% Claim S (shifted from 45% in Round 1). The upward shift reflects (a) replacing the media-reception narrative with actual benchmark data, which is more ambiguous than “disappointing” framing suggested, (b) noting the AAAI survey’s conflict-of-interest symmetry with lab CEO claims, and (c) the HLE trajectory, which objectively shows rapid improvement. The remaining uncertainty is genuine: whether this trajectory bends or continues is the single cleanest empirical test in this analysis.

Cross-crux dependency: Interacts with Crux 6 (compute availability). Even if scaling returns continue, achieving 1e29 FLOP requires solving power and capital constraints.

Crux 5: The Generalization-vs-Memorization Question

What this resolves: Whether frontier model performance reflects genuine reasoning or sophisticated pattern matching.

Round 1 operationalized this via a single perturbation-robustness metric. The critique correctly identified that this conflates “genuine reasoning” with “robustness to perturbation” – a system can reason genuinely and still be fragile, or match patterns robustly. This revision splits the crux into two sub-metrics.

Claim S: Frontier models have developed genuine reasoning capabilities that generalize beyond training distribution, as evidenced by both behavioral robustness and mechanistic evidence.

Claim L: Even frontier models are primarily doing sophisticated pattern matching, as evidenced by brittleness under perturbation, mechanistic analysis, and the jagged capability profile.

Dimension Specification
Metric (Behavioral) Performance drop (%) on RoR-Bench-style perturbation tasks for the best frontier model on AIME-level problems
Metric (Mechanistic) Whether causal intervention studies on frontier model internals (activation patching, circuit analysis) reveal genuine computational subroutines vs. pattern-matching coordination
Threshold (Behavioral) If best frontier model maintains >85% of unperturbed accuracy under perturbation: supports S. If accuracy drops >25%: supports L.
Threshold (Mechanistic) If published mechanistic interpretability work identifies verified computational circuits (not just correlational features) in frontier models: supports S. If best available evidence shows pattern-matching coordination: supports L.
Date June 2027
Data source Published perturbation benchmarks, mechanistic interpretability papers (Anthropic, Google DeepMind, independent labs)

Evidence for Claim S: - RRB (April 2026) found frontier proprietary models exhibit “resilience” to perturbations, maintaining most accuracy. This is genuinely different from open-weights models’ behavior (RRB, arXiv:2604.08571). - Test-time compute enables models to verify and correct their reasoning chains. - GPQA Diamond scores (94.2%) exceed PhD experts (69.7%) on questions specifically designed to require expert reasoning, not memorization (PricePerToken GPQA). - ARC-AGI-2 progress demonstrates genuine novel abstraction capability (since ARC tasks are designed to be out-of-distribution). The key technique is program synthesis, not memorization (ARC Prize 2025 Results).

Evidence for Claim L: - RoR-Bench: even o1 and DeepSeek-R1 suffer 60% drops on elementary school math with single phrase changes (arXiv:2504.00509). (However, the RRB study suggests this may reflect open-weights model weakness rather than a fundamental LLM limitation.) - GSM-Symbolic: up to 65% accuracy drop when only numerical values change while reasoning structure is preserved (GSM-Symbolic, ICLR 2025). - The analog clock problem: IMO-gold performance coexists with 50.1% accuracy on reading clocks (Stanford AI Index 2026). - The enterprise deployment gap (37% between lab and field) suggests benchmark scores overstate real capability (Kili Technology AI Benchmarks 2026).

Assessment: 50/50. This may be a false dichotomy: frontier models likely do some genuine reasoning AND some pattern matching, and the ratio varies by domain. The proprietary/open-weights robustness split is the most informative signal. The answer is probably “both, in proportions that vary by task.” Maintaining at 50% because the evidence genuinely does not resolve this.

Crux 6: Compute Scaling Continuation

What this resolves: Whether the infrastructure buildout actually delivers the projected compute.

Claim S: Power, packaging, and capital constraints are surmountable. Frontier training runs reach 1e28+ FLOP by 2028.

Claim L: Grid interconnection delays, CoWoS/HBM bottlenecks, and capital sustainability concerns limit actual frontier training runs to ~5e27 FLOP through 2028.

Dimension Specification
Metric Largest known training run (in FLOP)
Threshold >5e27 FLOP by resolution date: Claim S supported. <2e27 FLOP: Claim L supported.
Date December 2028
Data source Epoch AI Notable Models database, public announcements from frontier labs

Evidence for Claim S: - Hyperscaler capex projected at $660-690B in 2026, rising toward $1T in 2027 (Futurum AI Capex 2026; CNBC $1T in 2027). - Current frontier is ~5e26 FLOP (Grok 4); 10x growth to 5e27 requires only incremental scaling (Epoch AI trends). - NVIDIA Rubin (2026) delivers 2.5x per-chip improvement. NVL144 provides 3.3x system-level gain (NVIDIA GTC 2026; Tom’s Hardware Rubin specs). - Behind-the-meter gas can deploy in ~18 months, partially bypassing grid delays (Kroll Tax Insights Q1 2026). - TSMC advanced node capacity growing 40%+ YoY; CoWoS at ~80% CAGR (TrendForce; Fusion Worldwide CoWoS/HBM). - Anthropic committed to >1M Ironwood chips beginning 2026 (SemiAnalysis TPUv7).

Evidence for Claim L: - Grid interconnection queue: 2,600 GW requested, median time to operation ~5 years. Google reports up to 12-year delays (Enki AI Grid Delays; DataCenterKnowledge). - CCGT lead times stretched to 5-7 years due to order backlogs (Medium: The 5-Year Turbine Wait). - Combined hyperscaler capex consumes 94% of operating cash flows after dividends (Introl hyperscaler capex $600B). - MIT study: 95% of GenAI pilots fail. If revenue disappoints, capex moderation is inevitable (Fortune/MIT Report). - Goldman Sachs: $700B in 2025 AI investment produced “essentially zero” GDP growth (Fortune/Goldman Sachs). - BlackRock CEO: the AI investment race “will produce bankruptcies.”

Assessment: 65% Claim S. Unchanged from Round 1. The participants are enormously well-capitalized, and 5e27 FLOP is only 10x the current frontier – a modest target given historical growth rates. The binding risk is gradual capex moderation, not a sudden crash.

Crux 7: The Enterprise Deployment Gap

What this resolves: Whether AI capabilities translate into economic value at scale.

Claim S: Enterprise AI deployment matures rapidly. Failure rates drop from 80-95% to <50% by 2028 as tooling and organizational learning catch up.

Claim L: The failure rates reflect fundamental capability mismatches that improve slowly. Most AI value accrues to narrow use cases rather than transformative automation.

Dimension Specification
Metric Enterprise AI project success rate as reported by major consulting/research firms, disaggregated by application domain (coding tools, customer service, document generation, general enterprise).
Threshold If coding tools + document generation report >60% success rate AND general enterprise reports >30%: Claim S supported. If even high-performing categories remain below 40%: Claim L supported.
Date December 2027
Data source Gartner, McKinsey, RAND enterprise AI surveys, with domain disaggregation required

Note: Round 1 used an undifferentiated aggregate success rate. The critique correctly identified that the 80-95% failure rates aggregate across all company sizes, AI applications, and time periods. Coding tools specifically have a much lower failure rate and may generate enough revenue to sustain the compute buildout even if other categories struggle.

Evidence for Claim S: - Claude Code reached $2.5B ARR in 9 months; AI coding tools are producing measurable productivity gains (AI Futures Q1 2026). - AI-assisted coding: PRs per author up 20%, deployment frequency up across the board (Cortex Engineering Benchmark Report 2026). - Inference cost halving every 2 months makes previously uneconomic applications viable (Epoch AI training cost trends). - Klarna’s AI handled 55-70% of support volume with comparable satisfaction scores, even after the much-discussed partial reversal (FinTech Weekly; Entrepreneur). (The reversal was about complex cases requiring judgment, not about the basic volume handling, which remained in production.)

Evidence for Claim L: - 80.3% failure rate (RAND, 2,400+ initiatives); 95% see zero return from GenAI pilots (MIT NANDA) (Folio3 AI Failure Rate; Fortune/MIT). These are broad aggregates, but the breadth of failure is itself informative – it’s not just a few bad implementations. - 42% of companies scrapped at least one AI initiative in 2025 (up from 17% prior year). Average sunk cost: $7.2M (AI Governance Today). - Incidents per pull request increased 23.5% with AI assistance; change failure rates up 30%. AI acts as an “indiscriminate amplifier.” (Cortex Engineering Benchmark Report 2026) - The enterprise gap may narrow primarily for bounded tasks (coding, drafting) while persisting for open-ended ones (strategy, research, judgment calls), consistent with the bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern.

Assessment: 50% Claim S (shifted from 40% in Round 1). The upward shift reflects (a) disaggregating the evidence by domain, which shows that coding tools specifically are clearly working, (b) recognizing that the revenue from high-performing niches may be sufficient to sustain investment even without broad enterprise success, and (c) the critique’s point that the aggregated failure data overweights pre-frontier-era deployments and unsophisticated companies.

Cross-crux dependency: If Crux 7 remains L, Crux 6 probability shifts from 65% to ~50% (capex moderation). If Crux 7 resolves S, Crux 6 shifts to ~75%.

Crux 8: Architectural Sufficiency

What this resolves: Whether the transformer/LLM architecture (with extensions) is sufficient for AGI, or whether a fundamentally new architecture is needed.

Definitional boundary (added in response to critique): “Current approaches” means: autoregressive transformer with pre-training, RLHF/constitutional AI post-training, tool use via function calling, inference-time search/chain-of-thought, and multimodal training. Extensions that require a qualitatively new training paradigm (e.g., persistent weight updates from deployment experience, world-model pre-training objectives like JEPA, learned causal reasoning modules) count as “new architecture.”

Claim S: Transformers + test-time compute + tool use + scaffolding + multimodal training are sufficient. No architectural paradigm shift is needed.

Claim L: LLMs lack physical grounding, causal reasoning, persistent memory, and genuine world models. A fundamentally new architecture is required, adding 5-10+ years.

Dimension Specification
Metric (Composite) A composite of four gap-closing indicators, all measured for the best frontier model: (1) Escher-Bench score, (2) ARC-AGI-3 score, (3) METR 80% time horizon, (4) MIRROR or successor metacognition benchmark score
Threshold If >=3 of 4 indicators show >50% gap closure (from current levels toward human baselines) by the resolution date: Claim S strongly supported. If <=1 shows >50% gap closure: Claim L supported.
Date December 2028
Data source Published benchmark results across all four metrics

Note: Round 1 used only Escher-Bench, which the critique identified as insufficient for a crux about architectural breadth. The composite metric tests whether gap closure is broad or narrow.

Evidence for Claim S: - Historical pattern: every “this requires a new paradigm” claim has been refuted within 2-3 years. ARC-AGI-1 was supposed to require program synthesis; scaling + test-time compute solved it (ARC Prize 2025 Results). - Transformers have shown unexpected transfer to modalities they weren’t designed for (vision, audio, robotics). - Test-time compute represents a genuine paradigm extension within the transformer framework. - Claude Opus 4.7 scores 94.2% on GPQA Diamond, a benchmark originally thought to be extremely difficult (PricePerToken GPQA).

Evidence for Claim L: - 76% of AAAI experts say scaling current approaches is “unlikely” to yield AGI. [COI: academic researchers’ funding depends on the “new paradigm needed” thesis.] - LeCun (Turing Award winner) [COI: runs Meta FAIR, which is invested in the “new paradigm” thesis and world-model research]: LLMs are a “dead end” for AGI (Medium: World Models vs. Word Models; CryptoBriefing). - Sutskever [COI: SSI valued at $32B based on the “new paradigm” thesis]: “2026 onward will be another age of research.” (EA Forum highlights) - Physical reasoning (Escher-Bench: 57.3% vs. human 90.6%) has improved much more slowly than math/coding despite using the same models (EscherVerse, arXiv:2601.01547). - ARC-AGI-3 (0.37%) demonstrates that interactive reasoning remains essentially impossible for current transformers (arXiv:2603.24621). - Chollet: “Current paradigm is fatally flawed.” [COI: created ARC-AGI, whose relevance depends on current architectures failing.]

Assessment: 50% Claim S (shifted from 45% in Round 1). The upward shift reflects two corrections: (a) the expert opinion cited against current architectures comes almost entirely from researchers with conflicts of interest favoring the “new paradigm” thesis, and (b) the historical base rate of “this benchmark requires a paradigm shift” claims being overturned is very strong. However, the ARC-AGI-3 gap and the physical reasoning stagnation are genuinely concerning. The bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern suggests that the architecture may be sufficient for most bounded tasks but insufficient for integrative reasoning – and which of these matters more depends on the AGI definition (see Crux 12).

Cross-crux dependency: If Crux 8 resolves L, estimates for Crux 1 shift from 55% to ~30%, Crux 3 from 35% to ~15%, and timelines extend by 5-10 years.

Crux 9: Recursive Improvement Speed (NEW)

What this resolves: Even if AI automates AI research (Crux 3 Claim S), how fast does the resulting feedback loop run? This is the crux that separates “gradual acceleration” from “intelligence explosion” scenarios.

Claim S (fast): AI-automated AI research produces >10x faster progress (vs. human-only baseline) within 2 years of the first autonomous algorithmic discovery, compressing the remaining path to AGI from decades to years.

Claim L (slow): The acceleration is modest (2-3x), bottlenecked by compute availability, verification costs, diminishing returns to algorithmic search, and the irreducible difficulty of conceptual innovation. The feedback loop helps but does not transform timelines.

Dimension Specification
Metric Rate of algorithmic efficiency improvement (OOM/year) as measured by Epoch AI, before and after the first AI-autonomous discovery (conditional on Crux 3 S)
Threshold If the rate exceeds 2 OOM/year (vs. current ~0.5 OOM/year) for a sustained 12-month period: Claim S supported. If it remains below 1 OOM/year: Claim L supported.
Date 24 months after Crux 3 resolution (conditional)
Data source Epoch AI algorithmic progress tracking, frontier lab training reports

Evidence for Claim S (fast): - Aschenbrenner [COI: runs a $5.5B AI investment fund] and Kokotajlo model recursive improvement as the key mechanism for compressing timelines from decades to years (Situational Awareness; AI 2027). - The AI 2027 scenario assumes AI-automated research begins an acceleration that reaches ASI within 1-2 years (AI 2027). - AlphaEvolve already shows AI can search algorithmic improvement spaces at speeds impossible for human researchers (Google DeepMind blog). If the search space is large and fruitful, the feedback loop could be fast.

Evidence for Claim L (slow): - Davidson’s compute-centric model produces gradual acceleration, not explosion. His median takeoff time (20% to 100% task automation) is measured in years, not months (Epoch AI: Interactive Takeoff Model; 80,000 Hours podcast). - Christiano [relatively low COI: AISI, focused on alignment] emphasizes “gradual drift” rather than fast takeoff (EA Forum). - The ideation-execution gap suggests that even AI-generated ideas that look good require extensive real-world validation, which bottlenecks the loop (arXiv:2506.20803). - Compute and verification are serial bottlenecks. Discovering an algorithmic improvement takes time; verifying it requires training runs; deploying it requires engineering work. Each step has irreducible latency. - Historical base rate: the two biggest algorithmic improvements (Transformer architecture, Chinchilla scaling) each took years to fully exploit despite human researchers understanding them immediately (On the Origin of Algorithmic Progress, arXiv:2511.21622).

Assessment: 30% Claim S (fast). Even if AI automates research, the bottlenecks of verification, compute, and diminishing returns to search likely keep the acceleration modest. An “intelligence explosion” requires that each improvement makes the next improvement easier to find, which has not been demonstrated and seems unlikely given that the low-hanging algorithmic fruit has been picked. I model the most likely outcome as 3-5x acceleration, significant but not explosive.

Crux 10: Regulatory Disruption (NEW)

What this resolves: Whether regulation significantly constrains the development or deployment of frontier AI.

Claim S (light regulation): Regulatory capture by AI labs succeeds, or regulation focuses on deployment rather than development, leaving frontier training largely unconstrained. The EU AI Act’s impact is limited to compliance costs.

Claim L (heavy regulation): A major safety incident triggers emergency legislation (analogous to Three Mile Island for nuclear). Frontier development is significantly constrained – training runs above a threshold require licensing, compute caps are imposed, or deployment restrictions limit the most capable systems.

Dimension Specification
Metric Whether any G7 country implements binding compute caps, training-run licensing requirements, or deployment restrictions that apply to frontier model development (not just high-risk applications)
Threshold Binary: such regulation exists or does not by the resolution date.
Date December 2028
Data source Official government publications, regulatory agency announcements

Evidence for Claim S (light): - The EU AI Act (fully enforceable August 2026) focuses on deployment of high-risk applications, not on training runs per se (EU AI Act). - U.S. regulatory environment has shifted toward industry self-regulation under current administration. - AI labs have successfully framed safety as an argument for their own continued development (“we need to be at the frontier to make it safe”). - No G7 country has proposed binding compute caps as of May 2026.

Evidence for Claim L (heavy): - Every major safety evaluation has found frontier models engaging in misaligned behavior (scheming, deception faking). A high-profile incident could shift politics rapidly (Anthropic: Emergent Misalignment; Observer Effect). - The Clawdbot 72-hour crisis and Replit database destruction illustrate the kind of incidents that could trigger regulatory backlash (72-Hour Meltdown, Medium; Fortune: Replit). - Historical precedent: Three Mile Island (1979) effectively halted U.S. nuclear construction for decades. A comparable AI incident could have similar effects. - Multiple countries are developing AI-specific regulatory frameworks. The EU AI Act is a floor, not a ceiling (Wilson Sonsini: 2026 AI Regulatory Preview).

Assessment: 70% Claim S (light). The current political environment strongly favors industry self-regulation, and the economic incentives to continue development are enormous. However, a sufficiently dramatic safety incident could change this rapidly. The tail risk of heavy regulation is real but requires a triggering event that has not yet occurred.

Crux 11: Embodiment Requirement (NEW)

What this resolves: Whether AGI requires embodied experience in the physical world, or whether sufficiently capable text/code/vision models can achieve general intelligence without physical interaction.

Claim S (embodiment not required): Sufficiently rich multimodal training (text, images, video, simulation) combined with tool use and scaffolding provides adequate grounding. Physical reasoning gaps (Escher-Bench) are solvable through better training data and video understanding, not embodiment.

Claim L (embodiment required): LeCun’s critique is correct: genuine understanding of the physical world requires interacting with it. Moravec’s paradox reflects a deep truth – tasks trivial for embodied humans are fundamentally hard for disembodied AI. Robotics progress has its own set of constraints (hardware, safety, sample efficiency) that add 5-10 years.

Dimension Specification
Metric Whether a non-embodied frontier model (no robotic actuators, no real-world physical interaction during training or inference) achieves >80% on Escher-Bench AND >50% on ARC-AGI-3 by the resolution date
Threshold Both thresholds met: Claim S supported. Neither met: Claim L supported. One met: ambiguous.
Date December 2028
Data source Published benchmark results

Evidence for Claim S: - Video understanding models are improving rapidly, providing vicarious physical experience. - Simulation environments (MuJoCo, Isaac Sim) provide physical grounding without embodiment. - Many human cognitive tasks (mathematics, law, medicine, coding, writing) do not require embodiment. - If AGI is defined narrowly enough (Crux 12), embodiment may be irrelevant.

Evidence for Claim L: - Best household robot (Spirit v1.5) achieves only 50% task success. Robots use tiny models (2-7B parameters) compared to language models (CritPt Leaderboard, Artificial Analysis). - The analog clock problem (50.1% for IMO-gold models) suggests that spatial reasoning deficits are deeply architectural (Stanford AI Index 2026). - Escher-Bench progress has been much slower than math/coding progress despite using the same models (EscherVerse, arXiv:2601.01547). - Infants develop physical intuition through embodied interaction before language acquisition – this ordering may be causally necessary.

Assessment: 55% Claim S. Most cognitive tasks that matter for economic impact (and most AGI definitions) do not require physical embodiment. The physical reasoning gaps are real but may be solvable through multimodal training on video and simulation data. Embodiment matters more for some AGI definitions than others (see Crux 12).

Crux 12: AGI Definition (NEW)

What this resolves: Much of the apparent disagreement in the forecasting community is definitional. This crux makes the definitional choice explicit and operationalizes its consequences.

This synthesis maintains two tiers:

Tier A – Narrow Technical Superiority (NTS): AI systems that exceed the best human expert on the majority of well-defined cognitive tasks when measured individually (math, coding, writing, analysis, etc.), though not necessarily integrated, reliable, or autonomous.

Tier B – Broad Cognitive Parity (BCP): AI systems that can reliably replace a college-educated knowledge worker across a wide range of unstructured tasks, including novel problem-solving, sustained multi-day projects, social reasoning, and adaptation to unfamiliar domains. This requires integrated, reliable, sustained competence.

Dimension Specification
Metric (Tier A) Best frontier model exceeds human expert performance on >75% of a curated set of 20 diverse cognitive benchmarks spanning math, coding, science, writing, and reasoning
Metric (Tier B) METR 80% time horizon exceeds 1 week AND ARC-AGI-3 score >50% AND enterprise AI success rate (general category) >50%
Date Ongoing – used to frame all timeline estimates
Data source All benchmarks cited in this analysis

Which cruxes apply to which tier:

Crux Relevant to Tier A (NTS) Relevant to Tier B (BCP)
1 (Test-time compute) Yes Yes
2 (METR trend) Marginally Critical
3 (AI research automation) Yes Yes
4 (Pre-training scaling) Critical Important
5 (Generalization) Important Critical
6 (Compute availability) Important Important
7 (Enterprise deployment) Marginally Critical
8 (Architecture) Marginally Critical
9 (Recursive improvement speed) Conditional on 3 Conditional on 3
10 (Regulatory disruption) Marginally Important
11 (Embodiment) Marginally Important

3.3 Scenario Clusters

Rather than treating cruxes independently (which produced the joint probability inconsistency in Round 1), I model four scenario clusters:

Scenario A: Fast Convergence (weight: 15%) Cruxes 1, 3, 4, 8 all resolve S. Architecture is sufficient, scaling continues, AI automates research, and a mild feedback loop compresses timelines. NTS by 2027-2028, BCP by 2030-2032.

Scenario B: Steady Progress (weight: 40%) Most cruxes resolve ambiguously or mildly S. Current approaches work for bounded tasks, gradual progress on open-ended ones. No explosive feedback loop, but continued improvement. NTS by 2028-2030, BCP by 2033-2037.

Scenario C: Architectural Reset (weight: 25%) Crux 8 resolves L, requiring a new paradigm. Current approaches plateau on the hard benchmarks (ARC-AGI-3, physical reasoning, sustained autonomy). NTS by 2029-2031 (achievable under current architecture for bounded tasks), BCP by 2037-2045.

Scenario D: Investment Contraction (weight: 10%) Crux 7 (L) cascades into Crux 6 (L). Enterprise disappointment triggers capex moderation, slowing the compute trajectory. Timelines stretch regardless of architectural questions. NTS by 2030-2033, BCP by 2038-2045.

Scenario E: Regulatory Halt (weight: 5%) Crux 10 (L). A safety incident triggers restrictive regulation. Development slows significantly. Adds 3-7 years to all estimates.

Scenario F: Wild Card – Fast Recursive Improvement (weight: 5%) Crux 3 (S) AND Crux 9 (S, fast). An intelligence explosion scenario where AI-automated research produces >10x acceleration. NTS by 2027, BCP by 2028-2030. Low weight because it requires multiple cruxes resolving favorably and the recursive improvement being genuinely fast, which historical evidence does not support.

4. Timeline Estimates

4.1 Narrow Technical Superiority

Definition: AI systems that exceed the best human expert on the majority of well-defined cognitive tasks when measured individually.

Estimate: 2028-2031 (median ~2029)

Reasoning: Current trajectory suggests continued benchmark saturation on bounded tasks. Competition math is solved; graduate-level science is solved; coding is approaching saturation. The remaining hard benchmarks (HLE, FrontierMath) are being closed at 5-8x/year improvement rates. This estimate is robust across scenarios A, B, and C because even if a new architecture is needed for BCP, NTS on bounded tasks is achievable under current approaches.

Weighted calculation: 0.15(2027.5) + 0.40(2029) + 0.25(2030) + 0.10(2031.5) + 0.05(2031) + 0.05(2027) = 2029.2

Key caveat: “Narrow technical superiority” may be less meaningful than it sounds. Jagged capability profiles (IMO gold + 50% clock reading) mean superiority on any given task doesn’t imply consistent competence. The bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern means NTS coexists with fundamental gaps.

4.2 Broad Cognitive Parity

Definition: AI systems that can reliably replace a college-educated knowledge worker across a wide range of unstructured tasks.

Estimate: 2032-2042 (median ~2035)

Reasoning: This requires closing several gaps that show no clear path to resolution under current architectures:

  • Sustained coherence (35-minute degradation cliff must extend to multi-day operation)
  • Novel environment reasoning (ARC-AGI-3: 0.37% must approach human-level)
  • Reliability (80% time horizon must extend from 27 minutes to weeks)
  • Physical/common-sense reasoning (33+ point gap on Escher-Bench)
  • Metacognitive self-regulation (MIRROR results show current inability, though benchmark is unreplicated)

Weighted calculation: 0.15(2031) + 0.40(2035) + 0.25(2041) + 0.10(2041.5) + 0.05(2043) + 0.05(2029) = 2036.0

How this follows from the crux probabilities: Unlike Round 1, the median is derived from the weighted scenario clusters, which are themselves derived from the crux probability assessments. The fast scenario (A) has only 15% weight because it requires 4+ cruxes resolving S simultaneously. The slow scenarios (C, D) collectively carry 35% weight, pulling the median later. The dominant scenario (B, 40% weight) centers around 2035.

4.3 Calibration Against Existing Forecasts

AI Safety Leaders Survey (Feb 2026) 2033 (IQR: 2031-2036) 2029 2036
Kokotajlo (Q1 2026) ~2028 (TED-AI) 2029 2036

Professional superforecaster 40% by 2030 Consistent –

Directional lean: This synthesis leans slightly later than the Metaculus median for BCP (2036 vs. 2033) and slightly earlier for NTS (2029 vs. 2033). The split reflects the definitional distinction: most forecasters use an AGI definition between my two tiers. When the definition is held constant, the disagreement narrows substantially.

Explicit acknowledgment of remaining lean: Even after rebalancing, this synthesis assigns somewhat more weight to longer timelines than the prediction market consensus. This reflects (a) genuine uncertainty about whether the bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern resolves within current architectures, and (b) a prior that the historical base rate of over-optimistic AGI predictions should carry some weight – though adjusted for the fact that current capabilities are genuinely closer to the target than in any previous generation.

4.4 Key Risks to These Estimates

Risks that could shorten timelines: - An architectural breakthrough (analogous to Transformer’s impact) that addresses the generalization gap. SSI, Meta FAIR, and Google DeepMind are all pursuing this. - AI-automated research producing a genuine feedback loop earlier than expected (Crux 3 S AND Crux 9 S). - Underestimation of test-time compute scaling’s ceiling (Crux 1 S). - The “bounded” frontier shifting faster than expected as scaffolding and tool use expand what counts as bounded.

Risks that could lengthen timelines: - Investment bubble correction. If AI revenue disappoints in 2027-2028, capex could contract 30-50%, slowing the compute trajectory (Crux 7 L -> Crux 6 L). - Regulatory constraints. A major safety incident could trigger restrictive legislation (Crux 10 L). - The reliability problem proves fundamental. If the 35-minute degradation cliff reflects deep architectural limitations, multi-day autonomous operation may require the “new architecture” path (Crux 8 L). - The historical base rate. Every generation of AI researchers for 70 years has predicted AGI in 20-30 years and been wrong. This should carry some weight, but less than in Round 1 – adjusted for the fact that no previous generation was scoring 94% on PhD-level science questions or 100% on competition math.

5. Meta-Observations

5.1 The Conflict-of-Interest Landscape

Nearly every prominent forecaster has significant financial stakes in their prediction. This revision applies the conflict-of-interest analysis symmetrically:

Biased toward shorter timelines (financial incentives to promote urgency): - Lab CEOs (Amodei, Altman, Hassabis, Suleyman): fundraising, valuations, talent recruitment - AI investors (Aschenbrenner, Schmidt, Musk): direct financial returns on thesis - Hardware vendors (Huang): market cap tied to AI demand

Biased toward longer timelines (financial incentives to promote “new paradigm needed”): - Academic researchers (AAAI survey majority, LeCun, Marcus): continued fundamental research funding - Alternative-architecture researchers (Sutskever/SSI, Chollet): valuation/relevance depends on current paradigm failing - AI safety researchers (partial): urgency of safety work depends on timelines being neither too short (nothing to do) nor too long (not urgent), creating complex incentives

Least conflicted sources: Prediction markets (Metaculus, Polymarket), superforecaster surveys, Epoch AI empirical tracking. These cluster around 2030-2035, which anchors my estimates.

5.2 The Saturation-and-Replace Cycle

A meta-pattern: benchmarks are introduced, saturated in 6-18 months, then replaced by harder versions. This has played out for MMLU -> MMLU-Pro -> HLE, MATH -> AIME -> FrontierMath, ARC-AGI-1 -> ARC-AGI-2 -> ARC-AGI-3. Each replacement claims to be “unsaturatable,” and each is eventually saturated – but the time varies from <1 year (ARC-AGI-2) to “still open” (FrontierMath Tier 4, ARC-AGI-3). Whether the currently hard benchmarks represent genuine capability frontiers or merely the latest in a series of temporary barriers is itself informative but underdetermined.

5.3 The Bounded-Good / Open-Ended-Bad Pattern

The most important structural finding across the entire analysis: AI performance is strong on bounded, well-specified tasks and weak on open-ended, interactive, sustained tasks. This pattern predicts:

  • NTS (Tier A) is achievable under current approaches, because it requires superiority on individual bounded tasks.
  • BCP (Tier B) may require either (a) the boundary to shift dramatically outward as scaffolding improves, or (b) a new approach to open-ended reasoning.
  • Enterprise deployment succeeds for bounded applications (coding tools, document drafting) and struggles for open-ended ones (strategy, research, judgment). This explains why the aggregate failure data is so high while specific niches thrive.
  • The METR time horizon extrapolation is the key empirical test: if the 80% horizon extends to multi-day tasks, the boundary is shifting. If it stalls, the boundary is structural.
5.4 What Would Change My Mind
  • Toward shorter timelines: ARC-AGI-3 scores reaching >10% for frontier models within 6 months; the METR 80% time horizon exceeding 4 hours; an AI system autonomously discovering a significant algorithmic improvement (Crux 3 S); algorithmic efficiency improvement rate exceeding 1 OOM/year.
  • Toward longer timelines: METR 50% time horizon stalling below 48 hours for >12 months; HLE scores plateauing below 60% despite larger training runs; major investment pullback (capex growth falling below 20%/year); a prominent safety incident that triggers restrictive regulation; mechanistic interpretability work showing that frontier model “reasoning” reduces to pattern-matching coordination.

The Pessimist Case: Broad Cognitive Parity Is Likely 2040–2060+

Executive Summary

Core claim: Broad cognitive parity (BCP) — AI matching human performance across all cognitive domains, not just narrow benchmarks — is most likely to arrive between 2040 and 2055, with a median estimate of ~2045. The synthesis document’s median of 2036 is too aggressive by approximately a decade.

Why: Current AI systems exhibit a structural pattern — dominance on bounded, well-specified tasks paired with near-total failure on open-ended, interactive, long-horizon tasks. This pattern has persisted across multiple model generations, through orders-of-magnitude increases in compute and data, and despite billions in investment. The gap is not closing at a rate consistent with BCP by the mid-2030s. Closing it requires not just incremental scaling but architectural advances that do not yet exist, infrastructure that takes 5–7+ years to build, and sustained investment that current ROI metrics do not support.

Probability distribution:

Timeline Probability
Before 2032 5%
2032–2037 15%
2037–2045 35%
2045–2055 30%
After 2055 15%

Weighted median: ~2045. This is approximately 9 years later than the synthesis median of 2036, driven primarily by: (1) the bounded-good/open-ended-bad structural gap, (2) diminishing returns to scaling, (3) infrastructure and energy bottlenecks, and (4) the historical base rate of overoptimistic AI predictions.

The ten strongest evidence points, in brief:

  1. ARC-AGI-3: 0.37% vs 100% human — a 270x gap on novel reasoning that scaling hasn’t dented
  2. METR 80% time horizon: 27 minutes — reliable autonomous work collapses after half an hour
  3. GPT-5 diminishing returns — smaller gains per OOM despite massive compute increase
  4. 80–95% enterprise AI failure rates — lab capability is not translating to production value
  5. Error compounding math — 85% per-step accuracy yields only 20% success on 10-step workflows
  6. $700B invested, “essentially zero” GDP growth — the ROI gap threatens investment sustainability
  7. 4–7 year grid interconnection waits — physical infrastructure constrains compute scaling
  8. Ideation-execution gap — AI ideas degrade post-execution; research automation is slower than claimed
  9. 70 years of overoptimistic AI predictions — 100% base rate of being wrong
  10. LeCun’s $1B bet against LLMs — revealed preference from the field’s deepest experts

Key argument structure: The pessimist case rests on ten interlocking arguments, organized into three categories:

  1. Capability gaps that benchmarks miss (Sections 1–3): The benchmarks that saturate measure the easy part of cognition. The hard parts — novel reasoning, sustained autonomy, metacognition, error recovery — show little progress and face architectural barriers.

  2. Physical and economic constraints on the scaling trajectory (Sections 4–5, 8): Even if algorithms continue to improve, compute scaling faces diminishing returns, infrastructure bottlenecks with multi-year lead times, and investment sustainability risks from a massive ROI gap.

  3. Structural reasons the gap takes a decade+ to close (Sections 6–7, 9): Historical precedent, the ideation-execution gap blocking recursive improvement, serial dependencies among capability gaps, and the persistent relevance of Moravec’s paradox.

1. The Benchmark Illusion: Why Saturation ≠ Real Capability

1.1 The Selective Reporting Problem

Optimists point to benchmark saturation as evidence of rapid progress: AIME scores reaching 100%, GPQA at 94.2%, MMLU approaching ceiling. But this is a textbook case of selecting on the dependent variable. The benchmarks that saturate quickly are precisely the ones measuring bounded, well-specified tasks — the capability profile where current architectures excel.

The benchmarks that measure what actually matters for BCP tell a radically different story:

Benchmark What It Measures Best AI Humans Gap
ARC-AGI-3 Novel interactive reasoning 0.37% 100% 270x
FrontierMath Tier 4 Research-level math ~6–48% Expert solve Large
HLE Expert knowledge breadth 45–65% ~90% 25–45pp
Escher-Bench Physical/spatial reasoning 57.3% 90.6% 33pp
PhysReason (hard) Physics reasoning 31.95% — Severe
RoR-Bench Reasoning robustness -60% accuracy Stable Catastrophic

Sources: ARC-AGI-3 (Chollet), ARC-AGI-3 via The Decoder

This table reveals a pattern: the larger the gap, the more the benchmark measures open-ended, novel, or physically-grounded reasoning. The benchmarks that saturate (AIME, GPQA, MMLU) all share a key property — they are static, well-defined, and drawn from distributions similar to training data. The ones that don’t (ARC-AGI-3, Escher-Bench, PhysReason) require capabilities that next-token prediction doesn’t naturally develop.

1.2 ARC-AGI-3: The 270x Gap

ARC-AGI-3 deserves special attention because it is the cleanest test of genuinely novel reasoning. Each environment is unique, interactive, and requires real-time adaptation — there is no training distribution to memorize from. Humans solve 100% of these environments. All frontier AI systems score below 1%. Even GPT-4.5 and Llama 4 score 0% on ARC-2 without test-time augmentation.

François Chollet’s diagnosis is precise: models fail because they rely on memorization and retrieval; each environment is unique and requires genuine adaptation. This is not a gap that closes with more training data or larger models — it requires a different approach to cognition entirely.

The scale of this gap matters. Going from 0.37% to human-level (100%) is not a matter of incremental improvement. It is not like going from MMLU 60% to MMLU 90%, which happened within a few model generations. ARC-AGI-3 performance has barely budged across multiple frontier model releases precisely because it measures something orthogonal to what scaling improves.

To put the 270x gap in perspective: if progress on ARC-AGI-3 matched the fastest benchmark improvement rate ever observed (roughly 2x per year on bounded tasks), it would take approximately 8 years to reach human level. But ARC-AGI-3 improvement has been far slower than 2x per year — it has been approximately zero across multiple model generations. The gap is not just large; it is not closing at any measurable rate.

Operationalized claim: If ARC-AGI-3 scores remain below 10% for frontier models through 2028, this constitutes strong evidence that current architectures cannot achieve BCP without fundamental innovation.

1.3 Reasoning Robustness: The Surface-Level Pattern Matching Problem

The RoR-Bench results are perhaps the most damning single data point for the “models are genuinely reasoning” thesis. Models that achieve high accuracy on standard math and code problems suffer up to 60% accuracy drops when a single condition in the problem is modified. Chain-of-thought perturbation studies show that adversarial perturbations (numeric shifts, shuffling distractors) produce massive performance swings, and MCQ option order shifts model choices by 10–20+ points.

This directly undermines the “models are genuinely reasoning” interpretation. If you can change a trivial surface feature of a problem and flip the answer, the model is pattern-matching, not reasoning. A human mathematician who can solve a problem doesn’t suddenly fail when you change a constant from 7 to 11.

The implications for BCP are severe. Real-world cognitive tasks are not presented in standardized formats. They come with irrelevant details, unusual framings, and novel combinations of familiar elements. A system that breaks under trivial perturbations cannot reliably handle real-world cognitive work, regardless of how well it performs on clean, standardized benchmarks.

Consider what this means concretely: a model that scores 95% on a coding benchmark might score 35–55% on the same problems with minor, semantically-irrelevant changes to variable names, function signatures, or problem descriptions. This is not a model that has learned to code; it is a model that has learned to pattern-match on coding benchmarks. The distinction matters enormously for deployment.

1.4 Higher-Order Theory of Mind

Higher-order theory of mind — reasoning about what A believes that B believes that C intends — collapses to near-zero accuracy at 4th+ order for current models. This may seem like an academic concern, but it is foundational to complex social reasoning: negotiation, management, diplomacy, teaching, and any situation where multiple agents have nested beliefs about each other.

A CEO making a strategic decision must reason about what competitors believe about what customers expect about what regulators will do. This is routine 4th-order ToM. Current AI cannot do this, and the capability shows no signs of emerging from scaling.

1.5 What Benchmarks Don’t Even Measure

Several capabilities critical for BCP have no meaningful benchmarks at all:

  • Continual/lifelong learning: Current models do not improve from deployment. They are frozen at training time. A human professional improves every day on the job; an LLM on day 1,000 is identical to day 1. This is not a minor gap — it is a fundamental architectural limitation. Every human professional gets better with experience. No AI system does.

  • Long-horizon strategic planning: No benchmark exists for multi-week or multi-month planning with uncertainty. METR’s longest measurements top out at hours. Real-world strategic planning — running a project, managing a research program, building a company — requires planning horizons of months to years with constant replanning.

  • Cross-domain transfer: Few observations suggest transfer is weak. Models strong in math may not transfer that capability to novel scientific domains. Human experts routinely transfer reasoning patterns across domains (a physicist applies variational methods from mechanics to economics). We have little evidence AI can do this.

  • Metacognition: The MIRROR benchmark (~250K evaluation instances, 16 models) finds Compositional Calibration Error ranges 0.500–0.943, essentially random. Models cannot translate self-knowledge into appropriate action selection.

  • Embodied reasoning: Best household robot achieves only 50% task success. Embodied science research finds that text-based reasoning is “inherently insufficient” for physical dynamics, risking “hallucinated discoveries” that fail physical verification.

These unmeasured capabilities represent a large fraction of what separates human cognition from current AI. Including them in any honest BCP assessment pushes timelines substantially later. The fact that they are unmeasured does not mean they are unimportant — it means we are systematically underestimating the remaining distance to BCP.

2. Architectural Limitations: What the Current Paradigm Cannot Do

2.1 The Bounded-Good / Open-Ended-Bad Pattern

The single most important finding across all benchmark data is this structural pattern: AI excels at bounded, well-specified tasks and collapses on open-ended, interactive, long-horizon ones. This is not an accident of current model size or training — it reflects the fundamental mismatch between next-token prediction and the kind of cognition required for open-ended problem-solving.

The METR autonomous task completion data makes this precise: the 80% success time horizon is only 27 minutes, while the 50% horizon is approximately 15 hours — a 10x gap that indicates thin-tailed reliability. This means the models are not gradually degrading; they hit a cliff. Beyond ~35 minutes of autonomous operation, performance falls off rapidly.

Consider what the 50%/80% gap means in practice. A model that can do a task right half the time in 15 hours but can only do it reliably in 27 minutes is analogous to a worker who can occasionally complete a full project but can only be counted on for a half-hour of reliable work. That is not a worker you can deploy for autonomous cognitive labor. The gap between “can sometimes do” and “reliably does” is the gap between a lab demo and a deployable system — and it is enormous.

Why does this pattern exist? Next-token prediction optimizes for local coherence: given recent context, produce a plausible continuation. This works beautifully for bounded tasks where the full problem fits within the context window and the solution follows recognizable patterns. It fails for open-ended tasks where the system must maintain coherent goals across thousands of steps, recognize when it has gone off track, backtrack effectively, and adapt to unexpected intermediate results.

This is worth dwelling on because optimists often treat the bounded/open-ended gap as a quantitative deficit that more scale will close. But the pattern suggests it is qualitative — a different kind of capability is required, not just more of the same capability. The analogy is chess engines vs. general intelligence: making a chess engine twice as strong does not make it 1% closer to general intelligence, because chess-playing and general intelligence are qualitatively different capabilities. Similarly, making an LLM 50% better at AIME does not make it 1% closer to solving ARC-AGI-3, because closed-form math and novel interactive reasoning are qualitatively different capabilities.

The persistence of this pattern across model generations provides strong evidence for its structural nature. If it were a quantitative gap, we would expect it to narrow as models scale. Instead, the bounded/open-ended gap has remained roughly constant (or even widened on some metrics) across GPT-4, Claude 3, Gemini Ultra, GPT-5, and their successors. Performance on bounded tasks improves; performance on open-ended tasks does not.

2.2 Why Leading Researchers Are Betting Against Current Architectures

Yann LeCun, a Turing Award winner and former head of Meta AI, left Meta in late 2025 and founded AMI Labs in Paris, raising $1.03 billion at a $3.5 billion valuation in March 2026 to build “world models.” His core argument: LLMs cannot achieve AGI because they lack physical grounding, causal reasoning, and world models.

This is not just an opinion. This is a revealed preference backed by over a billion dollars. LeCun could have stayed at Meta with a top-tier salary and massive compute budget. Instead, he left to build something different — because he believes the current approach is a dead end. When someone with LeCun’s track record puts $1B behind a thesis, the thesis deserves serious weight.

Ilya Sutskever left OpenAI and founded SSI, now valued at $32 billion, on the thesis that a “new paradigm” is needed. A survey found that 76% of AAAI experts say scaling alone is unlikely to yield AGI.

The revealed-preference argument is important because it avoids the “just an opinion” dismissal. These researchers are not writing blog posts or giving interviews — they are restructuring their careers and raising billions of dollars around the conviction that current architectures are insufficient. Their actions carry information their words alone would not.

Counterargument: Optimists argue these are just opinions and that empirical scaling results matter more. But the departure of these specific researchers — who have the deepest understanding of current architectures — is evidence precisely because they know things the public doesn’t about where the current approach runs into walls. The empirical scaling results support them: GPT-5 showed diminishing returns, and ARC-AGI-3 scores haven’t budged.

2.3 Hallucination as Architectural Feature, Not Bug

Hallucination rates in 2026 remain at 3–18% for best models, with a broader 2026 benchmark across 37 models showing rates of 15–52%. The global cost of AI hallucinations reached $67.4 billion in 2024.

The critical insight is that hallucination is not a solvable bug. It is a structural consequence of how LLMs are trained: they are rewarded for confident, fluent completion of text, not for accurate representation of uncertainty. Standard training rewards guessing over acknowledging ignorance. Models “sound most confident when they’re wrong.” Fixing this within the current paradigm would require fundamentally changing the training objective — at which point you no longer have an LLM in the current sense.

The trajectory of hallucination reduction is informative. Despite enormous investment in RLHF, constitutional AI, retrieval augmentation, and other mitigation techniques, hallucination rates have improved only modestly. The best models in 2024 hallucinated at roughly 5–20% rates; the best models in 2026 hallucinate at 3–18%. This is progress, but it is slow, and the remaining hallucination is in precisely the hardest cases — the long-tail factual claims where the model has weak training signal but maximum confidence.

For autonomous deployment, even a 3% hallucination rate is devastating. A system that confidently asserts false facts 3% of the time cannot be trusted to make autonomous decisions in medicine, law, finance, engineering, or any other high-stakes domain. And these are the domains where BCP would be most economically valuable.

2.4 The Metacognition Void

The MIRROR benchmark demonstrates that models cannot self-monitor. Compositional Calibration Error ranges from 0.500 to 0.943 — essentially random performance on translating self-knowledge into action selection. A separate study found that only 4 of 10 models correctly predict themselves as the generator of text; performance is rarely above random chance.

Why does this matter? Autonomous operation requires knowing when you don’t know. A surgeon who doesn’t recognize they’re out of their depth kills patients. An autonomous AI agent that doesn’t recognize when it’s failing will compound errors indefinitely. External structure reduces Confident Failure Rate by 76% (from 0.600 to 0.143) — meaning the fix is scaffolding, not the model itself. This is a workaround, not a solution.

The metacognition void has a direct causal relationship to the 35-minute degradation cliff observed in METR data. Models cannot detect when their reasoning has gone off track, so errors compound undetected until the output becomes incoherent. Humans, by contrast, have a constant background process of metacognitive monitoring — “does this feel right? am I missing something? should I reconsider?” — that catches errors before they compound.

This is not a capability that emerges naturally from next-token prediction. Predicting the next token well does not require knowing whether your prediction process is reliable in the current context. It requires only producing plausible continuations. The metacognition gap may require explicit architectural support — a separate monitoring system, a different training objective, or a fundamentally different approach to self-evaluation.

3. The Reliability Wall: Error Compounding and Deployment Failure

3.1 The Mathematics of Error Compounding

The math of error compounding is devastating and inescapable:

Per-Step Accuracy 10-Step Workflow 20-Step Workflow 50-Step Workflow
85% 20% 4% 0.3%
95% 60% 36% 8%
99% 90% 82% 61%
99.9% 99% 98% 95%

For real-world autonomous tasks of the kind BCP requires — multi-hour, multi-step workflows with branching decisions — you need per-step accuracy at 99.9%+ to achieve acceptable end-to-end reliability. Current models achieve perhaps 85–95% per-step accuracy on well-specified tasks, yielding 4–60% success on 20-step workflows. The APEX-Agents 2026 benchmark confirms this: best models completed only 24% of real-world tasks on first attempt.

Gartner predicts that 40%+ of agentic AI projects will be scrapped by 2027 because of this reliability gap.

Note that this math is independent of architecture. Error compounding is a mathematical fact, not a property of transformers or LLMs. Any autonomous system faces it. The question is whether a system can achieve the 99.9%+ per-step accuracy required for reliable multi-step work. Current systems are 1–2 orders of magnitude short. Each order of magnitude improvement in per-step accuracy from 95% to 99.5% to 99.95% is harder than the last, because it requires handling increasingly rare and difficult edge cases.

Optimists sometimes counter that humans also compound errors and work around this through metacognition, error detection, and backtracking. This is exactly right — and it highlights why the metacognition gap (Section 2.4) is so critical. Humans are not 99.9% accurate per step either. But humans can detect and correct their errors, effectively resetting the compound error chain. Current AI cannot do this. Until models achieve robust metacognition and error recovery, the compound error math will dominate their autonomous task performance.

3.2 The METR Cliff

The METR data on autonomous task completion shows a characteristic “reliability cliff”:

  • 50% success time horizon: ~15 hours (models succeed at straightforward tasks)
  • 80% success time horizon: 27 minutes (models fail catastrophically at reliable multi-step work)

The 10x gap between these horizons indicates thin-tailed reliability — models are not gradually degrading, they hit a wall. This is exactly what error compounding predicts: once per-step accuracy drops below ~99%, compound errors accumulate rapidly and performance collapses.

For BCP, the 80% horizon is the relevant metric, not the 50% horizon. A system that succeeds only half the time is not a replacement for human cognition — it is a tool that requires constant human oversight. BCP means being reliably capable, not occasionally capable.

The 27-minute 80% horizon needs to extend to something like 8 hours (a full workday) for BCP in professional cognitive work. That is an 18x improvement in reliable autonomous duration. At the rate this metric has been improving (it was approximately 10 minutes two years ago and is now 27 minutes), reaching 8 hours would take roughly 6–10 more years — and that assumes the improvement rate is maintained, which is unlikely given diminishing returns.

3.3 Goal Drift in Long-Horizon Agents

Research on long-horizon agent behavior reveals that most “long-horizon agents” are “just fragile loops that haven’t failed yet.” EPAM’s analysis identifies five distinct categories of goal failure. Key failure modes include:

  • Meltdown looping: agents stuck repeating failed actions, unable to recognize the loop
  • Hallucinated context: agents acting on non-existent facts they generated in prior steps
  • Context window dilution: when planning and execution share the same context window, goals become diluted over time as execution details crowd out strategic context
  • Recovery failure: agents unable to recognize and recover from intermediate errors
  • Scope creep: agents drifting from the original goal as they encounter and respond to tangential information

The 35-minute degradation cliff is not a benchmark artifact. It reflects architectural limitations in how current models handle persistent goals — limitations that stem from the fixed context window and lack of genuine persistent state. A human working on a multi-day project maintains a stable representation of goals, constraints, and progress that persists across interruptions, sleep, and context switches. Current AI architectures have no equivalent mechanism.

3.4 Enterprise Deployment Confirms the Pattern
