If AI capabilities were genuinely approaching human level across domains, we would expect enterprise deployment to succeed at high rates. The opposite is true:

  • RAND (2,400+ initiatives): 80.3% fail to deliver intended value. 33.8% abandoned before production, 28.4% fail on value, 18.1% can’t justify cost.
  • MIT NANDA (July 2025): 95% of organizations deploying GenAI saw zero measurable P&L impact.
  • 42% of companies scrapped at least one AI initiative in 2025 (up from 17% the prior year), with average sunk cost of $7.2M.

These are not cherry-picked failures. These are comprehensive surveys showing that the gap between lab demos and production value creation is enormous and persistent.

The trend in failure rates is not encouraging. The 42% abandonment rate in 2025 was up from 17% in the prior year. Failure rates are increasing as organizations attempt more ambitious deployments, not decreasing as the technology matures. This is consistent with the bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern: simple use cases (chatbots, summarization) worked, but more complex autonomous deployments are failing at accelerating rates.

3.5 The Lab-to-Production Gap Is Structural

The gap between lab demos and production deployment is not merely a matter of engineering polish. It reflects fundamental differences between controlled environments and real-world conditions:

  • Distribution shift: Lab benchmarks use clean, well-formatted inputs. Production data is messy, inconsistent, and full of edge cases that benchmarks don’t capture.
  • Error tolerance: Benchmarks measure average-case accuracy. Production requires worst-case reliability. A model that is right 97% of the time is a research success but a production liability if the 3% errors cause harm.
  • Integration complexity: Deploying AI in production requires integration with existing systems, handling of authentication, data privacy, compliance, monitoring, fallback mechanisms, and human-in-the-loop workflows. Each integration point is a potential failure point.
  • Adversarial users: Lab benchmarks assume good-faith inputs. Production systems face adversarial users, unusual inputs, and conditions the designers never anticipated.

This gap is why the enterprise failure rate is 80–95% even though the underlying models score impressively on benchmarks. The benchmarks measure the easy part (bounded, well-specified performance). Production requires the hard part (robustness, reliability, integration, edge case handling). And the hard part is getting harder as deployments become more ambitious.

4. Infrastructure and Energy Constraints

4.1 Hard Physical Bottlenecks

Even if algorithmic progress continues, compute scaling depends on physical infrastructure that cannot be accelerated past certain limits:

  • Grid interconnection waits: 4–7 years in key markets
  • Full project timeline: Average 7+ years from application to operational status
  • Substation transformer lead times: Now exceed 160 weeks (3+ years)
  • Construction gap: Of ~12 GW targeted for 2026, only 1/3 is actively under construction
  • Energy consumption: Data center energy could reach 1,050 TWh by 2026 — making AI the 5th largest energy consumer globally, between Japan and Russia

A single AI inference task uses up to 1,000x more electricity than a traditional web search. Scaling inference-time compute (the current frontier of capability improvement) directly multiplies energy demand.

4.2 Money Cannot Buy Time

Hyperscalers have committed $650B+ in spending, but money cannot accelerate grid physics. You cannot build a substation faster by paying more; the manufacturing capacity for large power transformers is globally constrained with multi-year lead times. A 7 GW capacity crisis is already unfolding in 2026.

This is a different kind of constraint than algorithmic limitations. Algorithmic breakthroughs can happen unpredictably. But you cannot have an unpredictable breakthrough in building power infrastructure — the physics of transformers, transmission lines, and grid interconnection operates on fixed timelines that no amount of money or genius can compress below certain minima.

The implications for the compute trajectory are significant. If frontier model training requires 10x more compute every 2–3 years, and compute capacity is constrained by power availability that grows at a much slower rate, then effective compute scaling slows to match infrastructure growth. Optimist timelines assume compute continues to grow at historical rates; infrastructure constraints suggest it cannot.

Operationalized claim: If U.S. AI data center capacity in 2028 is less than 20 GW (vs. ~4 GW operational in 2024), this indicates physical infrastructure is binding on the scaling trajectory as predicted.

4.3 The Efficiency Treadmill

Recent research shows that maintaining historical scaling progress now requires repeated doublings of algorithmic efficiency, not just compute. Each doubling gets harder than the last. The compound effect of scaling + algorithmic improvement may be decelerating, not accelerating.

This means even if infrastructure constraints are relaxed, the effective compute growth rate may slow because the algorithmic side faces its own diminishing returns. The field is on a treadmill: to maintain constant progress, it needs accelerating efficiency improvements, each of which is harder than the last.

4.4 The Inference-Time Energy Multiplier

Inference-time compute scaling — the current frontier of capability improvement — compounds the energy problem. If achieving better performance on hard tasks requires 10–100x more inference-time computation (extended “thinking” chains), then deploying these capabilities at scale requires 10–100x more energy per task. This creates a direct conflict between capability improvement and deployability: the methods that produce better results are the ones that consume the most energy and are the hardest to scale.

A future where AI achieves BCP-level capability but each task costs $100 in energy is not a future of widely deployed AGI. It is a future of expensive, carefully rationed AI that supplements rather than replaces human cognition — which is a very different timeline from what optimists envision.

4.5 Geographic and Political Constraints

Infrastructure constraints are not purely technical — they are also geographic and political. Grid expansion faces NIMBY opposition, environmental review processes, permitting delays, and cross-jurisdictional coordination challenges. A data center needs not just power generation but transmission capacity, water for cooling, and political acceptance. Many proposed sites face years of regulatory delay even after the engineering is feasible.

International competition for AI compute adds another layer: export controls on advanced chips, supply chain vulnerabilities (TSMC concentration in Taiwan), and geopolitical tensions all create fragility in the hardware pipeline. A single supply chain disruption — whether from natural disaster, trade policy, or conflict — could delay compute scaling by 1–3 years.

4.6 Quantifying the Infrastructure Impact on Timeline

Taking infrastructure constraints seriously adds approximately 2–5 years to the optimist timeline:

  • If frontier training runs require 10x more compute by 2030, but data center capacity only grows 3–4x by then (due to grid constraints), the compute gap translates directly to delayed model generations.
  • If inference-time scaling is the primary capability improvement mechanism, but energy costs limit deployment scale, the gap between “achievable in a lab” and “deployable at scale” grows.
  • If chip supply is disrupted for even 12–18 months, a full model generation is delayed.

These are not tail risks. They are the most likely outcome given current infrastructure trajectories.

5. Scaling Laws: Diminishing Returns Are Here

5.1 The Empirical Evidence

A study of 400+ models finds that each decade of additional compute buys only 1–2 percentage points of MMLU-Pro improvement. Different capabilities plateau at different scales:

Capability Plateau Scale
Knowledge ~30B parameters
Reasoning ~70B+ parameters
Code ~34B+ parameters
Language understanding ~13B+ parameters

Source: LLM Scaling Laws Explained (2026)

The heterogeneous plateaus are important. They mean that throwing more compute at the problem does not uniformly improve all capabilities. A 10x increase in compute might noticeably improve reasoning but have zero effect on knowledge retrieval (already plateaued) or language understanding (already plateaued). This is diminishing returns at the capability level, not just the benchmark level.

5.2 GPT-5: The Concrete Example

GPT-5 is the most visible evidence of diminishing returns. Trained with substantially more compute than GPT-4, it produced smaller benchmark gains per order of magnitude of compute. OpenAI originally called it GPT-4.5 rather than GPT-5. Sam Altman gave it a “half-hearted introduction,” calling it “giant, expensive.” OpenAI is burning $8 billion per year with no profitability expected until 2029. Its Agent Mode received “mixed reviews.”

If the company at the frontier of AI development is itself lukewarm about its latest model’s gains, this is strong evidence that the scaling curve is bending. OpenAI has every incentive to hype its models — its valuation depends on sustained optimism about scaling. When even OpenAI undersells a model launch, the signal is unambiguous.

The GPT-5 trajectory is particularly important because it invalidates a key optimist assumption: that each generation of frontier models will produce proportional or accelerating capability gains. If the most compute-intensive model ever trained produces only incremental improvements, the scaling trajectory required for BCP by the mid-2030s is not achievable through pre-training scaling alone.

5.3 Data Exhaustion Compounds the Problem

Epoch AI estimates (80% confidence interval) that quality-filtered public text will be exhausted between 2026 and 2032. Synthetic data now comprises 60% of AI training data (up from 1% in 2021, per Gartner), but hits a “quality ceiling” and risks “model collapse” — outputs degrade when training on model-generated data. Human feedback remains the “critical ingredient” for advanced capabilities.

The data exhaustion problem interacts with diminishing compute returns in a compounding way. If each OOM of compute buys less improvement, AND the quality of training data is declining (more synthetic, less human), then the effective capability gain per dollar of investment is falling faster than either factor alone would suggest.

The model collapse risk from synthetic data deserves emphasis. When models are trained on data generated by prior models, errors and biases compound across generations. This has been demonstrated empirically: models trained on synthetic data from prior generations produce increasingly degenerate outputs. This means synthetic data is not a full substitute for human data — it is a supplement that faces its own diminishing returns and a hard quality ceiling.

5.4 The Multi-Axis Scaling Thesis: Weaker Than It Appears

Optimists argue that even if pre-training scaling hits diminishing returns, other scaling axes (inference-time compute, reinforcement learning, tool use, multi-agent systems) can continue to drive progress. This is true in principle but weaker in practice than it appears:

  1. Inference-time scaling has produced impressive results on math and coding (reasoning models), but has not been shown to improve ARC-AGI-3 scores, sustained autonomy, or metacognition. It improves thinking harder on well-specified problems; it does not improve thinking differently about novel problems.

  2. Reinforcement learning from code execution and self-play has improved verifiable domains (math, coding) but is difficult to apply to open-ended domains where the reward signal is ambiguous or delayed. Most real-world cognitive tasks do not have clean reward signals.

  3. Tool use and multi-agent systems are engineering improvements, not capability improvements. They extend what models can do by combining multiple model calls and external tools, but each component still faces the same per-step accuracy and error compounding constraints.

  4. Each new scaling axis provides a one-time paradigm shift, not a continuous source of improvement. Pre-training scaling dominated 2018–2023. Inference-time scaling dominated 2023–2025. Each subsequent axis will provide diminishing one-time boosts.

The multi-axis thesis amounts to claiming that enough one-time improvements, stacked together, will bridge the gap to BCP. This is possible but requires many such improvements in sequence, each solving a different aspect of the problem — which is another way of describing the serial dependency chain that adds years to the timeline.

Counterargument addressed: Optimists argue that inference-time compute scaling and reinforcement learning represent new scaling frontiers. This is partially correct — they do provide one-time paradigm shifts. But each new scaling axis faces its own diminishing returns, and inference-time scaling directly multiplies the energy/infrastructure constraints described above. RL from code execution and self-play have produced impressive results in narrow domains (math, coding) but have not been shown to generalize to the open-ended, interactive reasoning tasks where the largest gaps exist.

5.5 The Capability Plateau Pattern

There is a consistent pattern across AI capabilities: rapid early improvement followed by a long plateau that asymptotically approaches but never reaches human-level performance. This has been observed for:

  • Machine translation: Rapid improvement 2016–2020, followed by slow convergence that has still not reached professional translator quality for nuanced text.
  • Image generation: Rapid improvement 2021–2024, followed by persistent struggles with hands, text, spatial consistency, and compositional instructions.
  • Dialogue systems: Rapid improvement in fluency and knowledge 2022–2024, followed by persistent issues with truthfulness, coherence, and reliability.

The same pattern is now visible in reasoning models: rapid improvement on standard math and coding benchmarks through 2024–2025, followed by plateau-like behavior on harder and more novel tasks. This is consistent with the interpretation that scaling improves the “easy” (high-frequency, well-represented-in-training-data) part of each capability rapidly, but the “hard” (long-tail, novel, compositional) part of each capability resists scaling.

For BCP, the implication is that each remaining capability gap will follow this same trajectory: rapid early progress that generates optimism, followed by a long, grinding plateau phase where the last 20–30% of performance requires disproportionate time and resources.

6. The Ideation-Execution Gap: Why AI Research Automation Is Slow

6.1 Ideas Without Execution Are Worthless

The Stanford SALT Lab has documented a critical asymmetry: AI-generated research ideas score higher on novelty before execution but drop significantly after execution. Human ideas retain their scores through the execution process. The gap is structural: AI ideas lack experimental rigor, reproducibility, and resource awareness.

This directly limits the speed of recursive AI improvement (the optimist’s strongest acceleration mechanism). Even if AI generates impressive-sounding research directions, it cannot execute them into real results. The ideation bottleneck research finds that 70% of organizations struggle to move beyond the pilot phase.

The ideation-execution gap is a microcosm of the bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern. Generating novel research ideas is a bounded task: take a corpus of existing research, identify gaps, propose novel combinations. It fits squarely in the LLM sweet spot. But executing research — designing experiments, building infrastructure, handling unexpected results, iterating through failures, managing resources — is an open-ended, long-horizon task that requires exactly the capabilities LLMs lack.

6.2 Why This Blocks Recursive Improvement

The optimist case for fast BCP relies heavily on AI automating AI research (recursive improvement). But this requires not just ideation but execution — running experiments, debugging infrastructure, interpreting ambiguous results, pivoting when things don’t work. The ideation-execution gap shows that current AI excels at the first step and fails at all subsequent ones.

Historical analogy supports caution: even the transformer architecture (2017) and Chinchilla scaling laws (2022), which were understood immediately upon publication, took years to fully exploit. The bottlenecks of verification, compute provisioning, and engineering deployment are serial and irreducible. You cannot parallelize your way past a sequential dependency chain.

The recursive improvement thesis also faces a logical problem: AI must reach a certain threshold of capability before it can meaningfully accelerate AI research. But the threshold is high — you need reliable multi-day autonomous execution, strong metacognition, and cross-domain transfer. These are precisely the capabilities furthest from current performance. The recursive improvement loop cannot kick in until the hardest capabilities are partially solved, and those capabilities are the ones that will take the longest.

This creates a catch-22 for optimist timelines that depend on recursive improvement: the capabilities needed to start the loop (sustained autonomy, research execution) are themselves among the last capabilities to be achieved. If recursive improvement is the primary mechanism for reaching BCP quickly, and recursive improvement requires most of the BCP capabilities as prerequisites, then it cannot substantially accelerate the earlier, harder portion of the journey — which is the portion that determines the timeline.

Put differently: recursive improvement might compress the last 3–5 years of the journey to BCP into 1–2 years. But it cannot compress the first 10–15 years, because it doesn’t exist yet during that period. The optimist who claims BCP by 2035 is implicitly claiming that most of the hard work is already done and recursive improvement just needs to finish the job. The evidence in this document says otherwise — most of the hard work (novel reasoning, sustained autonomy, metacognition, physical reasoning) has barely begun.

6.3 The Verification Bottleneck

Even if AI generates good research ideas and can partially execute them, verifying results remains a bottleneck. Peer review, experimental reproducibility, and scientific verification are slow, human-intensive processes that resist automation. Verifying that a new training technique actually works (and doesn’t just appear to work on selected benchmarks) requires months of careful testing across diverse settings.

This is a serial bottleneck that limits the speed of any research automation loop, regardless of how fast the ideation and initial execution become. The speed of science is limited not by how fast hypotheses are generated but by how carefully they can be tested and validated.

The verification bottleneck is particularly severe for AI research specifically because the field has a replication crisis. Many published results don’t reproduce under slightly different conditions. An AI system that generates plausible-looking results without robust verification would exacerbate this crisis, not accelerate genuine progress. Automation of bad science is worse than no automation.

6.4 Historical Speed of Paradigm Exploitation

Even when genuinely paradigm-shifting ideas emerge, exploiting them takes time:

  • Transformers (2017): Despite being immediately recognized as significant, it took 3–4 years before GPT-3 demonstrated the full potential of the architecture, and another 2–3 years before the current generation of models. Total: ~6–7 years from invention to mature exploitation.
  • Deep learning (2012, ImageNet breakthrough): Practical deployment across diverse domains took 5–8 years. Many companies were still adopting deep learning in 2020.
  • Backpropagation (1986): Known since the 1960s, first effectively used in 1986, mainstream by late 1990s. Total: ~10–15 years from theoretical understanding to practical exploitation.

If a new architecture is invented tomorrow that solves the bounded/open-ended gap, the historical base rate suggests 5–10 years before it is fully exploited and deployed at scale. This is not pessimism about the architecture; it is realism about the speed of engineering, optimization, scaling, and deployment.

Operationalized claim: If by 2028, AI-authored papers at top-3 ML venues (NeurIPS, ICML, ICLR) remain below 5% of accepted papers (as primary intellectual contributor, not just tool-assisted), this supports the pessimist view that AI research automation is slower than optimists project.

7. Historical Precedent: Technology Plateaus and Amara’s Law

7.1 The 70-Year Track Record

Melanie Mitchell’s analysis documents the recurring pattern:

  • Herbert Simon (1960): Machines doing any human work within 20 years
  • Claude Shannon (1961): Robots within 10–15 years
  • U.S. Navy (1958): Perceptrons that “walk, talk, see, write, reproduce, and be conscious”
  • Marvin Minsky (1970): Machine with human intelligence within 3–8 years

Every generation of AI researchers has predicted AGI within 20–30 years. Every generation has been wrong. The base rate of overoptimistic AI predictions is effectively 100% across 70 years.

Mitchell identifies four fallacies that drive recurring overoptimism: 1. Assuming narrow intelligence lies on a continuum with general intelligence 2. Assuming “easy things are easy” (Moravec’s paradox) 3. The disembodiment assumption — intelligence without physical grounding 4. Assuming intelligence can exist without humanlike qualities (motivation, curiosity, embodied understanding)

All four fallacies are prominently visible in current optimist arguments. Today’s optimists assume that because LLMs can pass the bar exam (#1), the remaining capabilities are achievable with more scale (#2), that physical grounding is unnecessary for general intelligence (#3), and that human-like cognition can emerge from text prediction alone (#4). These are the same fallacies that have led to overoptimistic predictions in every prior AI generation.

The value of this historical pattern is not that it proves current AI will fail — it is that it should calibrate our confidence. If AI researchers have a 100% base rate of overoptimistic predictions, and current researchers are predicting BCP by the mid-2030s, then a reasonable Bayesian update is to add 50–100% to that estimate. This doesn’t mean the specific arguments for or against are irrelevant; it means the prior should be skeptical, and specific evidence should have to overcome that prior. As shown throughout this document, the specific evidence overwhelmingly supports skepticism rather than overcoming it.

The pattern is particularly informative because it is not random. Mitchell’s four fallacies explain why the pattern recurs: each generation makes the same structural reasoning errors because the errors are intuitive and the corrections are counterintuitive. The fact that today’s optimists exhibit all four fallacies does not prove they are wrong, but it should raise the evidentiary bar for their claims considerably.

7.2 Self-Driving Cars: The Closest Analogy

Self-driving cars are the closest technology analogy to AGI. The prediction record is instructive:

Who Prediction Actual
Musk Self-driving by 2016 Not achieved
GM Mass-produce driverless by 2019 Not achieved
Ford Driverless by 2021 Not achieved
Lyft Most rides autonomous by 2021 Not achieved
Business Insider 10M self-driving cars by 2020 Not achieved

Ford’s CEO admitted: “We overestimated the arrival.” Current status: L4 robotaxis available in a few cities; full rollout now expected 2030–2035 per GlobalData.

The pattern is identical to what we see in AI: rapid early progress on demo-level capability, followed by years of grinding work on edge cases and reliability. The “last 10%” took longer than the “first 90%.” Self-driving went from “one year away” to “ten years away” in the span of five years.

This is the bounded-good / open-ended-bad pattern in action. Self-driving works great on highways in good weather (bounded). It fails in construction zones, snow, unusual pedestrian behavior (open-ended). AGI benchmarks look great on closed-form math (bounded). They collapse on novel interactive environments (open-ended).

The self-driving analogy is particularly apt because: (a) it involves a real, deployable AI system in the physical world, not just benchmarks; (b) the prediction errors were systematic — every major player overestimated; (c) the technology was real — cars genuinely drove themselves in many conditions; (d) the remaining gap was dominated by edge cases and reliability, not core capability; and (e) the delay from “demos work” to “full deployment” was approximately 15 years.

If the same delay pattern applies to AGI — and the structural similarities are strong — then current demo-level capabilities (2024–2026) would reach full deployment-grade BCP around 2039–2041.

7.3 Amara’s Law and the Hype Cycle

“We tend to overestimate the effect of a technology in the short run and underestimate it in the long run.”

GenAI has entered the Trough of Disillusionment in 2026 per Gartner. Agentic AI, placed at Peak of Inflated Expectations in July 2025, is transitioning to the trough “faster than expected.” AI Engineering is soon to enter its own trough as DataOps/MLOps/DevOps unification proves harder and costlier than projected.

The trough is not the end. Technologies do emerge from it. But the trough typically lasts 2–5 years and represents a period of reduced investment and slower progress — exactly the mechanism that pushes BCP timelines later. The Gartner pattern has been remarkably consistent across technology generations: mainframes, PCs, the internet, mobile, cloud, social media, and now AI all followed the same trajectory.

7.4 The “This Time Is Different” Claim

Every technology hype cycle features the claim “this time is different because we have real capability.” The dot-com era had real capability — the internet genuinely transformed commerce and communication. Blockchain had real capability — distributed ledgers genuinely solve certain trust problems. Both still went through multi-year troughs of disillusionment before reaching productive maturity.

The distinguishing feature of “this time is different” claims is not whether the technology is real (it usually is) but whether the short-term trajectory is accurately estimated (it usually isn’t). AI is real. The question is whether the path from current capability to BCP is 10 years (optimist) or 20 years (pessimist).

7.5 The “Faster Than Expected” Fallacy

Optimists often cite that AI progress has been “faster than expected” compared to predictions from 5–10 years ago. This is true for specific benchmarks (MMLU, AIME, coding), but it conflates two different things: (a) progress on the capabilities LLMs are good at, and (b) progress toward BCP. The former has been fast; the latter has not.

A useful analogy: in the 1950s, progress on computer chess was “faster than expected” — programs went from barely knowing the rules to beating club players faster than many predicted. But progress from club player to world champion took another 40 years (1957 to 1997). The early fast progress was on the part of the problem that scale naturally solves; the remaining progress required fundamentally different approaches (deeper search, better evaluation, eventually Monte Carlo methods).

The “faster than expected” progress in AI has been on the equivalent of early chess — the bounded, well-specified capabilities that scale naturally with data and compute. The remaining distance to BCP is the equivalent of club-player-to-world-champion — it requires fundamentally different approaches, not just more of the same.

7.6 Why Prior AI Winters Were Different (And Why This Still Matters)

Optimists argue that prior AI winters were caused by theoretical dead ends (perceptrons couldn’t learn XOR, expert systems couldn’t scale), whereas current AI is fundamentally more capable and the current approach has no equivalent theoretical dead end. This is partially correct — current AI is genuinely more capable than any prior generation, and there is no known theoretical impossibility result for transformers.

But prior AI winters were not caused by impossibility results being discovered. They were caused by the rate of progress slowing below investor expectations. The perceptron wasn’t proven impossible in 1969; Minsky and Papert showed that single-layer perceptrons couldn’t learn XOR, but multi-layer networks could (and were known to exist). The AI winter happened anyway because progress slowed and investment dried up.

The same dynamic could happen now. The technology is real and capable, but if progress toward BCP slows (as the evidence in this document suggests), investment may contract regardless. The trigger for an AI winter is not “AI doesn’t work” — it is “AI doesn’t work fast enough to justify the investment.“

8. Economic Reality: The Bubble Risk

8.1 The ROI Gap

The investment-to-return gap in AI is historically unprecedented:

  • Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon: expects “a lot of capital that was deployed that doesn’t deliver returns”
  • Goldman Sachs Global Institute: projects $7.6 trillion cumulative AI capex 2026–2031
  • MIT study: 95% of 52 organizations achieved zero ROI from GenAI, spending $30–40B across 300+ initiatives
  • Goldman Sachs: $700B in 2025 AI investment produced “essentially zero” GDP growth
  • BlackRock CEO: AI investment race “will produce bankruptcies”
8.2 Historical Tech Bubble Pattern

Even when the underlying technology is real, investment bubbles follow a predictable pattern: 1. Genuine technical breakthrough creates excitement 2. Massive capital deployment based on extrapolated potential 3. ROI fails to materialize on projected timeline 4. Capital retraction, consolidation, bankruptcies 5. Slower, steadier progress resumes post-correction

The dot-com bubble didn’t mean the internet was fake. It meant that the path from technical capability to economic value was longer and harder than investors assumed. The same dynamic applies to AI: the technology is real, but the gap between lab capability and production value is consistently underestimated.

The magnitude of the current AI investment bubble may actually exceed the dot-com bubble in real terms. Goldman Sachs projects $7.6 trillion in cumulative AI capex through 2031. If 80–95% of AI projects continue to fail, trillions of dollars will be written off. The correction, when it comes, will be proportional to the overinvestment.

There is an important asymmetry in how bubbles affect timelines: the inflation phase (over-investment) accelerates short-term progress, but the deflation phase (correction) slows progress more than the inflation phase accelerated it. This is because the correction reduces not just investment but confidence, making it harder to attract talent, harder to justify long-term research programs, and harder to maintain the organizational infrastructure that sustained labs depend on. The net effect of a boom-bust cycle is slower progress than steady, sustainable investment would have produced.

8.3 Investment Contraction Slows Compute Trajectory

If the ROI gap triggers significant capex moderation (Scenario D in the synthesis), compute growth slows. This doesn’t kill progress — it delays it. A 30–50% reduction in annual capex growth for 3–5 years would shift the effective compute trajectory by roughly 2–4 years, compounding with other delays.

The mechanism is straightforward: if hyperscalers reduce their AI compute buildout, fewer frontier training runs happen, fewer researchers are hired, and the rate of progress slows. This is not speculative — it happened after the dot-com bust (reduced investment in internet infrastructure) and after the crypto winter (reduced investment in blockchain infrastructure). The technology continued to advance, but at a slower rate.

8.4 The Talent Bottleneck

Investment is not the only economic constraint. The pool of researchers capable of pushing the frontier of AI is limited. There are perhaps 1,000–3,000 people worldwide with the combination of deep theoretical understanding, engineering skill, and institutional access required to advance frontier AI. These researchers cannot be manufactured on demand, even with unlimited funding. Training a world-class AI researcher takes 5–10 years. Doubling the research workforce in 3 years is possible but not 10x-ing it.

This means that even sustained high investment faces diminishing returns on the human capital side. Each additional dollar of compute investment requires proportional research talent to use effectively. If talent growth is linear while compute growth is exponential, the binding constraint shifts from compute to researchers — slowing effective progress.

8.5 The Consolidation Risk

If investment contraction triggers consolidation — fewer, larger companies with less competition — the pace of innovation may slow further. History shows that monopolistic or oligopolistic market structures tend to produce slower innovation than competitive ones. If the AI industry consolidates to 2–3 frontier labs (which current dynamics suggest), the diversity of approaches decreases, the willingness to take architectural risks decreases, and the pace of paradigm-shifting innovation slows.

Operationalized claim: If total hyperscaler AI capex in 2027 is flat or declining compared to 2026 (inflation-adjusted), this is strong evidence of investment contraction consistent with the pessimist timeline.

9. The Narrow-to-Broad Gap: Why ~10 Years Between Narrow and Broad Parity

9.1 What “Broad Cognitive Parity” Actually Requires

BCP is not “AI passes a coding interview” or “AI scores well on MMLU.” It is AI matching human performance across all cognitive domains, including:

  • Novel situation reasoning (ARC-AGI-3: 0.37% vs 100%)
  • Sustained autonomous task execution (27-minute reliable horizon vs. humans’ hours/days/weeks)
  • Metacognition and self-monitoring (near-random performance)
  • Continual learning from experience (zero capability)
  • Physical and embodied reasoning (50% household robot success)
  • Long-form creative coherence (degrades beyond ~80k words)
  • Higher-order theory of mind (collapses at 4th+ order)
  • Cross-domain transfer (weak, poorly measured)
  • Research execution, not just ideation (ideas degrade post-execution)

The word “broad” does real work in “broad cognitive parity.” It means not just excelling at the top-3 benchmarks but matching humans across the full distribution of cognitive tasks, including the ones we haven’t thought to benchmark yet. History suggests that the unbenchmarked tasks are often the hardest (because we don’t know how to formalize them).

9.2 Why Each Gap Takes Years

Each of these gaps represents a different kind of challenge:

Novel reasoning (ARC-AGI-3) requires architectural innovation — current approaches have scored <1% through multiple model generations. This isn’t a gap that closes with scale; it requires a fundamentally different approach to program synthesis or in-context learning.

Sustained autonomy requires solving error compounding, which in turn requires either near-perfect per-step accuracy (99.9%+) or robust error detection and recovery. Neither exists in current systems. The metacognition void means models can’t even detect their own errors.

Continual learning is completely absent from current architectures. Models are frozen at training time. Adding it requires fundamental changes to how models store and update knowledge — potentially requiring new architectures entirely.

Physical reasoning requires either embodiment (with all the hardware challenges that implies) or much better simulation/world models. LeCun’s $1B bet on world models suggests the field’s best minds think this requires years of focused research. Current research finds that text-based reasoning is “inherently insufficient” for physical dynamics.

Research execution requires the full stack: ideation, experimental design, debugging, interpretation, and iteration. The ideation-execution gap shows current AI handles only the first step well.

9.3 Serial Dependencies

Critically, many of these gaps have serial dependencies. You can’t solve sustained autonomy without metacognition. You can’t achieve research execution without sustained autonomy. You can’t achieve recursive improvement without research execution. The gaps don’t close in parallel; they form a dependency chain where each step must substantially precede the next.

The dependency chain looks approximately like:

  1. Metacognition (knowing when you don’t know) → prerequisite for…
  2. Sustained autonomy (reliable multi-hour operation) → prerequisite for…
  3. Research execution (carrying out research, not just ideation) → prerequisite for…
  4. Recursive improvement (AI meaningfully accelerating AI research) → accelerator for…
  5. BCP across remaining domains

Each link in this chain represents years of research and development. Even under optimistic assumptions about progress on each individual link, the serial chain adds 8–15 years to the timeline — which is the core reason the pessimist estimate centers on 2045 rather than 2036.

9.4 Moravec’s Paradox Persists

Analysis of Moravec’s paradox in 2026 confirms it still holds. AI beats world champions at chess and Go but cannot reliably fold laundry. BCG’s assessment: perception has improved dramatically, but dexterous manipulation and physical cause-and-effect reasoning remain “far more difficult to scale.” Robotics adds physical hardware complexity and cost that software-only AI avoids.

For definitions of BCP that include physical-world capabilities, this adds a significant additional timeline. The Escher-Bench gap (57.3% vs 90.6%) shows progress on spatial/physical reasoning is much slower than on math/coding, despite using the same underlying models. The analog clock problem (50.1% for IMO-gold-level models) is an embarrassing specific example.

Even if we restrict BCP to purely cognitive (non-physical) tasks, Moravec’s paradox in its modern form still applies: the tasks that seem “easy” to humans (common sense reasoning, adapting to novel situations, recognizing when something is wrong) are the hardest for AI. The tasks that seem “hard” to humans (advanced math, chess, coding) are the easiest for AI. BCP requires solving the “easy” tasks, which are the ones that resist scaling.

9.5 The Embodied Science Gap

Research on embodied scientific discovery finds that most AI discovery approaches frame science as isolated predictions rather than continuous physical-world interaction. Current AI “lacks the cognitive architecture necessary for scientific thinking.” Grounding studies confirm that text-based reasoning is “inherently insufficient” for physical dynamics, risking “hallucinated discoveries” that fail physical verification.

For AI to match humans in scientific domains — one of the highest-value applications of BCP — it must close not just the text-reasoning gap but the embodied reasoning gap. This requires either:

  1. Physical robots that can interact with lab equipment (current best: 50% task success for household robots; laboratory robotics is much harder)
  2. Simulation environments accurate enough to substitute for physical experiment (current: far from sufficient for novel chemistry, biology, or materials science)
  3. Fundamentally new cognitive architectures that can reason about physical causality from text alone (LeCun’s world models thesis; estimated years away)

Each of these paths adds 5–10 years to the scientific-domain component of BCP.

9.6 The Long-Form Creative Gap

No AI system can produce a publishable novel. Creative coherence degrades beyond approximately 80,000 words. This is not merely an aesthetic concern — it reflects the inability to maintain a stable, evolving internal model of a complex domain over extended output sequences.

A 100,000-word novel requires consistent character psychology, plot causality, thematic development, and stylistic coherence across hundreds of scenes. This is a long-horizon, open-ended task that current architectures fundamentally struggle with. It is also, importantly, a task where human cognition operates effortlessly — most professional writers do not need scaffolding or external memory systems to maintain novel-length coherence.

The creative gap matters for BCP because it reveals a broader limitation: the inability to maintain coherent internal models across long time horizons. This same limitation affects autonomous task completion, strategic planning, and any task that requires sustained attention to an evolving complex state.

9.7 The Integration Problem

Even if each capability gap is individually closed, integrating all capabilities into a single coherent system presents its own challenges. BCP requires not just independent mastery of each cognitive domain but the ability to fluidly switch between domains, apply insights from one to another, and maintain a unified world model that spans all of them.

Current AI development proceeds domain by domain: one team works on math reasoning, another on code generation, another on vision, another on robotics. The assumption is that these capabilities can be composed into a unified system. But composition is not trivial — it introduces new failure modes, conflicts between domain-specific optimizations, and integration complexity that grows combinatorially with the number of capabilities.

Human cognition solves the integration problem by running on a single, general-purpose architecture (the brain) that handles all domains in a unified way. AI does not have this — it has specialized modules, each trained on different data with different objectives, stitched together with engineering glue. Moving from this modular approach to genuine integrated cognition may require architectural breakthroughs we have not yet conceived.

9.8 The Deployment Gap Beyond Lab Performance

There is a systematic gap between achieving a capability in a controlled lab setting and deploying it reliably in the real world. This gap exists for every technology, but it is particularly large for AI because:

  1. Real-world distributions shift continuously. A model trained on 2024 data may not handle 2027 situations correctly. Humans adapt continuously; current AI does not.
  2. Production environments have adversarial inputs. Users will deliberately or accidentally provide inputs the system was never designed to handle.
  3. Reliability requirements scale non-linearly. Going from 95% to 99% reliability requires addressing a long tail of rare failure modes, each of which may require its own solution.
  4. Safety and liability requirements. For high-stakes applications (medicine, law, engineering), the required safety margins add years to deployment timelines through necessary testing, validation, and regulatory approval.

The lab-to-deployment gap historically adds 3–7 years for complex technologies. Self-driving cars achieved “lab-level” capability by 2016 but are still not fully deployed in 2026. Pharmaceutical development routinely takes 10–15 years from discovery to deployment. AI deployment will face similar gaps for its most valuable (highest-stakes) applications.

10. Addressing the Strongest Optimist Arguments

10.1 “But progress has been exponential!”

Progress on bounded, well-specified benchmarks has been rapid. Progress on open-ended, interactive, long-horizon tasks has been slow. The optimist argument cherry-picks the former and ignores the latter. When you look at the full picture — including ARC-AGI-3, METR time horizons, reasoning robustness, metacognition, and enterprise deployment success rates — the rate of progress toward BCP is much slower than the rate of progress on headline benchmarks.

Moreover, the GPT-5 data point shows that even bounded benchmark progress is decelerating relative to compute investment.

10.2 “But inference-time compute scaling is a new paradigm!”

It is, and it has produced real gains. But it is a one-time paradigm shift, not a continuous source of acceleration. Inference-time scaling faces its own diminishing returns (each additional token of “thinking” yields less marginal improvement) and directly multiplies energy costs. It also does not address the fundamental gaps: ARC-AGI-3 scores remain <1% even with reasoning models that use extensive inference-time computation.

10.3 “But AI is already automating AI research!”

Partially true for narrow subtasks (code writing, literature search, hypothesis generation). But the ideation-execution gap shows that AI ideas degrade post-execution while human ideas don’t. And Gary Marcus noted that the biggest recent advance (Claude Code) works precisely because it is NOT a pure LLM — it’s a hybrid system with extensive scaffolding. This supports the “architectural innovation needed” thesis, not the “current approach scales to AGI” thesis.

10.4 “But this time is different because we have real capability, not just hype!”

This is exactly what was said about self-driving cars in 2015. They had real capability — cars were driving themselves on highways. The capability was genuine. The error was in extrapolating from demo-level capability to deployment-level reliability. The “last 10%” — edge cases, reliability, safety — took longer than the “first 90%.”

The same logic applies to AI: current capabilities are real, but the gap between current performance and BCP is dominated by the hardest problems (open-ended reasoning, sustained autonomy, reliability), which historically take longer than the easier problems that produce impressive demos.

10.5 “But the economic incentives are unprecedented!”

True, and this cuts both ways. Unprecedented incentives have produced unprecedented spending ($650B+ from hyperscalers). But that spending faces physical infrastructure constraints (7-year grid timelines), diminishing algorithmic returns, and an ROI gap that threatens sustainability. The largest incentives in history are also producing the largest potential bubble in history.

10.6 “But open-source models are catching up to frontier!”

Open-source models closing the gap to frontier models is real but actually supports the pessimist case. It means the frontier is slowing — open-source catches up because frontier progress is decelerating, not because open-source is accelerating unprecedentedly. The narrowing gap between frontier and open-source is evidence of diminishing returns to scale, not evidence of acceleration toward BCP.

Moreover, open-source model convergence on a capability ceiling is precisely what the diminishing returns thesis predicts: as low-hanging fruit is exhausted, the marginal value of more compute and data decreases, and smaller models with less compute can approach the performance of larger ones. This is a sign of plateau, not progress.

10.7 “But you’re just applying a base rate — the specific evidence matters more!”

The base rate is not the entire argument; it is one of ten interlocking arguments. The specific evidence — ARC-AGI-3 at 0.37%, METR 80% horizon at 27 minutes, GPT-5 diminishing returns, 80–95% enterprise failure rates, 4–7 year infrastructure timelines — is not generic skepticism. It is concrete, quantitative evidence that the specific path from here to BCP is longer than optimists assume.

The base rate matters because it shifts the burden of proof. Given 70 years of overoptimism, the default assumption should be that current predictions are also overoptimistic, and optimists should provide specific, quantitative evidence for why this time is different. The evidence they provide (benchmark saturation on bounded tasks) is precisely the kind of evidence that has been misleading in every prior cycle.

10.8 “But regulatory barriers will be overcome!”

This argument assumes that regulation is the main obstacle. In fact, the pessimist case does not rely heavily on regulatory barriers (Scenario E receives only 5% weight in the synthesis). The core arguments — diminishing scaling returns, error compounding, the bounded/open-ended gap, infrastructure constraints — operate independently of regulation. Even in a completely deregulated environment, the physics of power grids, the math of error compounding, and the architectural limitations of transformers would still apply.

That said, regulation is likely to add some delay. As AI systems are deployed in higher-stakes domains (healthcare, finance, legal, transportation), regulatory frameworks will be required. These take years to develop, as the experience with self-driving car regulation demonstrates. But this is a secondary factor, not the primary driver of the pessimist timeline.

10.9 “But multimodal models are closing the embodiment gap!”

Multimodal models (vision, audio, video) represent genuine progress in grounding language in perception. But perception is not embodiment. A model that can describe a video of someone folding laundry is very different from a model that can fold laundry. Perception is a solved problem for many purposes (image classification accuracy exceeds human level). Manipulation, planning in physical space, and real-time adaptation to physical dynamics remain far from solved.

The gap between perceiving the physical world and acting in it is Moravec’s paradox in its purest form. Multimodal models extend perception; they do not extend action. For the physical-world components of BCP, multimodality is necessary but far from sufficient.

11. Probability Distribution and Confidence Intervals

11.1 Detailed Timeline Distribution
Timeline Probability Scenario
Before 2030 2% Requires near-miraculous architectural breakthrough + infrastructure acceleration
2030–2035 13% Requires current approach to be more right than evidence suggests + no investment contraction
2035–2040 25% Requires significant architectural innovation happening ~now, plus sustained investment
2040–2050 35% Base case: architectural innovations needed, developed over ~5-10 years, then deployed at scale
2050–2060 15% Investment contraction + regulatory delays + architecture proves harder than expected
After 2060 10% Multiple simultaneous headwinds; possible AI winter 2.0

Median: ~2045 | 25th percentile: ~2038 | 75th percentile: ~2053

11.2 Key Uncertainty Drivers

The uncertainty range is wide (2038–2053 for 25th–75th percentile) because three factors are genuinely hard to predict:

  1. Architectural innovation timing: If a breakthrough equivalent to the transformer happens in 2027, timelines compress by 5–8 years. If it takes until 2035, they extend. The pessimist assigns meaningful probability (13%) to faster timelines because genuine architectural breakthroughs are unpredictable and have happened before.

  2. Investment trajectory: Sustained investment at current levels keeps compute growing; a significant correction delays by 3–5 years. The 80–95% enterprise failure rate makes correction likely but the timing is uncertain.

  3. Recursive improvement speed: If AI begins meaningfully automating AI research by 2030, it compresses the back half of the timeline. If the ideation-execution gap persists, it doesn’t. This is the factor with the most potential to move the timeline earlier, but also the one with the strongest evidence against near-term realization.

11.3 Why ~2045 and Not the Synthesis Median of ~2036

The synthesis assigns only 40% weight to slow scenarios (C, D, E) versus 60% to moderate-fast scenarios (A, B). The pessimist case argues this weighting is wrong for several reasons:

  1. The bounded/open-ended gap is underweighted. The synthesis acknowledges this as “the most important structural finding” but doesn’t sufficiently penalize the BCP timeline for it. A 270x gap on novel reasoning (ARC-AGI-3) and a 10x gap between 50th and 80th percentile time horizons suggest current architectures are fundamentally insufficient, not merely immature.

  2. Diminishing returns are underweighted. The GPT-5 data point, the 400+ model scaling study, and data exhaustion projections all point to pre-training scaling losing steam. The synthesis treats inference-time scaling as a new growth axis, but it faces its own diminishing returns and energy constraints. Critically, no scaling axis has been shown to improve ARC-AGI-3-type performance, which is the most important remaining gap.

  3. Investment sustainability is underweighted. An 80–95% enterprise failure rate, zero GDP growth from $700B in investment, and Goldman Sachs/BlackRock warnings constitute strong evidence that Scenario D (investment contraction) deserves more than 10% weight. A realistic weight might be 20–25%. The dot-com precedent suggests that even real technologies experience 2–4 year investment contractions when ROI disappoints.

  4. Historical base rates are underweighted. A 100% rate of overoptimistic predictions over 70 years should substantially shift the prior. Optimists bear the burden of proof for “this time is different.” The base rate alone would suggest adding 50–100% to optimist timeline estimates.

  5. Serial dependencies are underweighted. The gaps in novel reasoning, metacognition, sustained autonomy, continual learning, and physical reasoning form a dependency chain. Closing them is not embarrassingly parallel. Each step requires the prior step to be substantially solved.

11.4 What Would Change the Pessimist’s Mind

The pessimist estimate is not fixed. It would shift significantly earlier if:

  • ARC-AGI-3 scores exceed 30% for frontier models by 2028 (would suggest current architectures are more capable than believed)
  • METR 80% time horizon exceeds 8 hours by 2028 (would suggest error compounding is being solved)
  • A genuinely new architecture achieves strong results across both bounded and open-ended tasks by 2028
  • Enterprise AI success rates exceed 50% in large-scale surveys by 2028
  • AI-authored papers exceed 10% of top ML venue acceptances by 2028

The pessimist is not claiming certainty. The 25th percentile is ~2038 — only 2 years later than the synthesis median. There is genuine probability mass on faster timelines. But the weight of evidence favors the later timelines.

12. Falsifiable Predictions (2026–2028)

These predictions operationalize the pessimist case. If most are falsified, the pessimist should update substantially toward shorter timelines.

Prediction 1: ARC-AGI-3 Stays Below 10%

Claim: No frontier model will score above 10% on ARC-AGI-3 (or any successor benchmark measuring novel interactive reasoning) by December 2028. Metric: Best published score on ARC-AGI-3 or equivalent. Why it matters: If current approaches can’t crack novel reasoning, BCP requires architectural innovation, which adds years. Falsification threshold: >10% score by a production model (not a specialized ARC-only system). Confidence: 80%

Prediction 2: METR 80% Time Horizon Stays Under 2 Hours

Claim: The METR 80% success time horizon for frontier models will remain under 2 hours through December 2028. Metric: METR’s published autonomous task completion metrics (or equivalent industry-standard reliability benchmark). Why it matters: BCP requires sustained autonomous task completion measured in days, not minutes. If the 80% horizon doesn’t break 2 hours by 2028, the 2036 median is very unlikely. Falsification threshold: 80% time horizon exceeding 4 hours. Confidence: 75%

Prediction 3: Enterprise AI Failure Rates Stay Above 50%

Claim: Comprehensive surveys of enterprise AI deployment will continue to show >50% failure rates through 2028. Metric: RAND, Gartner, or equivalent large-scale survey of enterprise AI project outcomes. Why it matters: If AI can’t reliably create business value, the investment thesis weakens and the compute trajectory slows. Falsification threshold: <30% failure rate in a reputable large-scale survey. Confidence: 85%

Prediction 4: Hyperscaler AI Capex Growth Decelerates

Claim: Combined hyperscaler AI capex growth (year-over-year, inflation-adjusted) will be lower in 2027 vs. 2026 than in 2026 vs. 2025. Metric: Published capital expenditure from Microsoft, Google, Amazon, Meta (AI-attributed portion). Why it matters: Decelerating capex growth directly constrains the compute trajectory that optimist timelines depend on. Falsification threshold: Capex growth accelerating (higher year-over-year growth rate in 2027 vs. 2026). Confidence: 65%

Prediction 5: Hallucination Rates Remain Above 2%

Claim: Best-in-class frontier models will still hallucinate at >2% rate on comprehensive factual accuracy benchmarks through 2028. Metric: Published hallucination rates on standardized benchmarks (e.g., the 37-model benchmark series). Why it matters: Sub-1% hallucination is likely required for reliable autonomous operation. If rates stay above 2%, the reliability wall persists. Falsification threshold: <1% hallucination rate on a comprehensive benchmark for any frontier model. Confidence: 90%

Summary of Predictions
Prediction Confidence Falsification Signal
ARC-AGI-3 < 10% 80% >10% by production model
METR 80% < 2 hours 75% >4 hours
Enterprise failure > 50% 85% <30% in major survey
Capex growth deceleration 65% Growth rate accelerates
Hallucination > 2% 90% <1% on comprehensive benchmark

If 3 or more of these predictions are falsified by December 2028, the pessimist should shift their median estimate approximately 5 years earlier (to ~2040). If all 5 are falsified, the pessimist should substantially converge toward the optimist timeline.

13. Conclusion

The pessimist case does not claim that AI progress will stop. It claims that the rate of progress toward broad cognitive parity is slower than optimists project, for reasons that are structural (bounded/open-ended gap, error compounding, architectural limitations), physical (energy, infrastructure), economic (ROI gap, bubble risk), and empirical (diminishing scaling returns, persistent hallucination, metacognition failure).

The strongest single argument is the bounded-good / open-ended-bad pattern: AI dominates closed-form benchmarks while collapsing on open-ended tasks, and this pattern has persisted through multiple generations of models and orders-of-magnitude increases in compute. The self-driving car analogy — where the “last 10%” took longer than the “first 90%” — is the most instructive historical parallel.

BCP is likely to arrive in the 2040–2055 range, with a median of approximately 2045. The path there will require architectural innovations that do not yet exist, sustained investment through a likely correction period, physical infrastructure that takes years to build, and solutions to reliability problems that are structurally inherent in current approaches.

The optimist who predicts BCP by the mid-2030s is making a bet that current approaches will overcome the bounded/open-ended gap through scaling alone, that investment will sustain through an 80–95% failure rate, that infrastructure will be built faster than the historical 7-year timeline, and that the 70-year base rate of overoptimistic predictions will finally be broken. Each of these bets is individually uncertain; their conjunction is unlikely.

The pessimist is not a Luddite or a denier. The pessimist believes AI is one of the most important technologies in human history and that BCP will eventually be achieved. The disagreement is about when — and specifically, whether the remaining distance is 10 years (optimist) or 20 years (pessimist). The evidence, carefully weighed, favors the longer timeline.

What the Pessimist Expects to Happen

The pessimist envisions the following trajectory:

  • 2026–2030: Continued incremental progress on bounded tasks. Trough of disillusionment for enterprise AI. Investment moderation but not collapse. One or two significant architectural innovations published but not yet mature. METR 80% horizon reaches ~1–2 hours.
  • 2030–2035: New architectures begin to mature. Partial solutions to metacognition and error recovery. METR 80% horizon reaches 4–8 hours. ARC-AGI-3 scores reach 20–40%. First reliable autonomous agents for narrow professional tasks. Enterprise AI success rates improve to 40–60%.
  • 2035–2040: Architectural innovations combined with scale begin to close the bounded/open-ended gap. Sustained autonomous operation for 1–2 days on well-defined tasks. Partial AI automation of AI research begins to accelerate progress. Physical reasoning improves significantly.
  • 2040–2050: Progressive closing of remaining gaps. Cross-domain transfer improves. Continual learning partially achieved. Embodied reasoning reaches practical levels for laboratory science. BCP achieved for most cognitive domains.

This trajectory is not pessimistic about the ultimate destination — it is pessimistic only about the speed of the journey. And it is grounded in the specific, quantitative evidence assembled in this document.

Note that this trajectory implies enormous economic value from AI before BCP. By 2035, AI will likely be capable of reliably handling a wide range of bounded cognitive tasks, substantially augmenting human professionals across most knowledge-work domains. The economic impact may be comparable to the internet or mobile computing. The pessimist does not predict an AI winter — rather, a period of steady, substantial progress that falls short of the transformative BCP threshold the optimist envisions for the mid-2030s.

Appendix: Summary of Crux Assessments

The synthesis document identifies 9 cruxes (critical uncertainties) for the BCP timeline. The pessimist assessment of each:

Crux Description Pessimist Assessment Key Evidence
1 Current capability level BCP-critical gaps remain enormous ARC-AGI-3 270x gap, METR 27-min cliff
2 METR time horizons 80%/50% divergence is structurally serious (45% Claim S) Error compounding math, goal drift
3 AI research automation Ideation-execution gap blocks fast recursion (35% Claim S) Stanford SALT Lab findings
4 Pre-training scaling Diminishing returns now dominant GPT-5, 400+ model study, data exhaustion
5 Generalization Evidence genuinely unresolved (50/50) RoR-Bench fragility vs. transfer hints
6 Compute trajectory Infrastructure constraints are binding 4-7 year grid waits, 7 GW crisis
7 Lab-to-deployment Enterprise failure rates confirm massive gap 80-95% failure, increasing abandonment
8 Historical calibration 70-year base rate of overoptimism Mitchell’s four fallacies, self-driving cars
9 Recursive improvement Serial bottlenecks slow the loop (30% fast scenario) Verification, compute, engineering deployment

The pessimist assigns higher weight to slow scenarios (C, D, E) because the strongest evidence — structural architectural limitations, diminishing scaling returns, infrastructure constraints, and enterprise deployment failure — consistently favors later timelines.

This argument was compiled from research notes incorporating sources published through May 2026. All inline citations link to primary sources or first-hand reporting.

The Optimist Case for Broad Cognitive Parity by 2028-2033

Core claim: AI systems will match or exceed median human performance across virtually all economically relevant cognitive domains by 2030 (70% CI: 2028-2033). The evidence supporting near-term broad cognitive parity (BCP) has strengthened dramatically since early 2025, with multiple independent lines converging on this timeline.

1. Executive Summary

The synthesis document estimates BCP by 2036 (median), with a 25th-75th percentile range of 2031-2042. This argument contends that estimate is too conservative by 4-6 years, for three reinforcing reasons:

  1. Capability breakthroughs are accelerating, not decelerating. AI autonomously disproved an 80-year-old mathematical conjecture in May 2026 – a result verified by Fields-level mathematicians. This is not optimization; it is open-ended intellectual discovery.

  2. The compute-investment-revenue flywheel is spinning faster than projected. Hyperscaler capex hit $725B in 2026, Anthropic grew from $1B to $44B ARR in 18 months, and inference costs are dropping 10x/year. The “bubble” narrative requires ignoring these numbers.

  3. Every hard benchmark follows the same pattern: initial claims of unsaturability, rapid progress, saturation within 2-3 years. No exception has emerged. HLE, FrontierMath, and METR time horizons are all on steep trajectories that, even with deceleration, reach human-level thresholds by 2028-2030.

The argument is structured around the synthesis’s own crux framework. On 5 of the 12 cruxes, the synthesis already leans optimist. I argue the evidence pushes at least 3 more cruxes (Crux 3, 4, and 8) past the 50% threshold for the optimist position, which cascades through the dependency structure to compress all timeline estimates.

A key structural observation: the synthesis shifted 5 of 8 original cruxes toward S (the optimist position) in its Round 2 revision. The L-lean count dropped from 5/8 to 3/12; the S-lean count rose from 1/8 to 5/12. This directional trend is itself evidence – the synthesis’s own updating process consistently finds the data more optimist-favorable than initially assessed.

Falsifiable prediction: By December 2028, AI systems will score above 80% on HLE, above 70% on FrontierMath Tier 4, and will autonomously produce at least one novel result published in a top-5 ML venue. If all three fail, the optimist case is substantially weakened.

2. The Capability Explosion: May 2026 Changes the Picture

2.1 AI Disproves an 80-Year-Old Conjecture

On May 20, 2026, an OpenAI reasoning model autonomously disproved the planar unit distance conjecture, a problem posed by Paul Erdos in 1946 that had resisted 80 years of effort by professional mathematicians. The model discovered an infinite family of constructions using algebraic number theory (specifically Golod-Shafarevich theory), achieving n^(1+0.014) unit-distance pairs – exceeding the linear bound that was conjectured to be tight. The proof was verified by Fields-level mathematicians including Timothy Gowers, Noga Alon, Arul Shankar, and Jacob Tsimerman (ExplainX; Dataconomy).

This is the single most important piece of evidence in the entire timelines debate, because it directly addresses the strongest pessimist objection: that AI can optimize within known frameworks but cannot produce genuinely novel intellectual contributions. The Erdos disproof is:

  • Autonomous: the model was not hand-guided to the solution
  • Novel: no human had found this construction in 80 years
  • Deep: it draws on connections between algebraic number theory and combinatorial geometry that represent genuine mathematical insight
  • Verified: peer review by the highest-caliber mathematicians, not a self-reported claim

The synthesis (Crux 3) set the threshold as binary: “such a result exists or does not.” As of May 2026, such a result exists.

It is worth dwelling on the implications. The pessimist position on Crux 3 held that AI systems could optimize within known frameworks – solve competition problems, tune hyperparameters, search over known algorithms – but could not generate genuinely new ideas. The Erdos disproof does not merely weaken this position; it provides a concrete counterexample. The construction the model found was not in the training data. It was not a recombination of known proofs. It required identifying a non-obvious bridge between algebraic number theory and combinatorial geometry – exactly the kind of creative synthesis that was supposed to be beyond current architectures.

2.2 Aletheia: Autonomous Mathematical Research at Scale

Google DeepMind’s Aletheia system scored 95.1% on IMO-Proof Bench Advanced, shattering the previous record of 65.7%. More significantly, when deployed against 700 open mathematical problems, it discovered 63 technically appropriate solutions and resolved 4 open questions autonomously. On the FirstProof challenge (10 novel problems designed to test genuine research capability), it solved 6 of 10 (MarkTechPost; InfoQ).

This is not competition math. Solving open problems is the quintessential example of unbounded, open-ended intellectual work. The pessimist framing – “bounded good, open-ended bad” – predicted exactly this kind of result would not happen with current architectures. It happened.

The quantitative details matter. 63 technically appropriate solutions out of 700 open problems is a 9% solve rate. This is low in absolute terms, but the relevant comparison is not 100% – it is zero. Before Aletheia, no AI system had produced even one solution to an open mathematical problem through autonomous research. Going from 0 to 63 is a qualitative phase transition, not an incremental improvement. And the 4 fully resolved open questions represent permanent additions to mathematical knowledge.

The IMO-Proof Bench Advanced score (95.1% vs. previous 65.7%) is also significant because it demonstrates that the competition-math ceiling – which some argued was approaching – was not a ceiling at all. The 29.4 percentage point jump in a single model generation suggests substantial headroom remains.

2.3 Four Research-Grade Milestones in 30 Days

Between April 21 and May 20, 2026, four distinct research-grade breakthroughs landed:

  1. AlphaEvolve’s production deployment: 23% FlashAttention speedup, 32% kernel tiling speedup, and the first improvement to matrix multiplication beyond Strassen’s algorithm in 56 years
  2. DeepMind’s FrontierMath Tier 4 result: >30% on research-level mathematics, up from <2% just 16 months earlier
  3. WorldReasonBench video benchmark achievement: extending AI reasoning to multimodal domains
  4. OpenAI’s autonomous Erdos disproof: the first AI-generated disproof of a prominent open mathematical conjecture

The pace itself is evidence. Four results of this caliber in 30 days is not consistent with a field approaching diminishing returns – it is consistent with acceleration (Tech Jacks Solutions).

Consider the counterfactual: if someone in 2023 had predicted that by mid-2026, AI would autonomously disprove a famous open conjecture, solve open mathematical problems at scale, improve upon a 56-year-old algorithm, and exceed 30% on research-level mathematics benchmarks, that prediction would have been considered wildly optimistic even by most AI researchers. All four happened. The question is not whether to update toward shorter timelines, but by how much.

2.4 The Significance of Autonomous vs. Guided Discovery

A critical distinction in evaluating AI research capability is between guided and autonomous discovery. Much of the pessimist case rests on the observation that previous AI results (like AlphaFold or AlphaGo) involved substantial human design of the problem formulation, reward function, and search space. The Erdos disproof is qualitatively different:

  • The model was given the problem statement, not a carefully curated search space
  • It chose its own proof strategy (algebraic number theory) from the full space of mathematical approaches
  • It identified non-obvious connections that domain experts had not explored
  • The solution required multi-step reasoning across subfields

This is closer to how human mathematicians actually work: they receive a problem, draw on their broad knowledge, and search for non-obvious connections. The gap between “AI as a tool guided by human researchers” and “AI as an autonomous researcher” has narrowed dramatically.

The Aletheia results reinforce this. Solving 6 of 10 FirstProof challenge problems – problems specifically designed to require genuine research insight – demonstrates that autonomous mathematical research is not a one-off achievement but a repeatable capability.

2.5 AlphaEvolve and the Strassen Barrier

AlphaEvolve’s discovery of the first improvement to matrix multiplication beyond Strassen’s 1969 algorithm deserves separate emphasis. Matrix multiplication is one of the most studied problems in all of computer science. Thousands of researchers have worked on it for over half a century. Finding a better algorithm is not a matter of searching over a large but well-defined space – it requires genuine algorithmic insight.

The practical results compound this. A 23% speedup to FlashAttention (the most widely used attention mechanism in production AI systems) and a 32% improvement to kernel tiling operations represent direct economic value. These are not toy problems or benchmark games; they are optimizations deployed in production systems that serve billions of queries.

This result partially addresses the synthesis’s key caveat on Crux 3: “No AI system has autonomously discovered a significant ML breakthrough to date (May 2026).” While a FlashAttention speedup is not a fundamental ML breakthrough, an improvement to matrix multiplication that had eluded human researchers for 56 years is certainly significant. The gap between “significant algorithmic contribution” and “ML breakthrough” is narrowing.

3. The Benchmark Trajectory: No Sign of Slowdown

3.1 Current Scores on Hard Benchmarks
Benchmark Score (May 2026) Score (Jan 2025) Change Time
HLE 45-65% 8% 5-8x 16 months
FrontierMath (T1-3) >40% <2% >20x 16 months
FrontierMath (T4) >30% <2% >15x 16 months
SWE-bench Verified 93.9% ~60% 1.6x 12 months
GPQA Diamond 94.3% ~65% 1.5x 12 months
GAIA 74.5% ~20% 3.7x 12 months
ARC-AGI-2 98% 0% – ~12 months
AIME 2025 100% ~30% – ~12 months

Sources: agi.safe.ai, Epoch AI, swebench.com, PricePerToken

Note that GPQA Diamond at 94.3% exceeds the human expert baseline of 69.7%. This is not AI approaching human performance – it is AI surpassing human domain experts on a test designed to be difficult for domain experts. The synthesis should be counting this as a domain where BCP has already been achieved.

3.2 The Saturation-and-Replace Cycle

The synthesis itself identifies a recurring pattern (Section 5.2): a benchmark is introduced as “unsaturatable,” AI systems rapidly climb it, it saturates within 1-3 years, and a harder benchmark replaces it. This has happened with MMLU (saturated at 88-94%), HumanEval (97-98%), GPQA Diamond (94.3% vs. human expert 69.7%), and AIME.

The critical question: does this pattern reflect genuine capability growth, or merely narrow benchmark optimization? The evidence strongly favors genuine growth:

  • Different benchmarks, same trajectory. If progress were benchmark-specific overfitting, we would expect uneven performance across domains. Instead, we see consistent rapid improvement across mathematics (AIME, FrontierMath), science (GPQA), coding (SWE-bench, HumanEval), general reasoning (GAIA, ARC-AGI-2), and expert-level knowledge (HLE). The correlation across domains is strong evidence for general capability increase.

  • Transfer to real-world tasks. SWE-bench Verified tests actual software engineering on real GitHub repositories. METR time horizons measure agentic task completion in realistic settings. The Erdos disproof is a real mathematical contribution, not a benchmark result. The 84% developer adoption rate for AI coding tools confirms that benchmark progress translates to practical utility.

  • Independent evaluators confirm. GDPVal (designed by Morgan Stanley to measure economically valuable capabilities) shows GPT-5.4 at 83% – at or above human expert level on economically relevant tasks (Fortune). Morgan Stanley has no incentive to inflate AI capabilities – if anything, their fiduciary duty to investors creates an incentive toward accuracy.

  • ARC-AGI-2 specifically designed to resist pattern-matching. ARC-AGI was created by Francois Chollet as a test of “genuine fluid intelligence” that couldn’t be solved by pattern-matching on training data. ARC-AGI-2 was an even harder version designed specifically after ARC-AGI-1 was solved. It went from 0% to 98% in approximately 12 months. Chollet’s own benchmark, designed to be unsolvable by current approaches, was saturated within a year of release.

3.3 Already-Saturated Benchmarks: A Catalogue of “Impossible” Tasks

It is worth listing the benchmarks that have been saturated, along with the original claims made about their difficulty, because the pattern is so consistent:

  • MMLU (2020): Designed to test broad knowledge across 57 subjects at elementary through professional levels. Saturated at 88-94%. At introduction, authors expected it to take “many years” to saturate.
  • HumanEval (2021): Tests code generation from natural language descriptions. Saturated at 97-98%. Initially viewed as a demanding test of programming ability.
  • GPQA Diamond (2023): PhD-level science questions written by domain experts. Saturated at 94.3% – exceeding the human expert baseline of 69.7%. Designed to be difficult even for experts.
  • AIME 2025 (2025): American Invitational Mathematics Examination. Saturated at 100%. This is a competitive exam that selects the top ~5% of high school mathematics students.
  • ARC-AGI-2 (2024): Specifically designed by Francois Chollet as a test of “fluid intelligence” that current AI approaches could not solve. Saturated at 98% within approximately 12 months of release.

In every case, the benchmark was designed with the explicit or implicit belief that it would resist AI progress for years. In every case, it was saturated within 1-4 years. The base rate for “this benchmark will resist AI for 5+ years” is effectively zero.

The current “last resort” benchmarks are HLE and FrontierMath. If the historical pattern holds, they will be saturated by 2028-2029. The synthesis’s 2036 estimate implicitly assumes this pattern breaks – but provides no mechanistic account of why it would.

3.4 The Domain Coverage Argument

The pessimist might concede benchmark progress while arguing that the remaining unsaturated domains are qualitatively harder – that there are entire categories of cognition (physical reasoning, social intelligence, long-horizon planning, causal reasoning) where benchmarks haven’t been created yet, and these might take decades.

This argument has diminishing force for three reasons:

  1. The covered domains already represent the majority of economic value. Mathematics, science, coding, legal reasoning, medical diagnosis, and general knowledge work – all domains where AI is demonstrably near or above human expert level – account for most white-collar cognitive labor.

  2. The remaining domains are being tested. METR time horizons address long-horizon planning. WorldReasonBench addresses physical and multimodal reasoning. GAIA addresses multi-step web-based tasks. These are precisely the “hard” domains the pessimist points to, and they show the same rapid improvement trajectory.

  3. The synthesis’s own “embodiment not required” crux (55% S) acknowledges this. Most economically relevant cognitive tasks don’t require physical embodiment or the kinds of social intelligence that might be hardest for AI.

3.5 Extrapolation to Human-Level Thresholds

HLE is currently the hardest public benchmark for AI systems. The trajectory from 8% (January 2025) to 45-65% (May 2026) represents an improvement rate of roughly 2.5-4x per year. Even if this rate halves due to increasing difficulty at higher scores, the trajectory reaches 80%+ by late 2027 to early 2028.
