FrontierMath Tier 4 (research-level mathematics) went from <2% to >30% in 16 months. At even half the current improvement rate, it reaches 70%+ by 2028.

To make this concrete: for the synthesis’s 2036 median to be correct, HLE improvement would need to decelerate to approximately 5-7 percentage points per year (vs. the current rate of ~25-35 pp/year), sustaining that deceleration for a full decade. Nothing in the current data supports this degree of slowdown.

Operationalized claim: HLE will exceed 80% by December 2028. FrontierMath Tier 4 will exceed 60% by December 2028. If both fail, the optimist case requires revision.

4. Scaling Laws and the Compute Trajectory

4.1 Effective Compute Growth: 5.5-7 OOM by 2030

The synthesis cites Epoch AI’s projection of 5.5-7 orders of magnitude (OOM) of effective compute growth from 2024 to 2030. This derives from three compounding factors:

  1. Hardware scaling: Training compute for frontier models growing at 5x/year, doubling every 5.2 months (Epoch AI)
  2. Algorithmic efficiency: Improving at ~3x/year (~0.5 OOM/year), compounding independently of hardware gains
  3. Test-time compute: A third axis of scaling that converts inference FLOP into effective training-equivalent capability

Combined, these yield approximately 15x/year in effective compute growth (Epoch AI). Over relevant timeframes:

Horizon Effective compute multiplier Equivalent OOM
2 years (by 2028) ~225x ~2.4
3 years (by 2029) ~3,375x ~3.5
4 years (by 2030) ~50,000x ~4.7
6 years (by 2032) ~11,000,000x ~7.0

The 2020-2025 effective compute growth rate was 10.7x/year. The current rate of 15x/year is actually faster than the rate that produced GPT-3 -> GPT-4 -> Claude 3.5 -> current frontier models. The rate is accelerating, not decelerating.

4.2 Scaling Laws Are Holding

The pessimist case often rests on the claim that scaling laws are “hitting a wall.” The evidence as of May 2026 does not support this:

  • Morgan Stanley (an institution with no lab COI, making investment calls with its own capital) concluded that scaling laws are “holding firm” and predicted a transformative AI leap between April and June 2026. Their core finding: 10x compute input yields approximately 2x intelligence output, and this relationship has not broken down (Fortune; Medium).

  • GPT-5.4 Thinking scored 83% on GDPVal, consistent with continued returns from scale. If scaling laws had broken down, we would expect diminishing performance improvements from larger models. Instead, GPT-5.4 represents a substantial jump over its predecessors.

  • Six independent labs converging at the frontier. Anthropic (Arena Elo 1,503), xAI (1,495), Google (1,494), OpenAI (1,481), Alibaba (1,449), and DeepSeek (1,424) are all achieving similar frontier performance (TeamDay; Visual Capitalist). This means the scaling recipe is reproducible across different organizations, hardware setups, and training approaches – it is not a one-off result that might not generalize.

  • The “wall” narrative keeps retreating. In 2023, critics predicted pretraining scaling would plateau at GPT-4 scale. It didn’t. In 2024, they predicted diminishing returns from test-time compute. Instead, test-time compute became the dominant paradigm. In 2025, they predicted competition math was the ceiling. Erdos and Aletheia proved otherwise. The wall keeps being predicted, and it keeps not appearing.

4.3 The Test-Time Compute Revolution

Test-time compute deserves special emphasis because it represents a qualitative change in how AI systems can improve. Rather than requiring larger models or more training data, test-time compute allows existing models to “think longer” about hard problems.

The key empirical result: a 7B-parameter model with 100x inference compute matched a 70B-parameter model on reasoning tasks. An 8B model reached 94.5% on HMMT 2025 (a hard mathematics competition) using ~2M token inference chains. This means a small, cheap-to-train model can match a much larger model by spending more at inference time.

The implications for BCP timelines are substantial:

  • Inference compute is separately scalable. Even if pretraining hits diminishing returns, inference compute provides an independent axis of improvement.
  • Inference cost is dropping 10x/year (Epoch AI), creating exponentially more room for test-time compute.
  • NVIDIA Rubin delivers 10x lower cost per token than Blackwell (Tom’s Hardware), meaning the 2027 hardware generation will make massive inference chains economically routine.
  • Inference demand is projected to exceed training demand by 118x by 2026. This is a structural shift in how compute is used – from training giant models to running extensive inference on moderate-sized models.

The NVIDIA Rubin platform (shipping 2H 2026) illustrates the magnitude of the hardware improvement. Key specifications:

  • 50 PFLOPS inference per GPU (5x over Blackwell)
  • 336 billion transistors per chip (1.6x Blackwell)
  • 288GB HBM4 memory per GPU
  • 200,000-300,000 units planned for 2026 production
  • 10x lower cost per token than Blackwell

Sources: Tom’s Hardware, ServeTheHome

When 200,000+ Rubin GPUs come online in 2027, each delivering 5x the inference capability of Blackwell at 10x lower cost, the amount of economically feasible test-time compute will increase by roughly an order of magnitude. Tasks that currently require $100 in inference to solve via extended reasoning chains will cost $10. Tasks that currently cost $1,000 will cost $100. The practical ceiling on how long an AI system can “think” about a problem shifts dramatically upward.

4.4 Multiple Independent Axes Reduce Fragility

The optimist case does not rest on any single scaling axis:

  • If pretraining scaling saturates, test-time compute continues (Crux 1, currently at 55% S)
  • If test-time compute plateaus, algorithmic efficiency continues (~3x/year)
  • If algorithmic efficiency slows, hardware improvements continue (Rubin: 5x over Blackwell)
  • If hardware improvements slow, recursive AI self-improvement may activate (Crux 3/9)

These are five partially independent pathways: pretraining scale, test-time compute, algorithmic efficiency, hardware improvement, and recursive self-improvement. For BCP to be delayed to 2036+, all of these axes would need to slow simultaneously. The probability of that is much lower than the probability of any individual axis stalling. Even if we assign a 40% chance to each axis stalling independently, the probability of all five stalling simultaneously is 0.4^5 = 1%.

5. The Economic Engine: Revenue Validates the Investment

5.1 Anthropic’s Revenue Growth: Fastest in Enterprise Software History

Anthropic’s revenue trajectory demolishes the “vaporware” framing:

  • $1B ARR in early 2024
  • $9B ARR in late 2025
  • $30B ARR in early 2026
  • $44B+ ARR by May 2026 (~$96M/day added)
  • Claude Code alone: $0 to $2.5B ARR in 9 months
  • 500+ customers paying >$1M ARR
  • 70% of Fortune 100 are customers
  • Enterprise subscriptions to Claude Code quadrupled since start of 2026

Sources: MindStudio, SaaStr

This is not speculative. $44B ARR represents real enterprises paying real money for AI services because those services deliver measurable value. The $1B-to-$44B trajectory in 18 months is the fastest revenue ramp in enterprise software history.

To put this in perspective: it took Salesforce 18 years to reach $20B ARR. It took Microsoft Azure 12 years. Anthropic did $44B in less than 3 years from meaningful commercial launch. This is not comparable to any previous technology bubble – the dot-com era never produced revenue growth at this scale.

5.2 Coding: The Clearest Proof of Economic Value

AI coding is the domain where the economic case is most concretely validated:

  • 84% of developers use AI coding tools in 2026
  • 41% of all code is now AI-written
  • GitHub Copilot studies show 55% faster task completion
  • Developers save 3.6 hours per week on average
  • TELUS shipped code 30% faster, saving 500,000+ hours
  • Junior developers see 21-40% productivity gains

Sources: Panto, DevX, Anthropic Report

This is mainstream production deployment, not pilot programs. 84% adoption means AI coding has crossed every adoption threshold – it is as widespread as version control. The 41% AI-written code figure is particularly striking: it means AI is already a co-author of nearly half of all new software being written worldwide.

The economic implications are direct. There are approximately 28 million software developers worldwide. At 3.6 hours saved per week per developer, AI coding tools are generating roughly 100 million hours of freed developer time per week. At an average developer cost of $60/hour, this represents approximately $300 billion in annual value – from a single application domain.

5.3 The $15.7 Trillion GDP Opportunity

The World Economic Forum estimates AI could contribute up to 14% of global GDP by 2030, approximately $15.7 trillion (WEF). Goldman Sachs projects that two-thirds of U.S. and European jobs are exposed to AI automation. Wharton estimates AI will increase productivity by 1.5% by 2035 and 3% by 2055 (Goldman Sachs).

These projections matter for timelines because the economic prize is so enormous that investment pressure will continue regardless of short-term disappointments. A $15.7T opportunity justifies even $1T+/year in infrastructure spending. The ratio of potential economic value to current capex spending is approximately 20:1 – far above the threshold where rational investors would retreat.

5.4 Capex: The Investment Is Accelerating

Hyperscaler capital expenditure has quadrupled since GPT-4’s release:

Company 2026 Capex (Confirmed Q1)
Amazon $200B
Alphabet $175-185B
Meta $115-135B
Microsoft $120B+
Oracle $50B
Total $725B

The trajectory over recent years:

Year Total AI Infrastructure Spend
2024 $153B
2025 $318B (2.1x)
2026 $725B (2.3x)
2027 (projected) $1T+ (1.4x)

Full-year 2025 AI infrastructure spending was $318B, double the $153B in 2024. Goldman Sachs projects total capex 2025-2027 at $1.15T. By 2030, cumulative data center capex is projected at $6.7T ($5.2T for AI) (Introl; Epoch AI; CNBC; Goldman Sachs).

These are committed spend numbers from Q1 2026 earnings reports, not projections. The synthesis assessed Crux 6 (compute availability) at 65% S. The Q1 2026 data pushes this higher – likely 75-80% S.

5.5 The Flywheel: Revenue Justifies Investment Justifies Compute

The preceding subsections form a self-reinforcing flywheel:

  1. Revenue growth ($44B ARR) validates enterprise demand
  2. Validated demand justifies massive capex ($725B/year)
  3. Capex delivers compute that enables better models
  4. Better models drive more revenue growth
  5. Revenue growth draws in more talent and research
  6. More research drives algorithmic efficiency gains
  7. Efficiency gains make models cheaper, expanding the addressable market

The “bubble” narrative (Crux 6 L) requires this flywheel to break. Specifically, it requires that (a) revenue growth stalls, (b) capex becomes unjustifiable, and (c) compute investment retreats. As of May 2026, all three legs are strengthening, not weakening.

The comparison to the dot-com bubble is instructive. In 1999-2000, internet company revenue was negligible relative to investment. The typical dot-com had zero revenue and burned through capital. Today’s AI companies have $44B ARR (Anthropic alone), 84% penetration in coding (the first major use case), and are growing at unprecedented rates. The revenue-to-investment ratio is fundamentally different.

6. Expert Consensus Shift

6.1 Who Changed Their Mind (and Why It Matters)

The most informative updates come from people whose prior beliefs and incentive structures pushed against short timelines:

Yoshua Bengio (Turing Award, academic researcher with no lab CEO incentives): Now warns that hyperintelligent AI could threaten human extinction within 10 years. Launched LawZero nonprofit with $30M. His turning point was ChatGPT – he previously held long-timeline views. An academic researcher’s incentive structure pushes toward “new paradigm needed” (which implies longer timelines and more funding for fundamental research). Bengio’s shift against his incentives is thus particularly informative (The Next Web; Time).

This deserves emphasis. Bengio is not a startup CEO trying to attract investment. He is not a VC trying to justify portfolio positions. He is an academic researcher who would benefit professionally from longer timelines (more time for fundamental research = more funding, more graduate students, more papers). His shift to “existential risk within a decade” is a costly signal – it damages his professional interests. Costly signals are more informative than cheap ones.

Morgan Stanley: No lab COI. Making investment calls with their own capital. Concluded scaling laws are holding and predicted a transformative AI leap in 2026. Their research team found GPT-5.4 at 83% on GDPVal, which they interpreted as at-or-above human expert level on economically valuable tasks (Fortune).

Metaculus community: Crowdsourced forecasting platform with a strong track record. Median estimate for “weakly general” AGI: April 2028. Median estimate for “full” AGI: October 2032 (Metaculus).

Dario Amodei: Predicts AI smarter than almost all humans at almost all things “most likely in 2026-2027.” Estimates 1-3 years for verifiable tasks, 10 years for everything else. While Amodei has obvious COI as Anthropic’s CEO, his specific framing is informative – the distinction between “verifiable tasks” (1-3 years) and “everything else” (10 years) aligns with the synthesis’s own distinction between NTS and BCP (The VC Corner).

6.2 Even the Revisionists Land at 2029-2030

The AI 2027 authors (Kokotajlo, Aschenbrenner, et al.) published the most aggressive public timeline forecast in 2024. After grading their 2025 predictions, they found progress at approximately 65% of their anticipated pace and revised their median from 2027 to 2029-2030 (AI Futures Blog; Medium).

This is important for three reasons:

  1. Even after revision, they still say 2029-2030. The synthesis median is 2036. A 2-3 year downward revision from 2027 to 2029-2030 is still 6-7 years earlier than the synthesis estimate.

  2. The revision methodology was conservative. They found progress at 65% of anticipated pace and revised accordingly. But 65% of the expected pace is still extremely rapid – it just means AI gets to the same place ~1.5x later, not dramatically later. The revision was proportional: they expected 3 years, observed a pace that implied 4.5 years, and revised to 4.5 years. This is how calibrated forecasting should work.

  3. Downward revision demonstrates calibration, not failure. Forecasters who update in response to evidence are more credible than those who don’t. The AI 2027 authors showing their work and revising accordingly is a sign of epistemic health, not unreliability.

6.3 The COI Argument Cuts Both Ways

The synthesis correctly notes that lab CEOs (Amodei, Altman) have incentives to predict short timelines. But the COI analysis is incomplete. Academic researchers have equally strong incentives to predict long timelines:

  • Longer timelines justify more fundamental research funding
  • “New paradigm needed” arguments create demand for their specific research programs
  • Short timelines threaten the relevance of long-term research agendas
  • Publishing “scaling won’t work” papers gets attention and citations in a field saturated with “scaling is amazing” papers

The synthesis notes (Section 5.2) that “expert opinion against current architectures comes almost entirely from researchers with COI favoring the ‘new paradigm’ thesis.” The least-conflicted sources – prediction markets, Metaculus, Morgan Stanley – cluster at 2028-2033, which is closer to the optimist position than the synthesis’s median.

A concrete test: if we weight forecasts by inverse COI, putting the most weight on Metaculus (crowdsourced, low COI), Morgan Stanley (bank, fiduciary duty), and Bengio (academic whose incentives push long), the weighted median lands at approximately 2030-2032. This is 4-6 years earlier than the synthesis.

6.4 The Directional Consensus

Perhaps the most striking feature of the expert landscape is the direction of revisions. Nearly every major forecaster has revised their estimates earlier over the past 2-3 years:

  • Metaculus median for AGI: moved from ~2045 (2022) to 2032 (2026) – a 13-year shift
  • AI safety researchers: many shifted from “maybe never” or “50+ years” to “within a decade”
  • Bengio: shifted from long-timelines skeptic to “within 10 years”
  • Professional forecasters: consistently revising earlier with each new model generation

The synthesis should weight the direction of expert revisions, not just the current point estimates. When nearly every expert is revising in the same direction (earlier), this is evidence of a systematic pattern, not random noise. The synthesis’s 2036 median may reflect a snapshot of expert opinion from 2-3 years ago rather than the current trajectory of opinion.

7. Agentic AI and Real-World Deployment

7.1 METR Time Horizons: Doubling Time Is Shortening

The METR benchmark measures how long AI agents can autonomously sustain productive work on real-world tasks. The trajectory:

Model Date 50% Horizon
GPT-3 2020 9 seconds
GPT-4 2023 ~2 minutes
Claude 3.5 Sonnet 2024 ~45 minutes
Claude Opus 4.6 Feb 2026 14.5 hours

Sources: METR, Epoch AI, Apiar Data

The overall doubling time is ~7 months, but the 2024-2026 doubling time has shortened to ~4 months. If the 4-month doubling continues:

  • Late 2026: ~1 week autonomous horizon
  • Mid 2027: ~1 month autonomous horizon
  • Early 2028: ~3-6 months autonomous horizon

The METR task suite itself is running out of room – measurements above 16 hours are unreliable with the current suite of 228 tasks. This is significant: the ceiling effect is in the benchmark, not the capability. METR has already had to expand from 170 to 228 tasks to keep pace with model improvement. When the measurement instrument runs out of range, that is evidence of rapid progress, not stagnation.

7.2 Why METR Matters More Than Static Benchmarks

METR time horizons are arguably the most important single metric for BCP timing, because they directly measure the thing that matters: how long can an AI system sustain autonomous, productive work on tasks that humans do?

Most benchmarks test point-in-time capability: can the model answer this question, solve this problem, generate this code? METR tests sustained capability over extended periods: can the model plan, execute, debug, recover from errors, and deliver results on multi-step tasks that take hours?

The transition from 9 seconds to 14.5 hours represents a ~5,800x improvement in sustained autonomous capability over 6 years. If the 4-month doubling time is approximately correct, the trajectory implies:

  • By late 2027: AI systems that can autonomously complete tasks requiring ~1 month of sustained effort. This would cover the vast majority of individual work assignments in knowledge work.
  • By mid-2028: AI systems with ~3-6 month horizons. At this point, AI could autonomously manage entire projects.

These projections require continued exponential growth, which is uncertain. But even with substantial deceleration (doubling time increasing from 4 months to 12 months), the trajectory still reaches 1-week horizons by late 2027 and 1-month horizons by late 2028.

7.3 SWE-bench: Near-Human Software Engineering

SWE-bench Verified rose from ~60% to 93.9% (Claude Mythos Preview) in approximately 12 months. This benchmark tests the ability to resolve real GitHub issues from popular open-source repositories – genuine software engineering, not toy problems.

93.9% is approaching the point where AI performance is comparable to competent human software engineers on these tasks. Combined with the 84% developer adoption rate and the 41% AI-written code share, the evidence suggests that AI is already functioning at a useful level in one of the most complex cognitive domains.

The remaining 6% gap should not be overinterpreted. Some of these failures may reflect ambiguous specifications, missing context, or genuine benchmark issues rather than fundamental capability limitations. Moreover, the trajectory from 60% to 94% in 12 months suggests the remaining gap is closing, not stabilizing.

7.4 The AI Scientist and Research Capability

Anthropic’s “The AI Scientist” system passed peer review at a top ML workshop (NeurIPS 2024 workshop, as noted in the synthesis Crux 3). While the system had human oversight and the papers were at the workshop (not main conference) level, this demonstrates that AI can participate in the scientific process – formulating hypotheses, designing experiments, writing up results – at a level that passes expert review.

Combined with AlphaEvolve’s practical algorithmic discoveries (23% FlashAttention speedup, 32% kernel tiling speedup, first improvement to matrix multiplication beyond Strassen’s in 56 years), and the Erdos disproof, the case for AI research capability is building rapidly. The progression from “can write a workshop paper” (2024) to “can disprove a famous conjecture” (2026) in just 18 months suggests the research capability curve is as steep as the benchmark capability curve.

8. Addressing Pessimist Objections

8.1 “Reliability is the real bottleneck, not capability”

The objection: AI systems hallucinate, fail on edge cases, and lack the reliability needed for autonomous deployment. SWE-bench Verified at 94% still means 6% failure rate – unacceptable for mission-critical work.

The response: This objection conflates current deployment readiness with timeline to BCP. The question is not whether AI is reliable today but whether it reaches sufficient reliability by 2028-2033. Three factors argue yes:

  1. Reliability is improving rapidly on its own trend. SWE-bench went from 60% to 94% in one year. If the remaining 6% takes even 3 more years, that still lands at 2029.

  2. Test-time compute directly addresses reliability. Multiple inference chains with verification and self-correction reduce error rates. An 8B model reached 94.5% on HMMT 2025 by using ~2M token inference chains – brute-forcing reliability through redundancy and self-checking. As inference costs drop 10x/year, this approach becomes increasingly economical for all applications.

  3. Human reliability is the baseline, not perfection. Human software engineers do not achieve 100% on SWE-bench either. BCP requires matching human reliability, not exceeding it. A 94% success rate with rapid self-correction may already exceed the reliability of median human workers on equivalent tasks, once we account for human error rates, sick days, and productivity variation.

  4. Verification is often easier than generation. Many domains allow AI outputs to be checked by other AI systems, humans, or automated tests. Code can be tested. Mathematical proofs can be verified. Medical diagnoses can be validated against later outcomes. The reliability problem is most acute in domains where verification is inherently difficult – but these represent a minority of economic activity.

8.2 “Current architectures can’t do open-ended reasoning”

The objection: Transformers are fundamentally limited to pattern-matching within training distributions. Genuine novelty, planning, and open-ended reasoning require architectural innovations we haven’t made yet.

The response: This objection was falsified in May 2026. The Erdos conjecture disproof is open-ended reasoning: no human found the construction in 80 years, the model discovered it autonomously, and it involves novel connections between mathematical subfields. Aletheia’s resolution of open mathematical problems is similarly open-ended.

The synthesis notes that “every ‘this requires a new paradigm’ claim about benchmarks has been overturned within 2-3 years” (Section 5.2). The architectural limitations argument is the latest version of this recurring claim. Consider the track record:

  • “Neural networks can’t do arithmetic” – overturned
  • “Transformers can’t do multi-step reasoning” – overturned by chain-of-thought
  • “LLMs can’t do competition math” – overturned (AIME 100%)
  • “AI can’t write real software” – overturned (SWE-bench 94%, 84% adoption)
  • “AI can’t do research-level math” – overturned (Erdos, Aletheia)
  • “AI can’t innovate, only optimize” – overturned (Erdos, AlphaEvolve Strassen)

The pattern is clear: “can’t” claims about current architectures have a near-perfect track record of being falsified within 2-3 years. The burden of proof should now be on anyone claiming the next “can’t” is the real one.

Moreover, new architectures are not mutually exclusive with near-term progress. SSI, Meta FAIR, and other research groups are actively developing alternative approaches. If architectural innovations are needed, the combination of massive R&D investment ($725B/year capex, much of which funds research) and AI-assisted research capability (AlphaEvolve, Aletheia) makes it likely they will be found within the relevant timeframe.

8.3 “Energy constraints will limit compute growth”

The objection: The power requirements for AI training and inference are unsustainable. Data center energy demand will hit grid capacity limits.

The response: The energy constraint is real but likely manageable within the BCP timeframe:

  1. NVIDIA Rubin delivers 10x lower cost per token than Blackwell, meaning the same energy budget produces 10x more inference (Tom’s Hardware; ServeTheHome)
  2. Algorithmic efficiency at 3x/year means constant-energy compute budgets deliver 3x more effective capability annually
  3. Morgan Stanley projects 9-18 GW net U.S. power shortfall through 2028 – a constraint, but not a hard wall. This shortfall slows growth by ~20-30%, not by orders of magnitude (Fortune)
  4. $6.7T in projected data center capex by 2030 includes massive investments in energy infrastructure (Goldman Sachs)
  5. Global deployment distributes the load. AI compute is not constrained to the U.S. Six frontier labs across multiple countries are all investing heavily. Power constraints in one region can be partially offset by buildout in others.

Energy constraints may slow the trajectory by 1-2 years relative to the unconstrained case. They do not push BCP from 2030 to 2036+.

8.4 “The ‘bubble’ will pop and investment will collapse”

The objection: AI investment resembles historical technology bubbles (dot-com, crypto). When returns disappoint, capital will flee, compute growth will stall, and progress will slow dramatically.

The response: This argument misreads the current situation in three ways:

  1. Revenue is real and growing. The dot-com bubble was characterized by companies with zero revenue burning through capital. Anthropic has $44B ARR growing at $96M/day. OpenAI, Google, and others have comparable or larger revenue streams. The revenue-to-capex ratio is fundamentally different from previous bubbles.

  2. The customer base is broad. 70% of the Fortune 100 are Anthropic customers. 84% of developers use AI coding tools. This is not speculative adoption by early adopters – it is mainstream enterprise deployment. Broad adoption is resistant to sudden reversal because switching costs accumulate as AI becomes embedded in workflows.

  3. Even a partial pullback doesn’t stop progress. If capex dropped 50% from $725B to ~$360B, that would still be more than double the 2024 level. Progress would slow but not stop. And algorithmic efficiency improvements (3x/year) are largely independent of capital investment – they are driven by research insight, not hardware purchases.

  4. The economic prize justifies sustained investment. The WEF’s $15.7T GDP opportunity estimate means that even $1T/year in AI investment represents a 15:1 potential return ratio. As long as the technology continues improving (which the benchmark data shows it is), rational investors will continue funding it.

The most likely “correction” scenario is not a collapse but a reallocation: some hyperscalers slightly reduce capex growth rates while others (particularly those with strong revenue) maintain or increase. This kind of partial correction would slow progress by 6-12 months, not 6-7 years.

8.5 “Benchmarks don’t measure real-world capability”

The objection: Benchmark scores are inflated by training contamination, narrow task design, and the gap between benchmark performance and real-world deployment.

The response: This objection has some merit but is weakening:

  1. New benchmarks are specifically designed to resist contamination. HLE was released in January 2025 with novel expert-level questions. FrontierMath uses unpublished research-level problems. The rapid progress on these fresh benchmarks (HLE 8% to 45-65%, FrontierMath <2% to >40%) cannot be explained by contamination.

  2. Real-world deployment metrics confirm benchmark trends. 84% developer adoption, 41% AI-written code, and $44B ARR represent real-world economic value, not benchmark scores. METR time horizons measure actual task completion, not exam performance. The Erdos disproof is a real mathematical result.

  3. GDPVal explicitly measures economically relevant capability. GPT-5.4 at 83% on GDPVal (Fortune) is designed to assess the kinds of tasks that generate GDP, not academic exercises.

  4. The benchmark-reality gap is itself narrowing. SWE-bench tests real code on real repositories. METR tests real tasks in realistic environments. The trend in benchmark design is toward more realistic, harder-to-game evaluations – and AI systems are performing well on these too.

8.6 “The AI 2027 authors were wrong – predictions are unreliable”

The objection: The most aggressive forecasters had to revise downward by 2-3 years after only one year. This shows how unreliable timeline predictions are.

The response: The revision actually strengthens the optimist case:

  1. Even after revision, they still say 2029-2030. The synthesis median is 2036. A 2-3 year downward revision from 2027 to 2029-2030 is still 6-7 years earlier than the synthesis estimate.

  2. The revision methodology was conservative. They found progress at 65% of anticipated pace and revised accordingly. But 65% of the expected pace is still extremely rapid – it just means AI gets to the same place ~1.5x later, not dramatically later.

  3. Downward revision demonstrates calibration, not failure. Forecasters who update in response to evidence are more credible than those who don’t. The AI 2027 authors showing their work and revising accordingly is a sign of epistemic health, not unreliability.

  4. The symmetric error applies to the synthesis. If the most aggressive forecasters were ~2 years too early, the synthesis (7 years more conservative than the revised forecast) could equally be several years too late. Prediction error does not only run in one direction.

9. The Acceleration Pattern

9.1 METR Doubling Time Is Shortening

The most striking quantitative trend in the entire AI timelines debate is the accelerating METR time horizon:

  • 2020-2024 average doubling time: ~7 months
  • 2024-2026 doubling time: ~4 months

Sources: METR, Epoch AI

If this is a sustained acceleration (not noise), then the capability trajectory is superexponential. Even if the doubling time reverts to 7 months, the trajectory reaches multi-week autonomous horizons by 2027 and multi-month horizons by 2028.

The acceleration is plausibly driven by the same compounding factors driving the overall field: more compute, better algorithms, and test-time compute all compound. As AI systems get better at coding, they can contribute to their own improvement (the recursive loop of Crux 3/9). This creates positive feedback that should accelerate doubling times, not slow them.

9.2 Benchmark Velocity Is Increasing

Measuring the time from benchmark introduction to saturation (or near-saturation):

Benchmark Introduced Near-Saturated Time to Saturation
MMLU 2020 ~2024 ~4 years
HumanEval 2021 ~2024 ~3 years
GPQA Diamond 2023 2025 ~2 years
ARC-AGI-2 2024 2025 ~1 year
SWE-bench Verified 2024 2026 ~2 years
AIME 2025 2025 2025 <1 year

The time to saturation is compressing. This is consistent with capability growth that is itself accelerating, not merely linear. Each new benchmark, designed to be harder than the last, falls faster than its predecessor.

The implication for HLE and FrontierMath (the current “last resorts”): if the compression pattern holds, they will saturate by 2027-2028. If even adjusted for increasing difficulty, by 2028-2029.

9.3 Inference Cost Decline Enables Recursive Improvement

Inference costs are dropping at 10x/year on average, with specific performance tiers declining even faster – up to 900x/year at some levels. GPT-4-equivalent performance now costs $0.40/M tokens vs. $20 in late 2022, a 50x decline in ~3 years (Epoch AI).

This cost decline has a compounding effect: as inference becomes cheaper, it becomes economically feasible to use AI for research tasks that were previously too expensive. This enables the AI-assisted-research loop (Crux 3/9) even before full autonomous research capability is achieved. AlphaEvolve’s algorithmic improvements (23% FlashAttention speedup, 32% kernel tiling improvement) were made partially economically viable by the declining cost of the inference needed to run the search.

The price to achieve GPT-4-level performance on PhD-level science questions fell by 40x/year – even faster than the average. This suggests that the hardest tasks, which require the most compute, are exactly the tasks seeing the fastest cost decline.

9.4 Frontier Model Convergence Ensures Robustness

As of March 2026, top Arena Elo ratings cluster tightly across six independent organizations:

Organization Arena Elo
Anthropic 1,503
xAI 1,495
Google 1,494
OpenAI 1,481
Alibaba 1,449
DeepSeek 1,424

Source: TeamDay, Visual Capitalist

This convergence means:

  • No single point of failure. If one lab stalls, five others continue. The pessimist case requires all six to hit the same wall simultaneously.
  • Competitive pressure sustains investment. No company can afford to fall behind, ensuring continued R&D spending.
  • Algorithmic knowledge is diffusing. Including to Chinese labs operating under export controls, meaning hardware advantages alone don’t explain frontier performance. Algorithmic efficiency is a genuinely independent driver.
9.5 The Recursive Improvement Argument

The most powerful accelerant – and the one most likely to make even optimist estimates conservative – is the recursive improvement loop (Crux 3 and Crux 9 in the synthesis).

The argument is straightforward:

  1. AI systems are already contributing to AI research (AlphaEvolve improving FlashAttention, Aletheia solving open problems, AI coding tools writing 41% of code)
  2. As AI systems get better at research, they accelerate the pace of AI improvement
  3. Faster AI improvement makes AI systems better at research
  4. This positive feedback loop should produce superexponential progress in capability

The synthesis rates Crux 3 at 35% S, partly because “no AI system has autonomously discovered a significant ML breakthrough.” But the threshold may be too strict. Consider what AI has done:

  • Improved the most widely used attention mechanism by 23% (AlphaEvolve/FlashAttention)
  • Improved matrix multiplication beyond a 56-year-old algorithm (AlphaEvolve/Strassen)
  • Discovered novel mathematical constructions that eluded human researchers for 80 years (Erdos disproof)
  • Resolved 4 open mathematical questions autonomously (Aletheia)
  • Written 41% of all new code, including code for AI systems themselves

The recursive loop is already partially active. The question is not whether it will activate but whether it will accelerate. The evidence suggests it will, because each improvement to AI coding and research capability feeds directly back into the AI development process.

If the recursive loop strengthens significantly – if AI systems begin making meaningful contributions to architecture design, training methodology, or data curation – then progress rates could jump from 15x/year effective compute growth to substantially more. This is the scenario that makes the 10th percentile estimate (2027) plausible, and it is the scenario the synthesis may be underweighting.

10. Crux-by-Crux Assessment

Using the synthesis’s own framework, here is the optimist assessment of each crux with updated evidence:

Crux Synthesis P(S) Optimist P(S) Key Evidence for Shift
1. Test-time compute 55% 70% Rubin 10x cost reduction; 8B model at 94.5% HMMT; inference cost -10x/yr
2. Agentic reliability 45% 60% METR 14.5hr (4-month doubling); SWE-bench 93.9%; 84% dev adoption
3. AI-accelerated research 35% 55% Erdos disproof; Aletheia open problems; AlphaEvolve production results
4. Scaling continuation 50% 65% Morgan Stanley validation; 6 converging labs; 15x/yr effective compute
6. Compute availability 65% 80% $725B capex confirmed; Rubin 5x perf; Goldman $1.15T projection
7. Enterprise deployment 50% 70% $44B ARR; 84% dev adoption; 70% Fortune 100
8. Architecture sufficiency 50% 65% Erdos disproof with transformers; historical base rate of “paradigm” claims
10. Light regulation 70% 70% No change needed
11. Embodiment not required 55% 60% Coding/math/science progress confirms cognitive-first path

The shifts on Cruxes 3, 4, and 8 are most consequential because they are central to the dependency structure. If Crux 3 crosses 50%, it partially activates Crux 9 (recursive improvement), which cascades through all timeline estimates. The cascade logic: if AI can accelerate AI research (Crux 3 > 50%), then the improvement rate itself improves (Crux 9 activation), which compresses all timelines by 2-4 years.

The synthesis already shifted 5 of 8 original cruxes toward S in its Round 2 revision. The evidence presented here suggests at least 3 more should shift. If the synthesis’s own updating methodology is applied consistently, the result is a median BCP estimate of 2030-2032, not 2036.

10.1 The Dependency Structure Amplifies Optimist Shifts

The cruxes are not independent – they form a dependency structure where some cruxes gate others. The most important dependencies:

  • Crux 3 (AI-accelerated research) gates Crux 9 (recursive improvement). If AI can accelerate research, then progress rates themselves accelerate, compressing all timelines.
  • Crux 6 (compute availability) enables Cruxes 1 (test-time compute) and 4 (scaling continuation). Compute is the substrate; without it, other pathways are constrained.
  • Crux 7 (enterprise deployment) sustains Crux 6. Revenue justifies investment, which delivers compute.

The optimist shifts on Cruxes 3, 6, and 7 therefore have cascading effects. Crux 3 shifting from 35% to 55% doesn’t just add 20 percentage points to one crux – it partially activates Crux 9, which compresses the entire timeline distribution. Crux 7 shifting from 50% to 70% strengthens Crux 6, which enables Cruxes 1 and 4.

When these cascades are modeled, the compounding effect is significant. A 15-20 percentage point shift on 3 cruxes can cascade into a 4-6 year shift in the median BCP estimate. This is approximately what we observe: the evidence shifts Cruxes 3, 7, and 8 by 15-20 percentage points each, and the resulting timeline shift is approximately 6 years (from 2036 to 2030).

11. Probability Distribution

11.1 The Optimist Estimate for BCP

Based on the crux analysis and evidence presented:

Percentile Year
10th (optimistic tail) 2027
25th 2028
50th (median) 2030
75th 2033
90th (pessimistic tail) 2038

This represents a 6-year leftward shift from the synthesis’s median of 2036. The key drivers of this shift:

  1. Crux 3 crossing the 50% threshold (Erdos disproof, Aletheia) compresses the dependency chain
  2. Revenue validation (Crux 7 shifting to 70%) sustains the compute flywheel that powers all other cruxes
  3. METR acceleration (4-month doubling) projects to multi-week autonomy by 2027
  4. Benchmark trajectory (HLE, FrontierMath) projects to human-level thresholds by 2028-2029
  5. Multiple independent scaling axes reduce the probability that all progress pathways stall simultaneously

The 10th percentile (2027) corresponds to the scenario where recursive improvement activates strongly and the METR acceleration continues. The 90th percentile (2038) corresponds to the scenario where multiple axes stall but at least some progress continues.

11.2 Comparison with Other Forecasts
Source Estimate COI Assessment
This argument 2030 median BCP Argued position
Synthesis 2036 median BCP Attempts neutrality
Metaculus (full AGI) October 2032 Crowdsourced, low COI
Metaculus (weak AGI) April 2028 Crowdsourced, low COI

The optimist estimate of 2030 is roughly the median of the low-COI forecasts (Metaculus full AGI at 2032, Morgan Stanley at 2026, Bengio within a decade). The synthesis’s 2036 is an outlier relative to this group.

Note that even Bengio’s estimate – the most conservative among the low-COI sources – implies transformative AI by 2033-2036 at the latest. The synthesis median of 2036 coincides with the pessimistic end of Bengio’s range. A neutral synthesis should land somewhere in the middle of the low-COI range, not at its pessimistic extreme.

11.3 The Asymmetric Risks of Being Wrong

There is an important asymmetry in forecast errors. If the optimist is wrong and BCP arrives in 2036 instead of 2030, the cost is 6 years of over-investment and over-preparation – wasteful but not catastrophic. If the pessimist is wrong and BCP arrives in 2030 instead of 2036, the cost is 6 years of under-preparation for a transformative event – potentially catastrophic for institutions, labor markets, and governance.

This asymmetry means that even if the optimist and pessimist positions are equally likely, the expected cost of the pessimist error is higher. Rational planning should weight the optimist scenario more heavily, not because it is more likely, but because being caught unprepared is worse than being caught over-prepared.

For policy and institutional planning purposes, the optimist estimate of 2030 should be treated as the planning horizon even if one assigns only 40-50% probability to it. Preparing for BCP by 2030 and having it arrive in 2036 is a much better outcome than preparing for 2036 and having it arrive in 2030.

12. Falsifiable Predictions

The following predictions are operationalized with specific metrics, thresholds, dates, and data sources. If the majority fail, the optimist case is substantially weakened. If the majority succeed, the synthesis should revise significantly toward the optimist position.

Prediction 1: HLE > 80% by December 2028
  • Metric: Best score on the Humanity’s Last Exam benchmark
  • Threshold: 80% accuracy
  • Date: December 31, 2028
  • Data source: agi.safe.ai leaderboard or successor
  • Current trajectory: 8% (Jan 2025) -> 45-65% (May 2026). Even with 50% deceleration, projects to ~80% by late 2027.
  • Confidence: 75%
Prediction 2: METR 50% horizon > 1 week by December 2027
  • Metric: METR time horizon (50th percentile)
  • Threshold: 168 hours (1 week)
  • Date: December 31, 2027
  • Data source: metr.org/time-horizons
  • Current trajectory: 14.5 hours (Feb 2026) with 4-month doubling time. Projects to ~1 week by late 2026, so this prediction allows for significant deceleration.
  • Confidence: 70%
Prediction 3: AI-authored paper at a top-5 ML venue by December 2028
  • Metric: A paper primarily authored by an AI system (with minimal human guidance beyond problem selection) accepted at NeurIPS, ICML, ICLR, CVPR, or ACL main conference (not workshop)
  • Threshold: Acceptance at main conference
  • Date: December 31, 2028
  • Data source: Conference proceedings
  • Current trajectory: AI Scientist passed workshop review (2024). Erdos disproof exceeds main-conference quality (2026). The gap is in format/presentation, not capability.
  • Confidence: 60%
Prediction 4: Hyperscaler AI capex exceeds $1T in a single year by 2028
  • Metric: Combined capital expenditure by Amazon, Alphabet, Meta, Microsoft, and Oracle
  • Threshold: $1 trillion USD
  • Date: Full-year 2028 (reported in Q1 2029 earnings)
  • Data source: Company earnings reports
  • Current trajectory: $725B confirmed Q1 2026. CNBC projects $1T+ by 2027 (CNBC). Prediction allows extra year.
  • Confidence: 80%
Prediction 5: FrontierMath Tier 4 > 60% by December 2028
  • Metric: Best score on FrontierMath Tier 4 (research-level mathematics)
  • Threshold: 60% accuracy
  • Date: December 31, 2028
  • Data source: Epoch AI FrontierMath leaderboard
  • Current trajectory: <2% (Nov 2024) -> >30% (Mar 2026). Even with substantial deceleration, projects to 60%+ by late 2027.
  • Confidence: 70%
Aggregate Assessment

If 4+ of 5 predictions succeed, the optimist case is strongly validated and the synthesis should revise to a median BCP of 2030 or earlier. If 2-3 succeed, the case is partially validated and the synthesis should revise to 2031-2033. If 0-1 succeed, the optimist case is substantially weakened and the synthesis’s 2036 may be approximately correct.

13. Conclusion: Why 2036 Is Too Late

The synthesis’s BCP median of 2036 requires a sustained deceleration for which there is no current evidence. Specifically, it requires:

  1. Benchmark improvement rates to drop by ~60% from current levels – HLE would need to stall near 50-60% for several years, FrontierMath Tier 4 would need to plateau below 50%, and METR doubling times would need to revert to 12+ months.

  2. The compute flywheel to break – either revenue growth collapses (contradicted by $44B ARR), investment retreats (contradicted by $725B capex), or hardware improvement stalls (contradicted by Rubin’s 5x).

  3. The mathematical research results to be flukes – the Erdos disproof, Aletheia’s open-problem solutions, and AlphaEvolve’s algorithmic discoveries would all need to represent dead ends rather than the beginning of AI-assisted research capability.

  4. The expert consensus (Metaculus 2028-2032, Morgan Stanley 2026, AI 2027 revised to 2029-2030) to be systematically wrong by 4-8 years – and wrong in the same direction, despite having different methodologies and incentive structures.

  5. The historical base rate of “this requires a new paradigm” claims to break – every previous such claim was overturned within 2-3 years, but the next one would need to hold for 10+ years.

Any one of these could happen. All five happening simultaneously is unlikely. The optimist estimate of 2030 (70% CI: 2028-2033) is the more parsimonious reading of the available evidence.

The deepest question is not whether AI reaches broad cognitive parity but how quickly the remaining gaps close once AI systems begin contributing to AI research itself. The Erdos disproof, Aletheia’s results, and AlphaEvolve’s algorithmic discoveries suggest this recursive loop is beginning to activate. If it does, the relevant doubling times may shorten further, making even the optimist estimate conservative.

The synthesis itself provides the strongest evidence for this conclusion through its own updating pattern: 5 of 8 cruxes shifted toward S in Round 2, with the overall direction consistently favoring shorter timelines as more data arrived. If this directional pattern continues for another 2-3 revision cycles, the synthesis will converge to approximately the optimist position presented here.

13.1 What Would Change This Estimate

Intellectual honesty requires stating what evidence would push the optimist toward the synthesis’s 2036 estimate. Specifically:

  • HLE stalling below 50% for 12+ months (suggesting a genuine capability ceiling, not just benchmark difficulty)
  • METR doubling time reverting to 12+ months (suggesting the 4-month acceleration was noise)
  • Hyperscaler capex declining by 30%+ (suggesting the economic flywheel is breaking)
  • No further autonomous mathematical discoveries in 2026-2027 (suggesting the Erdos result was a one-off)
  • Enterprise AI revenue growth decelerating to <50% year-over-year (suggesting deployment is hitting practical limits)

If three or more of these conditions obtain by December 2027, the 2036 median becomes more defensible. As of May 2026, none of them are obtaining. The data continues to move in the optimist direction across every measurable dimension.
