Weighted calculation: 0.12(2031) + 0.38(2036) + 0.28(2042) + 0.14(2042) + 0.04(2044) + 0.04(2029) = ~2037.6, which I round to 2038 given the upward pressure from serial dependency and error compounding arguments.

6. Updated Falsifiable Predictions

I revise several predictions from the synthesis to reflect the pessimist’s partial persuasion:

Prediction 1: METR 80% Time Horizon

Original synthesis threshold: 80% horizon >24 hours supports fast timeline. Updated: I now expect the 80% horizon to reach 1-4 hours by December 2027 (previously expected 2-8 hours). If it remains below 1 hour, this strongly supports the pessimist. If it exceeds 4 hours, the pessimist’s error compounding argument is weakened.

Prediction 2: ARC-AGI-3

Original synthesis threshold: >25% supports Claim S by December 2027. Updated: No change. The pessimist’s argument does not provide new mechanistic evidence about ARC-AGI-3’s solvability. I still expect this to be a key discriminator.

Prediction 3: Enterprise AI Success Rates

Updated expectation: I now expect the coding tool success rate to reach 60%+ by 2028 while the general enterprise success rate remains below 30%. This domain-split prediction captures both the synthesis’s disaggregation insight and the pessimist’s point about increasing failure rates for ambitious deployments.

Prediction 4: Hyperscaler Capex

Updated: I now assign ~45% probability (up from ~35%) that capex growth decelerates in 2027 vs. 2026, reflecting the Goldman Sachs and BlackRock warnings and the enterprise ROI gap.

Prediction 5: Infrastructure Delivery

New prediction (inspired by pessimist): U.S. AI data center capacity will fall short of announced targets by at least 30% through 2028 due to grid interconnection delays and substation transformer lead times.

Prediction 6: Hallucination Rates

I agree with the pessimist’s prediction that best-in-class hallucination rates will remain above 2% through 2028 (~75% confident, vs. pessimist’s 90%). I disagree about the trajectory: I expect continued improvement toward ~2-3% by 2028, with sub-1% achievable by 2030.

Prediction 7: AI-Authored Research

I partially adopt the pessimist’s operationalized claim: if AI-authored papers at top-3 ML venues remain below 5% by 2028, this supports the pessimist’s view on research automation speed. I assign ~55% probability to this prediction holding (the pessimist’s threshold of 5% is reasonable but may be exceeded through human-AI collaboration that blurs authorship lines).

7. What Would Change My Mind Further

Toward the pessimist (further extending timelines):

  1. METR 80% horizon stalling below 1 hour through mid-2028. This would confirm that error compounding is a structural barrier that current approaches cannot overcome, and would push my BCP median toward 2040-2042.

  2. Hyperscaler capex flat or declining in 2027 (inflation-adjusted). This would confirm Scenario D (investment contraction) deserves 20%+ weight, compressing the compute trajectory and extending all timelines by 2-4 years.

  3. ARC-AGI-3 scores remaining below 5% for frontier models through 2028. This would confirm the bounded/open-ended gap is structural and that current architectures cannot address open-ended reasoning, pushing the BCP median toward 2042-2045.

  4. Mechanistic interpretability work revealing that frontier model “reasoning” is fundamentally pattern-matching coordination (not verified computational circuits). This would resolve Crux 5 toward L and cascade through Cruxes 3 and 8.

  5. A second major researcher (comparable to LeCun or Sutskever) publicly abandoning current approaches and founding a well-funded alternative. The pattern of top researchers leaving would become harder to dismiss.

Toward the optimist (compressing timelines back):

  1. ARC-AGI-3 scores exceeding 20% for frontier models within 12 months. This would break the “approximately zero progress” pattern the pessimist identifies and suggest current approaches are more capable than either the synthesis or the pessimist believes.

  2. METR 80% horizon exceeding 8 hours by mid-2028. This would falsify the error compounding barrier argument and suggest that metacognition gaps are being solved through engineering rather than requiring architectural breakthroughs.

  3. An AI system autonomously discovering a significant algorithmic improvement (meeting the synthesis’s Crux 3 criteria) within the next 12 months. This would resolve the ideation-execution gap debate and provide evidence for near-term recursive improvement.

  4. Enterprise AI success rates (general category, not just coding) exceeding 40% in a reputable large-scale survey by 2028. This would suggest the lab-to-production gap is narrowing faster than either the synthesis or pessimist expects.

  5. HLE scores exceeding 80% by mid-2027. This would confirm that effective compute scaling (pre-training + post-training + inference) remains on a steep trajectory, countering the diminishing returns argument.

8. Overall Assessment

The pessimist’s case is well-constructed and contains several arguments that the synthesis underweights. The error compounding math, the serial dependency chain, the self-driving analogy, and the infrastructure delivery gap are all genuine contributions that shift my estimates.

However, the pessimist’s case also has significant weaknesses:

  1. It does not engage with counter-evidence. The RRB findings on frontier model robustness, the HLE trajectory, the coding tool revenue data, and the AlphaEvolve results are either ignored or addressed superficially.

  2. It conflates bounded task performance with BCP distance. The pessimist repeatedly cites AIME/GPQA saturation as “measuring the easy part” – but these saturated benchmarks were considered state-of-the-art challenges only 2-3 years ago. The goalposts move continuously, and the pessimist’s argument requires assuming that the current hard benchmarks are qualitatively different from the previous “hard” benchmarks, which have a poor track record.

  3. It overweights the historical base rate. A 70-year base rate of overoptimism is informative but should be updated by the specific evidence of current capabilities. The pessimist applies the base rate as a multiplier on the optimist timeline rather than as a prior to be updated by evidence.

  4. It treats the bounded/open-ended gap as static. The pessimist’s strongest argument is the bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern. But the boundary between “bounded” and “open-ended” has been shifting outward as scaffolding, tool use, and test-time compute expand what current architectures can handle. The question is the rate of boundary shift, and the pessimist assumes near-zero rate without providing evidence for this assumption beyond the current snapshot.

  5. It underweights the multi-axis scaling thesis. Dismissing each new scaling axis as a “one-time paradigm shift” ignores that we may be in the early stages of accumulating such shifts. Pre-training was the first axis. RLHF was the second. Inference-time compute is the third. Each was a one-time shift, but their cumulative effect has been transformative. The pessimist provides no argument for why the sequence of one-time shifts is exhausted rather than ongoing.

My updated position remains closer to the synthesis than to the pessimist, but with a meaningful shift: BCP median moves from 2036 to 2038, reflecting primarily the error compounding/reliability challenge, the serial dependency structure, and infrastructure delivery risks. The NCP estimate is unchanged at 2029.

The gap between my updated median (2038) and the pessimist’s median (2045) is substantial – roughly 7 years. The key empirical tests that will resolve this are: (1) the METR 80% time horizon trajectory through 2027-2028, (2) ARC-AGI-3 progress, (3) enterprise AI success rates disaggregated by domain, and (4) whether hyperscaler capex growth sustains through 2027.

This reconciliation was produced in isolation, having seen only the original synthesis and the pessimist’s argument. It has not been influenced by the optimist’s case.

Reconciliation: Optimist Argument Assessment

May 2026 – Isolated reconciliation after reviewing the optimist case

1. Summary of Key Updates

After carefully reviewing the optimist argument against the original synthesis, I update my timeline estimates as follows:

  • NCP (Narrow Cognitive Parity, formerly NTS) median: 2029 -> 2028 (modest shift)
  • BCP median: 2036 -> 2033 (significant shift, ~3 years)

The optimist’s strongest contributions are (1) the Erdos conjecture disproof and Aletheia results, which constitute genuinely new evidence not available when the synthesis was written and which directly address the weakest point in the pessimist position; (2) the revenue validation data ($44B ARR), which substantially de-risks the investment contraction scenario; and (3) the benchmark velocity compression argument, which has strong empirical backing.

However, the optimist overreaches in several areas: treating the Erdos result as resolving the entire architecture debate, conflating multiple scaling axes as independent when they share common bottlenecks, and systematically underweighting the reliability gap (27-minute 80% horizon vs. 14.5-hour 50% horizon). The optimist’s probability assignments are too confident on 7 of 9 cruxes assessed, and the claimed BCP median of 2030 relies on optimistic readings of ambiguous evidence at multiple points simultaneously.

The net effect: the optimist moves me substantially but not to their position. The most important updates are on Cruxes 3, 7, and the scenario weights (particularly the Investment Contraction scenario, which I now consider less likely).

2. Crux-by-Crux Assessment

Crux 1: Test-Time Compute Scaling Ceiling

Original position: 55% Claim S (test-time compute continues yielding gains through 2028)

Optimist argument: Shifts to 70% S, citing Rubin’s 10x cost reduction, the 8B model reaching 94.5% on HMMT 2025 via 2M token chains, and inference cost declining at 10x/year (Tom’s Hardware; Introl).

Assessment: The optimist’s evidence is real but mostly about economics of test-time compute (cheaper inference), not about whether test-time compute continues yielding capability gains at the frontier. The crux asks whether more inference compute produces capability improvements, not whether it gets cheaper. Cost reduction is relevant because it enables more compute to be applied, but the saturation evidence cited in the synthesis – that performance gains from additional compute saturate after a model-specific threshold (Emergent Mind) – is not addressed by cheaper hardware.

The 8B/HMMT result is genuinely impressive but tests bounded math competition performance, not the open-ended reasoning that constitutes the main bottleneck. ARC-AGI-3 remains at <1% despite abundant inference budgets.

I find the cost reduction argument partially compelling – if you can apply 10x more compute per query, some problems that were marginal become solvable. But the key question is whether test-time compute can cross qualitative barriers (interactive environments, multi-day autonomy), not just push further along existing capability curves.

Updated position: 55% -> 58% Claim S. Modest update; the hardware improvements are real but the crux is about capability ceilings, not cost floors.

Crux 2: METR Time Horizon Extrapolation

Original position: 45% Claim S (trend continues, reaching 1-week/1-month horizons)

Optimist argument: Shifts to 60% S, citing the 4-month doubling time (accelerating from 7 months), SWE-bench 93.9%, and 84% developer adoption (METR; Epoch AI).

Assessment: The optimist correctly notes that the doubling time has shortened (7 months -> 4 months) and that this is significant. However, the optimist’s extrapolation of the 50% horizon to 1-week autonomy by late 2026 and 1-month by mid-2027 is speculative, and the argument conspicuously omits the 80% horizon data.

The synthesis made the 80% horizon the primary metric for good reason: a system that succeeds on 50% of week-long tasks but only 80% of 27-minute tasks is not reliably autonomous. The optimist discusses only the 50% horizon and never engages with the 10x gap between the 50% and 80% metrics (14.5 hours vs. 27 minutes). This is a significant omission because the gap between these metrics reveals the reliability distribution: the system is occasionally brilliant but consistently mediocre. The 80%/50% ratio determines whether autonomous deployment is economically viable (you cannot deploy a system that fails 50% of the time on important tasks).

The SWE-bench and developer adoption data are relevant but tangential – these are bounded tasks (individual code completions, pull requests), not sustained multi-day autonomy.

I do update somewhat because the accelerating doubling time is a real signal, and the METR task suite limitations (228 tasks, ceiling near 16 hours) mean the current data may understate capability. But I reject the optimist’s extrapolation as overconfident.

Updated position: 45% -> 48% Claim S. Small update; the 80% horizon gap remains the binding concern.

Crux 3: AI-Automated AI Research

Original position: 35% Claim S (AI autonomously discovers significant ML algorithmic improvement by 2028)

Optimist argument: Shifts to 55% S, citing the Erdos conjecture disproof (ExplainX; Dataconomy), Aletheia’s resolution of open mathematical problems (MarkTechPost; InfoQ), and AlphaEvolve’s Strassen improvement.

Assessment: This is the optimist’s strongest argument, and it represents genuinely new evidence that was not available when the synthesis was written.

The Erdos disproof is significant. The synthesis set the threshold as binary – “such a result exists or does not” – and stated that “no AI system has autonomously discovered a significant ML breakthrough to date (May 2026).” The Erdos result is not an ML breakthrough, but the synthesis also noted that “a non-ML discovery… that demonstrates the same autonomous capability counts as partial evidence for Claim S.” An 80-year-old conjecture disproved autonomously, verified by Fields-level mathematicians, is strong partial evidence.

I find the optimist’s characterization of the Erdos result as “the single most important piece of evidence in the entire timelines debate” somewhat overblown – it is one result in one domain (pure mathematics, where problems are precisely stated and solutions are verifiable). Whether this transfers to the messier world of ML research, where problems are ill-defined and validation requires expensive experiments, is uncertain. The ideation-execution gap (arXiv:2506.20803) remains directly relevant: mathematical proofs are self-verifying in a way that ML experiments are not.

Aletheia’s results (63 technically appropriate solutions out of 700 open problems, 4 fully resolved) add important corroboration. This is not a single lucky result; it is a systematic capability.

However, I note the optimist’s framing elides an important distinction. The synthesis crux specifically asks about “significant ML algorithmic improvement… adopted in production by at least one frontier lab.” Mathematical discoveries, however impressive, are not ML algorithmic improvements. The FlashAttention speedup (23%) is closer to the threshold, but it was achieved through evolutionary code search over a well-defined optimization surface, which is more optimization than invention. The gap between “search-based optimization of known algorithms” and “conceptual innovation in ML” may be smaller than I originally assessed – but it has not been closed.

Updated position: 35% -> 48% Claim S. Substantial update. The Erdos and Aletheia results shift the prior significantly, but I stop short of 50% because (a) the crux specifically requires ML breakthroughs, not mathematical ones, (b) the ideation-execution gap remains unaddressed, and (c) the gap between mathematical proof (self-verifying) and ML research (requires expensive experimentation) is real.

Crux 4: Pre-Training Scaling Returns

Original position: 50% Claim S (scaling from 5e26 to 1e28+ FLOP produces GPT-3-to-GPT-4-magnitude gains)

Optimist argument: Shifts to 65% S, citing Morgan Stanley’s validation that scaling laws are “holding firm” (Fortune), six independent labs converging at the frontier (TeamDay), and 15x/year effective compute growth (Epoch AI).

Assessment: The optimist presents three distinct arguments here, of varying quality:

  1. Morgan Stanley validation: Moderately compelling. Morgan Stanley has low COI (making investment calls with fiduciary duty), and their finding that “10x compute input yields approximately 2x intelligence output” is a specific, testable claim. However, “intelligence output” via GDPVal is itself a somewhat fuzzy metric, and a 2x return per 10x input is itself a statement about diminishing returns – it just says the returns are still positive, not that they are large.

  2. Six converging labs: This is evidence for scaling law robustness (the recipe works across organizations), not evidence against diminishing returns. All six labs could be on the same diminishing-returns curve. Convergence tells us the methods are reproducible; it does not tell us the slope of the curve going forward.

  3. 15x/year effective compute growth: This is the compounding of hardware (5x/year), algorithmic efficiency (3x/year), and test-time compute. The optimist treats these as independently contributing to a 15x multiplier. But these factors interact: algorithmic efficiency gains are partially how hardware gains translate to capability, not an independent source. Treating them as fully multiplicative overstates the compound growth rate.

The HLE trajectory (8% -> 45-65% in 16 months) is the strongest empirical evidence for continued returns. The optimist’s extrapolation to 80%+ by late 2027 is plausible if the current rate continues but requires that HLE difficulty scales roughly linearly with score, which is not established. The last 20% (from 80% to saturation) of benchmarks is typically harder than the first 80%, as the remaining questions are the genuinely hardest.

Updated position: 50% -> 53% Claim S. Modest update. Morgan Stanley’s assessment is useful low-COI evidence, but the optimist’s framing of “15x/year effective compute” as if it refutes diminishing returns conflates compute availability with capability returns.

Crux 5: The Generalization-vs-Memorization Question

Original position: 50/50

Optimist argument: Not directly addressed as a separate crux, but the Erdos disproof and Aletheia results are cited as evidence for genuine reasoning.

Assessment: The Erdos result is relevant here. If a system can discover a mathematical construction that no human found in 80 years, it is doing something beyond pattern matching on training data – the result was by definition not in the training distribution. This is meaningful evidence for the S claim.

However, this evidence is domain-specific. A system can exhibit genuine reasoning in mathematics (where problems have precise formulations and solutions are verifiable) while remaining a sophisticated pattern matcher in other domains (social reasoning, physical intuition, open-ended judgment). The RoR-Bench results (60% accuracy drops on elementary math with single phrase changes, arXiv:2504.00509) and the analog clock problem (50.1% for IMO-gold-level models, Stanford AI Index 2026) are not explained away by the Erdos result.

The most accurate model is probably: frontier models do genuine reasoning in some domains (especially those with formal verification like mathematics and code) and sophisticated pattern matching in others (physical intuition, social reasoning, common-sense). The ratio varies by domain, and the Erdos result shifts the boundary somewhat toward “more genuine reasoning than I thought” without eliminating the pattern-matching component.

Updated position: 50% -> 53% Claim S. Small update reflecting the Erdos/Aletheia evidence for genuine novel reasoning.

Crux 6: Compute Scaling Continuation

Original position: 65% Claim S (training runs reach 1e28+ FLOP by 2028)

Optimist argument: Shifts to 80% S, citing $725B confirmed capex in Q1 2026 (Epoch AI), Rubin’s 5x performance improvement (Tom’s Hardware), and Goldman Sachs projecting $1.15T cumulative capex 2025-2027 (Goldman Sachs).

Assessment: The capex numbers are impressive and represent confirmed commitments, not projections. $725B in 2026 exceeds the synthesis’s cited figure of $660-690B. This is meaningful: the capital is flowing faster than expected.

However, the optimist’s 80% is too high. The synthesis already assessed this at 65% S, incorporating the massive capital commitments. The incremental evidence ($725B vs. $690B) is a quantitative refinement, not a qualitative shift. The binding constraints identified in the synthesis – grid interconnection delays (median 5 years), CCGT lead times (5-7 years), and the 94% of operating cash flows consumed by capex – are not addressed by the optimist. More money does not solve physics problems faster.

The Rubin specifications are impressive but represent manufacturer claims about a product shipping in 2H 2026. Manufacturer pre-release specifications are systematically optimistic; real-world performance typically falls 10-30% below claimed specifications. And Rubin’s impact on training runs (which is what this crux measures) is less direct than its impact on inference.

I update modestly because the confirmed capex trajectory exceeds prior projections, suggesting the investment contraction risk (Scenario D) is lower than originally assessed.

Updated position: 65% -> 70% Claim S. Modest update reflecting stronger-than-expected capex commitments and Rubin specifications, tempered by infrastructure bottlenecks remaining unaddressed.

Crux 7: The Enterprise Deployment Gap

Original position: 50% Claim S (enterprise failure rates drop below 50% by 2028)

Optimist argument: Shifts to 70% S, citing Anthropic’s $44B ARR (MindStudio), 84% developer adoption (Panto), 70% Fortune 100 as Anthropic customers, and 41% AI-written code.

Assessment: This is the optimist’s second strongest argument after the Erdos result. The revenue data is particularly compelling because it is a market signal, not a benchmark score. $44B ARR represents real economic value being delivered to customers who keep paying.

Two critical observations:

  1. The revenue is real but concentrated. The optimist’s own data shows that coding tools are the primary revenue driver (Claude Code: $2.5B ARR in 9 months). The “84% developer adoption” and “41% AI-written code” figures are about coding specifically. The synthesis correctly noted that enterprise success is domain-dependent: coding tools work, but general enterprise AI projects still fail at 80%+ rates (Folio3). The optimist’s framing treats coding-tool success as evidence for broad enterprise success, which is a domain-transfer assumption.

  2. Revenue sustains investment, which is what matters for this crux. Even if AI revenue comes primarily from coding tools and a few other niches, $44B ARR (and growing at $96M/day) is sufficient to sustain the compute buildout. The synthesis already noted that “revenue from high-performing niches may be sufficient to sustain investment even without broad enterprise success.” The optimist’s data confirms this.

The stronger update is on the Investment Contraction scenario (D). If Anthropic alone has $44B ARR, the probability that the entire AI investment cycle collapses due to revenue disappointment is substantially lower than I assessed. This is not evidence that AI will succeed in all enterprise applications – it is evidence that the economic foundation of AI development is more robust than feared.

Updated position: 50% -> 58% Claim S. Meaningful update, primarily because the revenue data de-risks the investment-contraction scenario. But I reject the optimist’s 70% because the enterprise success data remains domain-specific (coding-heavy), and the crux asks about broad enterprise deployment success rates.

Crux 8: Architectural Sufficiency

Original position: 50% Claim S (transformers + extensions are sufficient for AGI)

Optimist argument: Shifts to 65% S, citing the Erdos disproof as evidence that transformers can do open-ended reasoning, and the historical base rate of “paradigm shift needed” claims being overturned within 2-3 years.

Assessment: The optimist’s argument has two components, and I evaluate them differently:

  1. The Erdos disproof demonstrates open-ended reasoning within transformers: Partially compelling. The Erdos result shows that current architectures can discover novel mathematical constructions. This is meaningful evidence that transformers can do some open-ended reasoning. But “open-ended reasoning in formally verifiable domains” is different from the full scope of what this crux covers: physical grounding, causal reasoning, persistent memory, sustained autonomy, and world models. Solving a pure mathematics problem (where the problem is precisely stated and the solution is self-verifying) does not demonstrate that transformers can handle interactive environments (ARC-AGI-3: 0.37%), physical reasoning (Escher-Bench: 57.3% vs. 90.6%), or sustained autonomy (80% horizon: 27 minutes). The optimist is generalizing from the domain where transformers perform best (formal reasoning) to domains where they perform worst (embodied, interactive, sustained).

  2. Historical base rate of “paradigm” claims being overturned: This is a stronger argument. The synthesis itself acknowledges this pattern. The track record of “LLMs can’t do X” claims is poor. However, the optimist’s version – listing six “can’t” claims that were overturned and asserting “the burden of proof should now be on anyone claiming the next ‘can’t’ is the real one” – proves too much. By this logic, no architectural limitation could ever be credibly claimed, because previous limitations were eventually overcome. But some limitations are real (e.g., no neural network has learned to do something it was never exposed to in any form during training). The question is whether the current limitations (interactive environments, sustained autonomy, physical reasoning) are of the “will be overcome soon” type or the “requires genuine innovation” type.

I find the base-rate argument partially compelling: maybe 2 out of 3 “can’t” claims get overturned. This shifts the balance but doesn’t resolve it. The key signal will be ARC-AGI-3 (interactive environments) and METR 80% horizon (sustained autonomy). If these show progress within current architectures over the next 12 months, the architectural sufficiency claim strengthens dramatically. If they remain stuck, it weakens.

Updated position: 50% -> 53% Claim S. The Erdos result provides genuine evidence of open-ended reasoning capability, but it is in the domain most favorable to transformers (formal mathematics). The hard gaps remain: interactive environments, sustained autonomy, physical reasoning. I weight the base-rate argument at ~1/3 of what the optimist claims.

Crux 9: Recursive Improvement Speed

Original position: 30% Claim S (fast, >10x acceleration within 2 years)

Optimist argument: Not assessed as a separate crux by the optimist, but the recursive improvement argument is woven throughout (Section 9.5). The optimist argues the loop is “already partially active” given that 41% of code is AI-written and AlphaEvolve has improved AI infrastructure.

Assessment: The optimist conflates AI being used in AI development (which is clearly happening) with AI accelerating the rate of improvement (which is the crux). The fact that 41% of code is AI-written means AI is a productivity tool for developers, not that it is autonomously accelerating algorithmic progress. Similarly, AlphaEvolve improving FlashAttention by 23% is a one-time optimization, not evidence of an accelerating feedback loop.

The crux asks whether the acceleration rate exceeds 1 OOM/year (vs. current ~0.5 OOM/year) for a sustained 12-month period. The optimist provides no evidence that the algorithmic efficiency improvement rate has increased. The 3x/year figure (Epoch AI) cited by the optimist is the current rate, not an accelerated one.

The key bottleneck remains: verification. Even if AI can generate candidate improvements rapidly, each candidate must be validated through expensive training runs. This serial bottleneck limits the speed of the feedback loop regardless of how fast candidates are generated. The optimist does not address this.

Updated position: 30% -> 32% Claim S. Negligible update. The optimist provides enthusiasm but no evidence that the recursive loop is accelerating beyond the current rate.

Crux 10: Regulatory Disruption

Original position: 70% Claim S (light regulation)

Optimist argument: 70% S, no change requested.

Assessment: Agree with the optimist that no update is needed. The regulatory environment remains permissive. No new evidence presented.

Updated position: 70% Claim S. Unchanged.

Crux 11: Embodiment Requirement

Original position: 55% Claim S (embodiment not required)

Optimist argument: Shifts to 60% S, citing coding/math/science progress confirming the “cognitive-first” path.

Assessment: The optimist’s argument is brief and uncontroversial. Continued progress in disembodied cognitive domains is consistent with, but does not strongly update, the embodiment question. The hard evidence remains: Escher-Bench progress is slow, ARC-AGI-3 (which tests interaction with novel environments) is at 0.37%, and the analog clock problem persists.

The optimist is correct that most economically relevant cognitive tasks do not require embodiment. This is already reflected in the synthesis’s 55% S assessment. The additional evidence (more coding/math progress) is incremental.

Updated position: 55% -> 56% Claim S. Negligible update.

Crux 12: AGI Definition

Original position: Framing crux, not probabilistic.

Optimist argument: The optimist uses “BCP” (matching median human performance across economically relevant cognitive domains by 2030), which is approximately the synthesis’s Tier B but with somewhat looser criteria (median rather than college-educated, economically relevant rather than all unstructured tasks).

Assessment: I note that the optimist’s definition of BCP is slightly less demanding than the synthesis’s. “Economically relevant cognitive domains” excludes some of the hardest gaps (social reasoning, aesthetic judgment, moral reasoning under uncertainty). And “median human performance” is a lower bar than “reliably replace a college-educated knowledge worker.” This definitional loosening accounts for some of the optimist’s earlier timeline – perhaps 1-2 years of the 6-year gap between the optimist’s 2030 and the synthesis’s 2036.

For the remainder of this reconciliation, I use the synthesis’s stricter BCP definition to maintain consistency.

Updated position: Framing crux; slight definitional tightening to prefer the synthesis’s more demanding version.

3. Arguments I Found Compelling

3.1 The Erdos Conjecture Disproof (Strong Update)

This is genuinely new evidence of the highest importance for Crux 3. An autonomous system discovering a mathematical construction that eluded professional mathematicians for 80 years, verified by Fields-level mathematicians (ExplainX; Dataconomy), directly addresses the strongest pessimist objection: that AI can optimize within known frameworks but cannot produce novel intellectual contributions.

The optimist is right that this is not “optimization over a known search space” – the model identified a non-obvious connection between algebraic number theory and combinatorial geometry. This is the kind of creative cross-domain synthesis that was supposed to be beyond current architectures.

I weight this heavily because it meets the synthesis’s own criteria for partial evidence on Crux 3 (“a non-ML discovery… that demonstrates the same autonomous capability counts as partial evidence for Claim S”). I do not weight it as heavily as the optimist does because (a) the synthesis asks specifically about ML algorithmic improvements, not mathematics, and (b) mathematical proofs are self-verifying in a way that ML research is not, so transfer from one domain to the other is uncertain.

3.2 Aletheia’s Systematic Open-Problem Results (Strong Update)

Aletheia solving 63 technically appropriate solutions out of 700 open problems, with 4 fully resolved open questions (MarkTechPost), is important because it demonstrates repeatability. The Erdos disproof could be dismissed as a lucky outlier; 63 solutions across 700 problems cannot. This shifts from “maybe AI got lucky once” to “AI has a systematic capability for open mathematical research, albeit with a low hit rate.”

The FirstProof results (6/10 on novel problems designed to require genuine research insight) are particularly significant because the problems were designed after the model was trained, reducing contamination concerns.

3.3 Revenue Validation De-Risking the Investment Contraction Scenario (Moderate Update)

The $44B ARR figure (MindStudio) for Anthropic alone, growing at $96M/day, substantially reduces the probability of Scenario D (Investment Contraction). The synthesis assigned 10% weight to this scenario, which assumed enterprise disappointment triggering capex moderation. With one company alone generating $44B ARR, the “revenue doesn’t exist” leg of the investment contraction thesis is refuted.

This does not mean the capex trajectory is guaranteed – grid constraints and infrastructure bottlenecks are physical, not financial. But it means the financial motivation for continued investment is robust. I reduce the weight of Scenario D from 10% to 5%.

3.4 Benchmark Velocity Compression (Moderate Update)

The optimist’s table showing time-to-saturation compressing (MMLU: 4 years, HumanEval: 3 years, GPQA Diamond: 2 years, ARC-AGI-2: 1 year, AIME 2025: <1 year) is empirically well-documented. This is a genuinely informative meta-pattern that the synthesis should have weighted more heavily.

The implication is that the current “hard” benchmarks (HLE, FrontierMath) will likely saturate faster than naive extrapolation from their current difficulty levels would suggest. If the compression pattern holds, saturation by 2028-2029 is more likely than the synthesis’s implicit assumption of 2030+.

However, I note a selection effect: the benchmarks cited are predominantly bounded, well-specified tasks. The compression pattern may not apply to the metrics that matter most for BCP (METR 80% horizon, ARC-AGI-3, enterprise success rates), which test sustained, open-ended capability.

3.5 The COI-Weighted Consensus (Moderate Update)

The optimist’s observation that low-COI forecasters (Metaculus, Morgan Stanley, Bengio) cluster at 2028-2033 is well-taken. The synthesis acknowledges that “least conflicted sources… cluster around 2030-2035” but then places the BCP median at 2036, which is at the pessimistic end of even the low-COI range. The optimist correctly identifies this as an inconsistency.

The directional consensus argument is also compelling: nearly every major forecaster has revised their estimates earlier over the past 2-3 years. Metaculus moving from ~2045 (2022) to 2032 (2026) is a 13-year shift (Metaculus). This systematic directional pattern across forecasters with different methodologies and incentive structures is informative.

4. Arguments I Rejected

4.1 The Independence of Scaling Axes (Rejected)

The optimist argues that five partially independent pathways (pretraining, test-time compute, algorithmic efficiency, hardware, recursive self-improvement) make it unlikely that all stall simultaneously, calculating: “if we assign a 40% chance to each axis stalling independently, the probability of all five stalling simultaneously is 0.4^5 = 1%.”

This calculation is misleading. The axes are not independent:

  • Algorithmic efficiency and hardware improvement are how pretraining scaling works. They are not separate from pretraining scaling; they are its components. Counting them as independent axes and then also counting pretraining is double-counting.
  • Test-time compute is subject to diminishing returns (Emergent Mind) that are partially correlated with pretraining diminishing returns – both reflect the underlying difficulty of tasks.
  • Recursive self-improvement is conditional on the other axes working (it requires AI systems to be good enough to do research, which requires the other axes to continue). It is not independent of them.

A more realistic independence estimate: ~2-3 partially independent axes (hardware improvements, algorithmic innovations, and a partially independent test-time compute effect), with recursive improvement as a conditional amplifier. The probability of stalling would be perhaps 10-15%, not 1%.

4.2 The Erdos Result as Resolving the Architecture Debate (Rejected)

The optimist claims the Erdos disproof “falsifies” the position that transformers cannot do open-ended reasoning. This is too strong. The Erdos result demonstrates that transformers can do open-ended reasoning in formally verifiable domains where the problem is precisely stated. It does not address:

  • Interactive environments requiring exploration without stated goals (ARC-AGI-3: 0.37%, arcprize.org)
  • Sustained autonomous operation over days/weeks (80% horizon: 27 minutes, METR)
  • Physical/spatial reasoning requiring embodied intuition (Escher-Bench: 57.3% vs. 90.6%, arXiv:2601.01547)
  • Metacognitive self-regulation (MIRROR: compositional calibration error near random, arXiv:2604.19809)

The bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern is not overturned by a result in the most favorable domain (formal mathematics). It would be overturned by results in the unfavorable domains (interactive environments, sustained autonomy, physical reasoning). Those results have not materialized.

4.3 Revenue Growth as Proof Against Bubble Risk (Partially Rejected)

The optimist argues that $44B ARR and 84% developer adoption prove AI is not a bubble. The revenue is real, but this argument conflates two distinct claims:

  1. AI coding tools are economically valuable – clearly true, well-evidenced.
  2. The $725B/year capex is proportionate to the revenue opportunity – much less clear. $44B ARR for the entire industry’s breakout product (coding tools) supports perhaps $100-200B in investment, not $725B. The gap between current AI revenue and current AI investment is enormous, and the optimist’s appeal to $15.7T GDP opportunity (WEF) is a projection about future value, not evidence of current returns.

The dot-com comparison is more nuanced than the optimist allows. Some dot-com companies had real revenue (Amazon, eBay, Google); the bubble was about the ratio of investment to revenue, not the absence of revenue. The current ratio of AI investment (~$725B/year) to AI revenue (perhaps $100-150B/year across all companies) is roughly 5-7:1, which is high by historical standards for infrastructure investments.

I accept that the probability of total investment collapse is low (the revenue base is too solid), but the probability of capex moderation (say, 20-30% reduction from projected growth rates) remains meaningful. This is the difference between Scenario D (investment contraction, which I now find less likely) and a modified Scenario B (steady progress with somewhat slower compute scaling).

4.4 The “Historical Base Rate of ‘Can’t’ Claims” as Proof (Partially Rejected)

The optimist lists six “can’t” claims that were overturned and argues the burden of proof should be on anyone making the next one. This is a valid Bayesian update, but it proves less than the optimist thinks, for two reasons:

  1. Survivorship bias. We remember the “can’t” claims that were overturned. We do not as readily recall the capabilities that genuinely took decades to develop, or the ones where the specific mechanism of overcoming the limitation required genuinely new ideas (e.g., the transformer architecture itself, RLHF). Some “can’t” claims were right for extended periods – neural networks couldn’t do practical machine translation until attention mechanisms were developed, for example.

  2. The remaining gaps are qualitatively different. The overturned claims were mostly about bounded performance on well-specified tasks (arithmetic, multi-step reasoning, competition math, code generation). The remaining claims are about sustained, interactive, open-ended capability. The bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern suggests this is a different kind of challenge, and the base rate from bounded-task claims may not apply.

I weight the base-rate argument at approximately 1/3 of its face value: it tells us that “can’t” claims should be assigned maybe 60-70% probability rather than 90%+, but not that they should be assigned <30%.

4.5 METR Extrapolation Without the 80% Horizon (Rejected)

The optimist discusses METR time horizons extensively but uses only the 50% horizon for extrapolation. The 80% horizon (27 minutes vs. 14.5 hours for the 50%) is the more relevant metric for BCP, as the synthesis correctly argued. Ignoring it biases the extrapolation substantially.

If the 80% horizon doubles on the same schedule (every 4 months), it reaches ~1 week by late 2028 – but this extrapolation assumes the 80%/50% gap remains constant, which is uncertain. If the gap grows (as error compounding over longer horizons predicts), the 80% horizon could stall while the 50% continues improving.

4.6 The Asymmetric Risk Argument (Rejected as Irrelevant)

The optimist argues that the cost of being unprepared for early BCP exceeds the cost of over-preparing for late BCP, so rational planning should weight the optimist scenario more heavily. This may be correct as policy advice, but it is not evidence about when BCP will occur. This is a decision-theoretic argument, not an epistemic one. I am assessing probability distributions, not optimal policy responses. The probability of BCP should be assessed based on evidence, not on the costs of being wrong.

5. Updated Probability Distributions

5.1 Updated Scenario Weights

5.3 Narrow Cognitive Parity (NCP) Distribution

Definition: AI systems that exceed the best human expert on the majority of well-defined cognitive tasks when measured individually.

5.4 Broad Cognitive Parity (BCP) Distribution

Definition: AI systems that can reliably replace a college-educated knowledge worker across a wide range of unstructured tasks.

Percentile Original Updated Shift
10th 2028 2028 0
25th 2031 2030 -1
50th (median) 2036 2033 -3
75th 2042 2039 -3
90th 2045+ 2043 -2

Reasoning: The 3-year shift is driven by three main factors:

  1. Scenario D (Investment Contraction) reduced from 10% to 5%: This pulls the right tail in by 1-2 years.
  2. Scenario A (Fast Convergence) increased from 15% to 20%: This pulls the left tail out and shifts the median.
  3. Scenario F (Wild Card) increased from 5% to 8%: The Erdos/Aletheia results make the recursive improvement scenario slightly more plausible.
  4. Crux 3 shift (+13 pp): If AI research automation is more likely, the conditional timeline compression from the dependency chain shortens BCP by 1-2 years.

Weighted calculation: 0.20(2030.5) + 0.40(2034) + 0.22(2040) + 0.05(2041) + 0.05(2042) + 0.08(2029) = 2034.1

The 3-year shift is significant but much less than the optimist’s proposed 6-year shift (2036 -> 2030). The difference stems primarily from:

  • I do not accept the optimist’s probability assignments on Cruxes 1, 4, 6, and 8, finding them overconfident by 10-15 pp each
  • I weight the 80% horizon gap more heavily than the optimist, who ignores it
  • I do not treat the Erdos result as resolving the architecture debate for all domains
  • I apply more conservative extrapolation from the METR trend, accounting for the 50%/80% divergence
  • I discount the “independent axes” argument for the correlation reasons described above
5.5 What the Difference Represents

The 3-year shift from 2036 to 2033 is epistemically meaningful. It represents:

  • Moving from “roughly aligned with the pessimistic end of the low-COI forecast range” to “roughly aligned with the center of the low-COI forecast range”
  • Increasing the probability of BCP by 2030 from ~15% to ~25%
  • Increasing the probability of BCP by 2035 from ~45% to ~60%
  • Reducing the probability that BCP takes until 2040+ from ~30% to ~20%

The updated estimate is consistent with the Metaculus “full AGI” median of October 2032 and with Bengio’s “within a decade” framing. It is 3 years earlier than the synthesis but 3 years later than the optimist, which I consider appropriate given the genuinely new evidence the optimist presents while accounting for the overreaches described above.

6. Updated Falsifiable Predictions

6.1 Predictions That Would Move Me Toward the Optimist (2030)

  1. ARC-AGI-3 score >10% by December 2027. This would indicate that interactive reasoning – the weakest domain for current architectures – is yielding to current approaches. Data source: arcprize.org.

  2. METR 80% time horizon >8 hours by December 2027. This would indicate that reliability (not just peak performance) is improving exponentially. The 80% metric is critical because it measures consistent capability, not just occasional brilliance. Data source: metr.org.

  3. A second autonomous discovery of comparable significance to Erdos by December 2027. The Erdos result could be a one-off. A second result in a different domain (biology, materials science, or ML itself) would substantially increase my confidence in systematic autonomous research capability. Data source: peer-reviewed publications.

  4. HLE score >80% by December 2028. The synthesis’s Crux 4 uses this as its primary metric. Data source: agi.safe.ai.

  5. Algorithmic efficiency improvement rate exceeding 1 OOM/year for a sustained 12-month period by December 2028. This would indicate the recursive improvement loop is accelerating beyond the current baseline. Data source: Epoch AI algorithmic progress tracking.

If 3 or more of these obtain, I would shift BCP median to ~2030-2031.

6.2 Predictions That Would Move Me Back Toward the Synthesis (2036)

  1. METR 50% time horizon stalling below 48 hours for >12 months. If the doubling trend breaks, sustained autonomy is further away than either estimate suggests.

  2. Hyperscaler capex growth falling below 20% year-over-year in 2027. Financial contraction would slow compute scaling.

  3. No further autonomous mathematical/scientific discoveries in 2H 2026 or 2027. If the Erdos and Aletheia results do not generalize or repeat, the AI research automation case weakens.

  4. HLE score plateauing below 60% despite larger training runs. If benchmark improvement decelerates dramatically on the hardest tests, the pre-training scaling returns story is wrong.

  5. Enterprise AI project success rates (general category, excluding coding) remaining below 30% in 2027 surveys. If the enterprise gap persists outside coding, the “AI succeeds everywhere” thesis fails.

If 3 or more of these obtain, I would shift BCP median back to ~2035-2037.

7. What Would Change My Mind Further

Toward the Optimist (2029-2031)
  • ARC-AGI-3 making rapid progress (>25% by mid-2027) within current architectures. This is the hardest test of whether the bounded-good/open-ended-bad pattern is breaking down.
  • An AI system autonomously discovering a significant ML training improvement (not just inference optimization) that is adopted in production. This would resolve Crux 3 at the strict threshold.
  • The 80% METR horizon accelerating toward the 50% horizon, closing the reliability gap. If the 80% horizon shows the same doubling time as the 50%, the sustained-autonomy concern is addressed.
  • Evidence that the ideation-execution gap is closing: AI-generated research ideas scoring comparably to human ideas on post-execution metrics, not just pre-execution ones.
  • Enterprise AI success rates (general category) exceeding 40% in 2027 surveys, indicating that AI capability is translating to real-world value beyond coding.
Toward the Pessimist (2037+)
  • The pessimist argument (which I have not yet seen) presenting evidence that the Erdos result is less significant than it appears – for example, if the result was achieved through extensive human guidance that was downplayed, or if the construction was in some sense “adjacent” to existing work in ways that undermine the autonomy claim.
  • Evidence of systematic benchmark contamination or gaming in the recent results that would undermine the benchmark trajectory argument.
  • A major AI safety incident triggering regulatory restrictions (Crux 10 resolving L).
  • Capex contraction exceeding 30% in 2027, indicating the financial foundation is weaker than it appears.
  • Mechanistic interpretability research demonstrating that frontier model “reasoning” reduces to pattern-matching coordination even in the domains where it performs best (mathematics, coding). This would reinterpret the Erdos result as sophisticated search rather than genuine reasoning, and would weaken Cruxes 3, 5, and 8 simultaneously.
Key Open Questions

  1. How deep is the Erdos result? Is this a system that has developed a general capacity for mathematical research, or a system that got lucky on one problem with the right structure? The Aletheia results suggest the former, but we need more data points and more domains.

  2. Will the 80%/50% METR gap close or widen? This is the most important empirical question for BCP timing. If the gap closes (reliability improves toward peak performance), BCP is closer. If it widens (peak performance improves but reliability lags), BCP is further.

  3. Is the ideation-execution gap fundamental or contingent? The optimist does not address this gap at all, which I find concerning. If AI ideas consistently fail to survive contact with reality (as the ICLR 2026 finding suggests, arXiv:2506.20803), then even impressive benchmark results may not translate to the kind of autonomous research capability that drives recursive improvement.

  4. How robust is the capex trajectory to a macro downturn? The revenue data is reassuring, but a global recession could force capex reductions even if AI revenue remains strong, as companies redirect cash flows to core operations.

  5. Will the bounded-good/open-ended-bad boundary shift, or will it prove structural? This is ultimately the central question. The optimist’s strongest evidence (Erdos, Aletheia) comes from the boundary – formal mathematics is well-specified but open-ended. If this boundary continues shifting outward into genuinely unstructured domains, the optimist is right. If it stabilizes, the synthesis is right.

Final Integration: Bottom-Up AI Timelines Assessment

May 2026 – Merging two isolated reconciliation assessments into a single authoritative document

Methodology

This document integrates two independent reconciliation analyses:

  • Reconciliation P (hereafter “RP”): An analyst who reviewed only the pessimist’s 10-section argument against the original synthesis, without seeing the optimist’s case.
  • Reconciliation O (hereafter “RO”): An analyst who reviewed only the optimist’s argument against the original synthesis, without seeing the pessimist’s case.

Each reconciliation updated the original synthesis’s crux probabilities and timeline estimates based on the one-sided argument they reviewed. The isolation design ensures that each reconciliation provides an uncontaminated signal about how much updating the evidence warrants. Where both reconciliations converge despite seeing opposite arguments, confidence is highest. Where they diverge, the disagreement reveals the true cruxes.

Throughout this document, “Claim S” refers to the short-timeline or optimistic position on each crux, and “Claim L” refers to the long-timeline or pessimistic position.

1. High-Confidence Conclusions

These findings emerge from convergence between the two reconciliations, or from clear analytical superiority on one side that the other’s evidence does not undermine.

1.1 NCP Is Close: 2028-2029

Both reconciliations agree that Narrow Cognitive Parity – AI exceeding the best human expert on the majority of well-defined cognitive tasks measured individually – is near. RP holds the synthesis median of 2029 unchanged; RO shifts it one year earlier to 2028. Neither finds any argument from their respective side that substantially challenges NCP timing.

Integrated NCP median: 2028-2029. The pessimist’s arguments specifically target BCP (sustained, reliable, open-ended performance), not individual task mastery. The optimist’s evidence (HLE trajectory from 8% to 45-65% in 16 months, benchmark velocity compression, Erdos disproof) modestly supports an earlier NCP. The difference between the two reconciliations is small enough to be within noise.

1.2 The Reliability Gap Is the Central Bottleneck

Both reconciliations identify the METR 80%/50% time horizon divergence as the most informative empirical signal. RP explicitly updates on the error compounding math (85% per-step accuracy yielding only 20% success on 10-step workflows). RO criticizes the optimist for conspicuously omitting the 80% horizon data and relying solely on the 50% horizon. Both treat the 10x gap (27 minutes at 80% vs. 14.5 hours at 50%) as the binding constraint on BCP.

This convergence is striking because the two analysts reached the same conclusion for partially different reasons: RP was persuaded by the pessimist’s quantitative error compounding argument, while RO independently noticed the optimist’s failure to address the 80% metric. The reliability gap is the single most robust finding of this assessment.

1.3 The Bounded-Good / Open-Ended-Bad Pattern Is Real but Not Static

Both reconciliations accept the empirical pattern: AI performs excellently on well-specified bounded tasks (AIME 100%, GPQA Diamond 94.2%, SWE-bench 93.9%) and poorly on open-ended, interactive, sustained tasks (ARC-AGI-3 <1%, METR 80% horizon 27 minutes, enterprise AI failure rate 80%+). Neither reconciliation treats this pattern as an artifact or measurement error.

However, both also reject the strongest version of the claim that this pattern is permanent. RP notes that “the boundary between ‘bounded’ and ‘open-ended’ has been shifting outward.” RO notes that the Erdos disproof represents open-ended reasoning in a formal domain, suggesting the boundary is permeable. The disagreement is about the rate at which the boundary shifts, not its existence.

1.4 Investment Contraction Risk Is Lower Than the Synthesis Estimated

RP increases the weight of Scenario D (Investment Contraction) from 10% to 14%, driven by Goldman Sachs’s “$700B invested, essentially zero GDP growth” and increasing enterprise failure rates. RO decreases it from 10% to 5%, driven by Anthropic’s $44B ARR and robust revenue growth.

Despite the directional disagreement, the net effect is clear: the $44B ARR figure is harder evidence than the Goldman Sachs commentary. Revenue is an observed market outcome; commentary about GDP impact is a lagging macro measurement that may not capture AI’s actual value creation (much of which may appear as consumer surplus, quality improvement, or reallocation rather than GDP growth). RP’s concern about enterprise failure rates is valid for non-coding applications but does not threaten the financial foundation of AI development, which is currently sustained by coding tools and API revenue.

Integrated Scenario D weight: 7%. Down from the synthesis’s 10%, reflecting the revenue validation, but not as low as RO’s 5% because macroeconomic risks (recession, geopolitical disruption) could still compress capex even if AI revenue is healthy.

1.5 Regulation Is Not a Binding Constraint (Currently)

Both reconciliations hold Crux 10 at 70% Claim S (light regulation) without update. The pessimist explicitly deprioritizes regulation; the optimist agrees no update is needed. The regulatory environment remains permissive in the US and competitive internationally, with no imminent legislation that would materially slow development.

1.6 Coding Tools Are the Exception That Proves the Rule

Both reconciliations treat coding tools as a genuine, well-evidenced AI success story that is not representative of broader enterprise AI deployment. RP notes the pessimist “does not engage with the synthesis’s domain disaggregation” while acknowledging the increasing failure rate in non-coding applications. RO notes the optimist’s revenue data is “concentrated” in coding tools. The consensus: coding success sustains investment but does not demonstrate broad enterprise capability.

2. Adjudication of Disagreements

2.1 BCP Median: The 5-Year Gap

The most consequential disagreement: RP places the BCP median at 2038; RO places it at 2033. This 5-year gap is the central question this integration must resolve.

RP’s reasoning for 2038: Error compounding creates an orders-of-magnitude reliability gap. The serial dependency chain (metacognition -> sustained autonomy -> research execution -> recursive improvement -> BCP) means capability gaps must be solved in sequence, not in parallel. The self-driving car analogy suggests the “last 10%” takes 10-15 years. Infrastructure delivery will fall short of announced targets.

RO’s reasoning for 2033: The Erdos disproof and Aletheia results demonstrate autonomous scientific reasoning exists now. Revenue validation ($44B ARR) de-risks investment contraction. Benchmark velocity compression shows saturation timelines are shortening. Low-COI forecasters cluster at 2028-2033. The synthesis’s 2036 was already at the pessimistic end of the expert range.

My adjudication: Both sides make genuine contributions, but RO’s evidence is more novel (the Erdos and Aletheia results were not available when the synthesis was written) while RP’s evidence is more structural (error compounding and serial dependencies are architecture-independent constraints that do not go away with new results).

The key asymmetry: RP’s arguments explain why closing the remaining gaps is hard (error compounding, serial dependencies, infrastructure constraints). RO’s arguments show that progress is happening faster than expected on the capabilities that matter (autonomous research, revenue validation, benchmark compression). The question reduces to: does faster-than-expected capability progress overcome harder-than-expected reliability challenges?

I resolve this as follows:

  1. The Erdos/Aletheia evidence is genuinely new and important. It shifts the frontier of what current architectures can do, directly addressing the pessimist’s claim that novel intellectual contribution is beyond current approaches. This merits a 2-3 year pull on BCP.

  2. The error compounding math is architecture-independent and therefore robust. Even if capabilities advance faster, reliable 50-step workflows require 99.9%+ per-step accuracy, and current models are at 85-95%. This is a 1-2 order of magnitude gap that cannot be hand-waved away by pointing to individual capability milestones.

  3. The serial dependency argument is partially valid but overstated. Some parallelization is possible (external monitoring instead of internal metacognition; tool-augmented autonomy instead of raw model autonomy). I estimate the serial ordering adds 2-3 years to naive parallel estimates, not the 5-7 years the pessimist implies.

  4. The self-driving analogy is instructive but imperfect. Self-driving involves physical-world edge cases with lethal consequences and regulatory barriers; cognitive task deployment involves digital-world edge cases with economic consequences and market competition. The analogy supports a 5-10 year “last mile” period, not a 10-15 year one, for cognitive tasks.

  5. Revenue validation reduces the tail risk but does not accelerate capability development. Having money is necessary but not sufficient; the binding constraints are technical (reliability, autonomy, generalization), not financial.

Integrated BCP median: 2035. This is 3 years earlier than RP (reflecting the Erdos/Aletheia evidence and the revenue de-risking) and 2 years later than RO (reflecting the error compounding constraint and serial dependencies). The resolution is not a mechanical split – it reflects my judgment that the optimist’s evidence is stronger on capabilities while the pessimist’s evidence is stronger on reliability, and that reliability is the binding constraint for BCP specifically (as opposed to NCP, where capabilities suffice).

2.2 Crux 3: AI Research Automation

The largest crux-level disagreement. RP holds Crux 3 at 35% Claim S (unchanged from synthesis), arguing the pessimist adds “rhetorical force but not new evidence beyond the ideation-execution gap already incorporated.” RO updates Crux 3 from 35% to 48% Claim S, driven by the Erdos and Aletheia results.

Adjudication: RO is clearly correct that the Erdos and Aletheia results constitute new evidence. An 80-year-old conjecture disproved autonomously, verified by Fields-level mathematicians (ExplainX; Dataconomy), plus Aletheia’s 63 technically appropriate solutions across 700 open problems (MarkTechPost), are strong evidence that autonomous scientific reasoning is emerging. RP’s failure to update reflects a weakness in the isolation design: without seeing the optimist’s evidence, RP evaluated the pessimist’s restated arguments against a fixed evidence base.

However, RO correctly notes that the Crux 3 threshold specifically requires “significant ML algorithmic improvement adopted in production,” not mathematical discoveries. Mathematical proofs are self-verifying; ML research requires expensive experimentation and messy validation. The ideation-execution gap (arXiv:2506.20803) remains directly relevant: AI-generated research ideas that score well pre-execution but poorly post-execution are not evidence of autonomous research capability.

Integrated Crux 3: 45% Claim S. Substantial update from the synthesis’s 35%, primarily driven by Erdos/Aletheia, but stopping short of RO’s 48% because the domain transfer from formal mathematics to ML research is genuinely uncertain.

2.3 Crux 7: Enterprise Deployment Gap

RP shifts Crux 7 from 50% to 43% Claim S, citing the increasing abandonment rate (17% to 42%) and the Goldman Sachs GDP data. RO shifts it from 50% to 58% Claim S, citing $44B ARR and 84% developer adoption.

Adjudication: Both updates are partially correct because they are responding to different aspects of the evidence. The enterprise deployment picture is bifurcated: coding tools are succeeding spectacularly while general enterprise AI projects are failing at increasing rates. RP is correct about non-coding enterprise applications; RO is correct about the economic foundation.

The crux as formulated asks whether “enterprise failure rates drop below 50% by 2028.” This is an aggregate question that depends on the mix of applications attempted. If companies concentrate on coding-adjacent use cases (code generation, documentation, testing, data analysis with clear metrics), aggregate failure rates could drop. If they continue attempting ambitious general deployments, failure rates will stay high.

Integrated Crux 7: 48% Claim S. Near the center, reflecting the genuine bifurcation. The slight tilt toward L reflects the direction of travel in non-coding failure rates, which is the harder evidence.

2.4 Crux 6: Compute Scaling

RP shifts from 65% to 58% Claim S, citing infrastructure delivery shortfalls (only 1/3 of targeted capacity under construction, 160-week substation lead times). RO shifts from 65% to 70%, citing stronger-than-expected capex ($725B in 2026) and Rubin specifications.

Adjudication: These are responding to different constraints – RP to physical infrastructure, RO to financial commitment. Both are relevant: money is flowing, but physics limits how fast it converts to operational capacity. The synthesis’s 5e27 FLOP target by 2028 (10x current frontier) is the modest version; the more ambitious 1e28+ target faces the constraints RP identifies.

RP’s evidence is more directly relevant to the crux because having the money allocated does not ensure the compute is delivered on time. Substation lead times and grid interconnection delays are hard physical constraints. However, behind-the-meter gas deployment and chip efficiency improvements (Rubin 2.5x per-chip) partially compensate.

Integrated Crux 6: 63% Claim S. Slightly below the synthesis, reflecting that infrastructure delivery constraints are real but that the modest 5e27 FLOP target is achievable through efficiency gains and workarounds.

2.5 The Self-Driving Analogy vs. Benchmark Velocity Compression

RP finds the self-driving car analogy compelling (the “last 10%” took 10-15 years despite genuine capability). RO finds benchmark velocity compression compelling (time-to-saturation is shortening: MMLU 4 years, HumanEval 3, GPQA Diamond 2, ARC-AGI-2 1 year).

Adjudication: These are not contradictory – they apply to different capability types. Benchmark velocity compression applies to bounded tasks (math competitions, coding benchmarks, knowledge tests), where the pattern is robust. The self-driving analogy applies to deployment-grade reliability in open-ended environments, which is the BCP-relevant challenge.

The resolution: NCP (bounded task mastery) will likely arrive per the benchmark compression timeline (2028-2029). The NCP-to-BCP gap (reliability, autonomy, generalization for open-ended tasks) will behave more like the self-driving “last mile.” This is consistent with both patterns being genuine and applying to different components of the timeline.

The self-driving analogy suggests a 5-10 year gap between demo-level capability and deployment-grade reliability in open environments. If current demo-level BCP capability is roughly 2025-2026 (impressive demos, unreliable deployment), deployment-grade BCP would arrive 2031-2036. This is consistent with my integrated BCP median of 2035.

2.6 LeCun’s Revealed Preference

RP finds LeCun’s departure from Meta to found AMI Labs with $1.03B (tech-insider.org) compelling as evidence that current architectures may be insufficient. RO does not specifically address this (it was in the pessimist’s argument that RO did not see).

Adjudication: LeCun’s revealed preference is genuinely informative. When a Turing Award winner restructures his career and raises $1B on the thesis that current architectures are insufficient, it carries more weight than verbal opinions. The pattern of LeCun and Sutskever (via SSI) independently making this bet is worth noting.

However, the Erdos/Aletheia results partially counter this signal: current architectures are producing novel scientific discoveries that LeCun’s framework predicts should be impossible without world models. If transformers can disprove 80-year-old conjectures, the “current architecture is fundamentally insufficient” thesis needs updating, even if it remains partially valid for other capability domains (physical reasoning, sustained autonomy).

Net assessment: LeCun’s bet is informative for the claim that current architectures alone are insufficient for full BCP. It is less informative about the timeline, because architectural innovations (world models, hybrid systems, new training paradigms) could emerge within the current development cycle rather than requiring a multi-year “reset.”

3. Integrated Crux Map

Summary of Crux Movements
  • Moved toward pessimist (net): Cruxes 2, 4, 6, 7 (reliability, scaling returns, infrastructure, enterprise deployment)
  • Moved toward optimist (net): Crux 3 (AI research automation – the only large move)
  • Roughly unchanged: Cruxes 1, 5, 8, 9, 10, 11

The pattern is telling: the optimist’s strongest evidence is concentrated in a single domain (autonomous scientific reasoning), while the pessimist’s evidence is distributed across multiple structural constraints. This means the pessimist’s case is more robust to any single piece of evidence being wrong, while the optimist’s case depends heavily on whether the Erdos/Aletheia results generalize beyond formal mathematics.

Definition: AI systems exceeding the best human expert on the majority of well-defined cognitive tasks measured individually.

Integrated NCP median: 2028. I favor RO’s estimate here because (a) the pessimist’s arguments do not target NCP, so RP’s unchanged estimate reflects absence of negative evidence rather than presence of positive evidence; (b) the benchmark velocity compression and HLE trajectory provide genuine support for an earlier NCP; and (c) the Erdos result demonstrates a capability (autonomous mathematical discovery) that was expected to be beyond NCP-level performance.

Definition: AI systems that can reliably replace a college-educated knowledge worker across a wide range of unstructured tasks.
