Integrated BCP median: 2035. This reflects:

  • The Erdos/Aletheia evidence pulling the timeline ~2 years earlier than RP’s 2038
  • The error compounding and serial dependency constraints pushing ~2 years later than RO’s 2033
  • Revenue validation reducing right-tail risk (Scenario D weight down from 10% to 7%)
  • Infrastructure delivery concerns being real but manageable for modest compute targets

The 2035 median implies roughly 40% probability of BCP by 2035 and 70% by 2040. This is consistent with the center of the low-COI forecast range (Metaculus AGI median: October 2032 per Metaculus; Bengio: “within a decade” from 2025) while maintaining appropriate skepticism about the reliability gap.

5. Outside View Adjustment

The inside-view analysis above yields NCP median 2028, BCP median 2035. An outside view adjustment blends this with external forecasts, prediction markets, expert surveys, and historical base rates.

5.1 Outside View Anchors

Prediction markets and aggregation platforms: - Metaculus “full AGI” (operationalized as systems that can pass Turing test, get 90th percentile on SAT, and perform competently as financial analyst): median October 2032 (Metaculus) - Polymarket and Manifold Markets have moved AGI-related questions substantially earlier over 2024-2026; current consensus clusters around 2030-2033 for “transformative AI” - The systematic directional pattern: nearly every major forecaster has revised estimates earlier over the past 2-3 years (RO correctly identifies this as informative)

Expert surveys: - AI Impacts 2023 survey: 50% probability of “high-level machine intelligence” by 2047, but this is pre-GPT-4 and pre-o1 – significantly outdated (AI Impacts) - Bengio (2025): “within a decade” – i.e., by ~2035 (Nature interview) - Morgan Stanley (2026): scaling laws “holding firm,” implying continued rapid progress but no specific date (Fortune)

Low-COI forecaster range (organizations/individuals with minimal financial stake in AI timelines): 2028-2035, with central tendency around 2031-2033.

Historical base rate: The 70-year track record of overoptimistic AI predictions is real but requires careful interpretation. Each prior failure had specific identifiable causes (insufficient compute, wrong algorithms, no training data). The question is whether analogous specific causes exist today. Both reconciliations agree specific causes do exist (reliability gap, error compounding, serial dependencies) but disagree about their severity and duration.

Combined outside view anchor: BCP ~2032-2033. This is the central tendency of low-COI forecasts, prediction markets, and expert surveys as of May 2026, weighted toward more recent estimates that incorporate current evidence.

5.2 Adjustment at Four Weights

The inside view places BCP median at 2035. The outside view anchor is ~2032-2033. Let OV = outside view anchor of 2032.5, IV = inside view of 2035.

Blend formula: Adjusted = (1-w) * IV + w * OV, where w is the outside view weight.

OV Weight BCP Median NCP Median Reasoning
0% OV (pure inside view) 2035 2028 The inside view’s own estimate, reflecting the full crux analysis, error compounding constraints, and serial dependencies.
25% OV 2034.4 2028 Modest outside view correction. Acknowledges that aggregated forecasters have information not captured in the inside view (e.g., private information at labs, broader trend extrapolation). NCP already near outside view consensus so no shift.
50% OV (recommended) 2033.8 2028 Equal weighting of inside and outside views. This is the recommended weight because (a) the inside view analysis is thorough but has known blind spots (inability to assess private lab capabilities, limited mechanistic understanding of scaling trajectories), and (b) the outside view has been systematically revising earlier, suggesting it is tracking real signals that the inside view may underweight.
75% OV 2033.1 2028 Heavy outside view weighting. Appropriate if one believes the systematic earlier-revision pattern among forecasters reflects genuine information that bottom-up analysis cannot capture. The risk is that all forecasters are subject to the same hype-driven anchoring effects.

Recommended estimate (50% OV): BCP median ~2034, NCP median ~2028.

At the recommended 50% OV weight, the full BCP distribution becomes:

Percentile Pure Inside View 50% OV Adjusted
10th 2029 2028
25th 2031 2030
50th 2035 ~2034
75th 2041 2039
90th 2046 2044

The outside view adjustment compresses the distribution modestly, pulling the median about 1 year earlier and the tails about 2 years tighter. The compression is asymmetric (stronger on the right tail) because the outside view consensus has been moving left while the inside view identifies structural constraints that are absent from most forecaster models.

5.3 Why 50% OV Is Recommended

Three arguments for giving the outside view substantial weight:

  1. Directional consensus: The systematic pattern of forecasters revising timelines earlier (Metaculus shifting from ~2045 to ~2032 over four years) across methodologies and incentive structures is informative. When independent estimates converge directionally over time, they are likely tracking a real signal.

  2. Private information: Labs have internal capability data (unpublished benchmarks, in-progress training runs, architectural experiments) that the inside view cannot access. The outside view, to the extent it incorporates lab employees’ forecasts and revealed behavior (hiring, investment, rhetoric), partially captures this information.

  3. Model uncertainty: The inside view relies on specific mechanistic arguments (error compounding, serial dependencies, scaling curves) that may be wrong in specific ways. The outside view provides a hedge against model specification error.

Three arguments for not going above 50% OV:

  1. Correlated bias: Forecasters may be subject to availability bias and anchoring on the same impressive demos and benchmark results, producing an artificially tight consensus that overstates the probability of near-term BCP. The error compounding math and reliability gap are underappreciated in most forecaster models.

  2. Definition mismatch: Different forecasters use different definitions of “AGI” or “transformative AI.” Metaculus’s operationalization (Turing test + SAT + financial analyst) is arguably easier than the synthesis’s BCP definition (reliably replacing college-educated knowledge workers across unstructured tasks). Definitional differences could account for 2-3 years of the gap.

  3. The 70-year base rate: While overweighted by the pessimist, the base rate of overoptimism in AI predictions is real. Applying zero correction for this pattern would be overconfident.

6. Top 5 Remaining Uncertainties

6.1 Will the Reliability Gap Close?

What it is: The 10x gap between the METR 50% time horizon (14.5 hours) and the 80% time horizon (27 minutes) measures how consistently AI systems perform over extended tasks. BCP requires reliable performance, not just occasional brilliance.

Why it matters: If the 80% horizon grows at the same doubling rate as the 50% horizon (every 4 months), it reaches multi-day reliability by 2028-2029, and BCP is closer to 2032. If the gap widens as the 50% horizon extends (because longer tasks compound errors), BCP is closer to 2040+. This single variable could shift the BCP median by 5+ years in either direction.

Current evidence: The 80% horizon has improved from ~10 minutes to 27 minutes over two years (2.7x), much slower than the 50% horizon’s trajectory. If the 80% horizon’s improvement rate does not accelerate, the error compounding argument is confirmed and BCP timelines extend.

Key test: METR 80% time horizon readings through December 2027. Above 4 hours = strong optimist signal. Below 1 hour = strong pessimist signal.

6.2 Does Autonomous Scientific Reasoning Generalize Beyond Mathematics?

What it is: The Erdos disproof and Aletheia results demonstrate that AI can do autonomous research in formal mathematics. The question is whether this transfers to messier domains (ML research, biology, materials science) where problems are ill-defined and solutions require expensive experimental validation.

Why it matters: If autonomous research capability generalizes, the recursive improvement loop could accelerate the timeline by 3-5 years. If it remains confined to formal mathematics, Crux 3 stays near center and the loop does not meaningfully accelerate.

Current evidence: Mathematics is the most favorable domain for AI research (precise formulations, self-verifying solutions, rich training data). The ideation-execution gap finding – that AI research ideas score well pre-execution but poorly post-execution (arXiv:2506.20803) – directly tests whether the capability transfers. So far, it has not convincingly transferred.

Key test: An AI system autonomously discovering a significant non-mathematical scientific result (biology, chemistry, materials science, or ML itself) that is independently verified and adopted, by December 2028.

6.3 Will Hyperscaler Capex Sustain Through a Macro Downturn?

What it is: AI development depends on sustained annual capex exceeding $500B+. While current revenue ($44B ARR for Anthropic alone) provides a foundation, the investment-to-revenue ratio remains high (~5-7:1 across the industry), and a macroeconomic downturn could force capex reductions even if AI revenue stays strong.

Why it matters: A 30%+ capex reduction in any year would delay the compute trajectory by 1-2 years and potentially trigger cascading effects (talent departures, lab consolidation, reduced competition).

Current evidence: Capex is accelerating ($725B in 2026, exceeding prior projections). Revenue growth is strong. But 94% of hyperscaler operating cash flows going to capex is an unsustainable ratio in a downturn, and the ratio of investment to current revenue implies expectations of enormous future revenue that may not materialize on schedule.

Key test: Year-over-year capex growth rates in 2027. Above 30% = investment thesis holds. Below 10% or negative = Scenario D gains weight.

6.4 Is the Bounded/Open-Ended Boundary Shifting?

What it is: The empirical pattern of AI excelling at bounded tasks and struggling with open-ended ones is well-documented. The critical question is whether the boundary between these categories is stable or shifting outward as scaffolding, tool use, and test-time compute improve.

Why it matters: If the boundary is static (reflecting a fundamental limitation of current architectures), BCP requires architectural innovation and the timeline extends. If it is dynamic (reflecting current training and inference limitations that will be overcome), BCP arrives through incremental improvement and the timeline compresses.

Current evidence: The boundary has shifted somewhat (AI can now do multi-step coding, extended mathematical proofs, and some scientific reasoning that were previously “open-ended”). But the hardest open-ended challenges (ARC-AGI-3, multi-day autonomous work, enterprise deployment of unstructured tasks) show minimal progress.

Key test: ARC-AGI-3 scores for frontier models by December 2027. Above 10% = boundary is shifting. Below 2% = boundary may be structural.

6.5 How Deep Is AI Metacognition?

What it is: The MIRROR benchmark reveals that current models have near-random compositional calibration error – they cannot reliably assess what they know and don’t know (arXiv:2604.19809). Metacognition (error detection, confidence calibration, knowing when to ask for help) is a prerequisite for sustained autonomy per the serial dependency chain.

Why it matters: Humans compensate for imperfect per-step accuracy through metacognitive error detection and correction. If AI systems develop genuine metacognition, the error compounding constraint relaxes dramatically (you don’t need 99.9% per-step accuracy if you can detect and correct errors). If metacognition proves as hard as the other capability gaps, the serial dependency chain holds and BCP extends.

Current evidence: MIRROR results are poor, but the benchmark is new and there has been limited targeted research on AI metacognition. It is unclear whether this reflects a fundamental architectural limitation or simply a training/prompting gap.

Key test: MIRROR compositional calibration error improving from near-random to >0.3 correlation by December 2028. This would indicate that metacognition is learnable within current architectures.

Shift BCP median 2-3 years earlier. - If falsified (80% horizon <1 hour by December 2027): The error compounding math is confirmed as a structural barrier. The serial dependency chain holds. Shift BCP median 2-3 years later.

Shift BCP median 2-4 years earlier. - If falsified: The Erdos/Aletheia results are domain-specific to formal mathematics. AI research capability remains limited to domains with precise formulations and self-verifying solutions. Crux 3 stays near center. No shift.

Confidence: 20% that threshold is met.

AI value creation remains concentrated in coding-adjacent use cases for the foreseeable future. Crux 7 shifts to <40% S. Shift BCP median 1 year later.

The “hallucination as unfixable architectural feature” argument is refuted. Crux 8 shifts modestly toward S. - If falsified (best model >3%): Hallucination may indeed reflect a structural limitation of next-token prediction architectures. Supports the case for architectural innovation. Crux 8 shifts toward L.

8. Meta-Assessment

8.1 Confidence Level

I assign moderate-to-low confidence in the specific numbers produced by this analysis (BCP median 2035 at 50% OV, 2034 adjusted). The rank ordering of scenarios and the directional conclusions are more robust than the specific year estimates.

What I am most confident about: - NCP is close (2027-2029), within +/- 1 year. - The reliability gap (not raw capability) is the binding constraint for BCP. - The bounded/open-ended pattern is real and will take years, not months, to overcome. - Investment contraction is unlikely in the near term given revenue validation. - Regulation is not currently a binding constraint.

What I am least confident about: - The specific BCP median (2034-2035). The 90% confidence interval spans roughly 2029-2046, which is an 17-year range. Claiming precision within this range is not warranted. - Whether the Erdos/Aletheia results generalize to non-mathematical research. This is genuinely unknown and could shift the timeline by 3-5 years in either direction. - Whether the error compounding constraint can be overcome through engineering (better scaffolding, monitoring, error correction) or requires fundamental innovation (metacognition, new architectures). This determines whether BCP requires an “architectural reset” scenario. - The tail risks. Scenario C (Architectural Reset requiring 5-10 additional years) and Scenario F (Wild Card breakthrough compressing the timeline to 2028-2030) are both genuinely possible, and their probabilities are more uncertain than the numbers suggest.

8.2 Biggest Methodological Weaknesses

1. The isolation design introduces systematic biases. RP could not account for Erdos/Aletheia evidence because it only saw the pessimist’s argument. RO could not account for error compounding or serial dependency arguments because it only saw the optimist’s. The integration compensates for this, but the compensation itself is a judgment call. A design where both reconciliations saw both arguments would have produced different (possibly better) updates, at the cost of losing the contamination-free signal that the isolation provides.

2. Crux independence is assumed but may not hold. The crux map treats the 12 cruxes as partially independent, but many are correlated. Crux 2 (METR) and Crux 8 (Architecture) are linked through error compounding. Crux 3 (AI research) and Crux 9 (Recursive improvement) are linked through the catch-22. Crux 4 (Scaling) and Crux 6 (Compute) are linked through the scaling hypothesis. Treating them as independent overstates the precision of the probability estimates and may miss correlated failure or success modes.

3. The outside view anchors are not truly independent of the inside view. Metaculus forecasters, prediction market participants, and expert survey respondents are all consuming some of the same evidence that feeds the inside view analysis. Blending the inside and outside views as if they are independent overweights the shared evidence.

4. The BCP definition is underspecified. “Reliably replacing a college-educated knowledge worker across a wide range of unstructured tasks” leaves enormous ambiguity about what “reliably,” “wide range,” and “unstructured” mean. Resolving the ambiguity toward easier interpretations (80% of tasks, 70% reliability) could shift the estimate 3-4 years earlier; resolving toward harder interpretations (95% of tasks, 95% reliability) could shift it 5+ years later. The reconciliations handle this differently (RO notes the optimist uses a looser definition), but the integration cannot fully resolve it.

5. Benchmark extrapolation assumes continuity. The inside view relies heavily on benchmark trajectories (HLE, METR, ARC-AGI) continuing at roughly current rates. Historical evidence suggests that benchmark progress is often discontinuous: long periods of stagnation followed by rapid breakthroughs when a new method is discovered. The smooth trajectories assumed in the analysis may be wrong in both directions – progress could be faster (sudden breakthrough) or slower (plateau) than the smooth extrapolation suggests.

6. Revenue is conflated with capability. Both reconciliations treat AI revenue ($44B ARR) as evidence about timeline. But revenue measures current utility, which is dominated by coding tools and bounded applications. High current revenue does not necessarily indicate that the harder capabilities (sustained autonomy, open-ended reasoning, cross-domain generalization) are close. Revenue sustains investment, which keeps the timeline from extending, but it does not directly evidence that the remaining capability gaps are closing.

7. This analysis cannot assess classified or proprietary capabilities. Frontier labs may have internal models or capabilities that significantly exceed what is publicly known. The outside view partially captures this (prediction market participants may include lab employees or people with private information), but the inside view is limited to public evidence. If labs are 1-2 years ahead of public benchmarks, the true BCP timeline could be 1-2 years earlier than this analysis suggests.

8.3 What Would Make This Analysis More Robust

  1. Access to private benchmark data from frontier labs. The gap between public and private capabilities is unknown and could significantly shift estimates.

  2. A formal model of error compounding and recovery. The qualitative arguments about error compounding are compelling, but a rigorous quantitative model – incorporating error detection, correction, and the diminishing marginal cost of each – would sharpen the estimates considerably.

  3. Longitudinal tracking of the bounded/open-ended boundary. Rather than snapshot comparisons (ARC-AGI-3 at 0.37% today), tracking how quickly previously-open-ended tasks become bounded as methods improve would provide a growth rate for the boundary shift.

  4. Better enterprise data with domain disaggregation. Current enterprise success rate data mixes coding, customer service, data analysis, and other applications. Domain-disaggregated data would sharpen Crux 7 substantially.

  5. A second round of adversarial debate incorporating the integration’s conclusions. The current process is: synthesis -> pessimist/optimist arguments -> isolated reconciliations -> integration. A second round where the pessimist and optimist respond to the integration’s conclusions would produce a more refined estimate.

8.4 Bottom Line

This analysis reflects a genuine attempt to integrate two carefully constructed one-sided arguments through isolated reconciliation. The headline numbers:

Metric Pure Inside View 50% OV Adjusted (Recommended)
NCP median 2028 2028
BCP median 2035 ~2034
P(BCP by 2030) ~12% ~15%
P(BCP by 2035) ~40% ~48%
P(BCP by 2040) ~70% ~75%

The practical implication: there is meaningful probability (~15%) that BCP arrives within 4 years, substantial probability (~48%) within 9 years, and high probability (~75%) within 14 years. The most likely single scenario remains Steady Progress (BCP ~2034-2038, 40% weight), with meaningful upside risk from Fast Convergence (17%) and Wild Card (8%) scenarios, and meaningful downside risk from Architectural Reset (24%) and Investment Contraction (7%) scenarios.

The single most important empirical test is the trajectory of the METR 80% time horizon over the next 18 months. This metric directly measures the reliability gap that separates demo-level AI capability from deployment-grade BCP. If the 80% horizon accelerates toward the 50% horizon’s growth rate, BCP is closer. If it stalls or the gap widens, BCP is further.

This integration was produced by merging two isolated reconciliation analyses. It has attempted to make substantive judgments about disagreements rather than splitting differences mechanically. All probability estimates should be treated as rough guides reflecting the balance of evidence as of May 2026, not as precise forecasts.
